## Module 11 — Water & Irrigation
## Management
1. Plant Water Requirement
## 1. Introduction
Plant water requirement is the amount of water a crop needs over a particular period to maintain
physiological processes, growth, development, and the desired yield under specific environmental and
management conditions.
In simple terms:
Plant Water Requirement = Water needed by the crop for healthy growth and production  
It is one of the most important concepts in irrigation management because irrigation should supply the
crop's actual water deficit, rather than simply applying as much water as possible.
The basic concept is:
Water Requirement→Crop Water Use+Other Necessary Water Uses 
 +    
For field irrigation planning, the most important component is evapotranspiration (ET).

## 2. Definition
Plant water requirement is the quantity of water needed by a crop during a specified
period to compensate for water losses through transpiration and evaporation and to
support growth, development and production under given soil, climatic, crop and
management conditions.
The crop continuously loses water through:
Transpiration+Evaporation +  
Therefore:
Evapotranspiration (ET) = Evaporation + Transpiration  

3. Why Plants Need Water
Water is required for almost every major plant process.

Major functions include:
1. seed imbibition and germination,
2. cell expansion,
3. cell division,
4. photosynthesis,
5. nutrient transport,
6. nutrient uptake,
7. enzyme activity,
8. metabolic reactions,
9. maintenance of cell turgor,
10.stomatal regulation,
11.cooling through transpiration,
12.transport of assimilates,
13.flowering,
14.pollination,
15.fertilization,
16.fruit/grain/pod development,
17.seed filling,
18.maintenance of yield and quality.
Therefore:
Water Availability→Physiology→Growth→Yield 

4. Water Requirement Is Not the Same as
Irrigation Requirement
This is one of the most important distinctions.

Plant/Crop Water Requirement
Total water needed by the crop.

Irrigation Requirement
The amount of water that must be supplied through irrigation after accounting for other available water
sources.
Conceptually:
Irrigation Requirement=Crop Water Requirement−Effective Rainfall−Useful Soil Water±Other Contrib
utions 
The exact irrigation requirement depends on the chosen water-balance method.
Therefore:
Water Requirement≠Irrigation Requirement 

5. Main Components of Crop Water
Requirement
A simplified crop water balance can be represented as:
ETc=E+T 
where:
- EE = evaporation from soil/water surface,
- TT = transpiration from plants,
- ETcET c = crop evapotranspiration.
Other water flows may include:
- effective rainfall,
- irrigation,
- runoff,
- drainage,
- deep percolation,
- changes in soil-water storage.

## 6. Evaporation
Evaporation is the conversion of liquid water into water vapor from surfaces such as:
- soil,
- water,
- wet plant surfaces,
- irrigation water on the field.
E=Water loss from non-plant surfaces  
Immediately after irrigation or rainfall, soil evaporation can be an important part of total ET.

## 7. Transpiration
Transpiration is the loss of water vapor from plant tissues, mainly through stomata.
T=Water loss through plants  
Water pathway:
Soil→Roots→Xylem→Leaves→Stomata→Atmosphere 

## 8. Evapotranspiration
Evapotranspiration combines:
ET=E+T 
It is the principal water-loss term used in crop water requirement calculations.

9. Reference Evapotranspiration
A standard concept is reference evapotranspiration, commonly written as:
ET0 
It represents atmospheric evaporative demand from a standardized reference surface under specified
conditions.
It is determined mainly by weather variables such as:

- solar radiation,
- air temperature,
- humidity,
- wind speed.
A widely used formulation is the FAO-56 Penman–Monteith equation:
ET0=0.408Δ(Rn−G)+γ900T+273u2(es−ea)Δ+γ(1+0.34u2)  T+273  u 2(e s-e a)     + (1+0.34u 2)    
where:
- ET0ET 0 = reference evapotranspiration,
- Δ = slope of saturation vapor-pressure curve,
- RnR n = net radiation,
- GG = soil heat flux,
- TT = mean air temperature,
- u2u 2 = wind speed at 2 m,
- ese s = saturation vapor pressure,
- eae a = actual vapor pressure,
- γ = psychrometric constant.
Usually:
ET0 is calculated first  0  
and then crop-specific water use is estimated.

10. Crop Evapotranspiration
The crop's evapotranspiration is represented as:
ETc=Kc×ET0 
where:
- ETcET c = crop evapotranspiration,
- KcK c = crop coefficient,
- ET0ET 0 = reference evapotranspiration.
Thus:

Weather Demand×Crop Characteristics=Crop Water Use 
 =    

11. Crop Coefficient KcK c
KcK c adjusts reference ET for crop characteristics.
It changes during crop development.
Typical conceptual stages are:
Initial→Development→Mid-season→Late-season      
Generally:
- early stage → small canopy,
- development → canopy expands,
- mid-season → maximum canopy,
- late season → senescence and maturity.
Therefore:
Kc=f(crop type, growth stage, canopy, ground cover) )  

12. Why Water Requirement Changes During
Crop Growth
A seedling has a small leaf area.
A mature crop may have a large canopy.
Therefore:
Leaf Area↑→Transpiration Potential↑ 
At the same time:
Crop Development→Changing Root System + Canopy + Surface Cover 
 Therefore water requirement is dynamic, not constant.

13. Crop Growth Stage and Water Requirement
Water demand varies across stages.
A simplified pattern is:
Seedling→Vegetative→Reproductive→Maturity 
Water demand often increases as canopy develops, reaches a high level during substantial canopy
development, and changes again during maturity/senescence.
But water sensitivity and water demand are not the same thing.
A stage may not have the highest total water use but may be highly sensitive to water stress.

14. Critical Growth Stages
Some crop stages are particularly sensitive to water shortage.
Depending on crop, important stages may include:
- germination,
- seedling establishment,
- flowering,
- pollination,
- fertilization,
- fruit set,
- grain formation,
- grain filling,
- pod development.
Therefore:
Water Stress During Critical Stage→Potentially Large Yield Loss 
The exact critical stages are crop-specific.

15. Plant Water Requirement and Soil Water
The atmosphere creates a water demand, while soil provides the water available to roots.
A useful conceptual relationship is:
Atmospheric Demand↔Soil Water Supply↔Plant Water Status 
Even when soil contains water, the plant may experience stress if water is held too strongly or
atmospheric demand is extremely high.

16. Soil Water-Holding Capacity
Important soil-water terms are:

Saturation
Most soil pores contain water.

Field Capacity (FC)
Water remaining after excess gravitational water has drained under defined conditions.

Permanent Wilting Point (PWP)
Soil water condition at which plants cannot recover turgor under the defined reference conditions.
Approximate available water:
AWC=FC−PWP 

17. Available Water
Available water is not simply all the water present in soil.
Available Water≈FC−PWP 
The crop can access only a portion of the stored water effectively before stress becomes significant.
Thus:
Total Soil Water≠Plant Available Water 

18. Root Zone
The root zone is the portion of soil from which the crop obtains significant water and nutrients.
Conceptually:
Root Zone=Soil Volume Explored by Functional Roots 
Water storage in the root zone can be approximated by:
Root Zone Water≈AWC×Root Zone Depth 
with consistent units.

19. Rooting Depth and Water Requirement
A deeper root system can access a larger soil volume when suitable water is available.
Root Depth↑→Potential Soil Volume Explored↑ 
However, deep roots do not automatically mean low irrigation requirement because atmospheric
demand, crop characteristics and soil water still matter.

20. Effective Rainfall
Not all rainfall contributes equally to crop water supply.
Some rainfall may:
- run off,
- evaporate,
- drain below the root zone,
- remain temporarily on surfaces.
The portion that contributes usefully to crop water supply is called effective rainfall.
Effective Rainfall≤Total Rainfall 
The exact calculation depends on soil, rainfall event, root zone, method and water balance.

21. Irrigation Requirement
A simplified conceptual equation is:
IR≈ETc−Pe−Useful Soil-Water Contribution   
where:
- IRIR = irrigation requirement,
- ETcET c = crop evapotranspiration,
- PeP e = effective precipitation.
A complete field water balance may also include:
- runoff,
- deep percolation,
- capillary rise,
- groundwater contribution,
- changes in soil-water storage.

22. Crop Water Balance
A general water balance can be represented as:
ΔS=P+I+CR−R−D−ET 
where:
- ΔS S = change in soil-water storage,
- PP = precipitation,
- II = irrigation,
- CRCR = capillary rise,
- RR = runoff,
- DD = drainage/deep percolation,
- ETET = evapotranspiration.
This equation is extremely important for understanding irrigation management.

23. Positive and Negative Water Balance
Positive balance
Water inputs exceed losses:
ΔS>0 S>0
So soil-water storage increases.

Negative balance
Water losses exceed inputs:
ΔS<0 S<0
So soil-water storage decreases.
If storage becomes too low:
Soil Water↓→Plant Water Stress 

24. Water Stress
Water stress occurs when water availability cannot adequately meet plant demand.
Water Demand>Effective Water Supply→Water Stress 
Typical sequence:
Soil Water↓  ↓
Plant Water Potential↓  ↓
Stomatal Closure 
↓
CO2 Entry↓CO 2\ Entry ↓
Photosynthesis↓Photosynthesis ↓
Growth↓Growth ↓
Potential Yield Loss 

25. Stomata and Plant Water Requirement
Stomata regulate gas exchange and water loss.
Under drought:
Water Stress→ABA Signaling→Stomatal Closure 
This reduces transpiration but also reduces CO₂ entry.
Therefore:
Water Conservation↔Carbon Assimilation 
Plants must balance both.

26. Transpiration and Cooling
Transpiration can cool leaves because evaporation requires energy.
Water Evaporation→Latent Heat Loss→Leaf Cooling 
When drought causes stomatal closure:
Transpiration↓→Cooling↓→Leaf Temperature↑Transpiration  Cooling  Leaf\ Temperature This becomes especially important under heat stress.

27. Water Requirement and Temperature
Higher temperature can increase atmospheric evaporative demand.
Therefore:
Temperature↑→Potential ET↑ 
But actual ET also depends on:

- humidity,
- wind,
- radiation,
- soil moisture,
- crop canopy,
- stomatal conductance.
Temperature alone does not determine water requirement.

28. Water Requirement and Humidity
Relative humidity affects atmospheric drying demand.
Generally:
RH↓→VPD↑→Atmospheric Water Demand↑RH  VPD  Atmospheric\ Water\ Demand where:
VPD=es−ea 
Higher VPD generally increases transpiration demand when water is available.

29. Water Requirement and Wind
Wind can remove humid air surrounding leaves.
Wind↑→Boundary Layer↓→Vapor Exchange↑ 
Consequently, under suitable conditions:
Wind↑→ET Demand↑Wind  ET\ Demand 30. Water Requirement and Solar Radiation
Radiation supplies energy for evaporation and transpiration.
Generally:
Radiation↑→Potential ET↑ 

Therefore sunny conditions can increase crop water demand compared with low-radiation conditions,
all else being equal.

31. Water Requirement and Leaf Area
As canopy develops:
Leaf Area↑→Transpiring Surface↑    But simultaneously, soil evaporation may decrease because the canopy shades the soil.
Therefore total ET is a combination of:
ET=Soil Evaporation+Plant Transpiration 

32. Early vs Mature Crop
Early stage
- small canopy,
- low transpiration,
- greater relative contribution from soil evaporation.

Mature canopy
- large transpiring surface,
- greater transpiration,
- lower exposed soil fraction.
Thus crop water use changes with canopy development.

33. Water Requirement and Crop Type
Different crops have different:
- canopy structures,
- rooting patterns,
- growing durations,

- stomatal behavior,
- crop coefficients,
- biomass production patterns.
Therefore:
Water Requirement=f(Crop,Environment,Stage) 
Rice, wheat, maize, cotton, sugarcane, vegetables and fruit crops do not have identical water
requirements.

34. Water Requirement and Variety
Even within the same crop, varieties can differ in:
- duration,
- canopy,
- root architecture,
- drought response,
- maturity,
- water-use characteristics.
Therefore:
Same Crop≠Same Water Requirement 

35. Water Requirement and Plant Population
Plant population affects canopy size and transpiration.
Population↑→Canopy↑→Potential Water Use↑      But the relationship is not simply linear because canopy development, soil evaporation and crop
compensation change with density.

36. Water Requirement and Soil Texture
Sandy soil
- low water storage,
- rapid drainage,
- frequent irrigation may be required in some systems.

Clay soil
- high water retention,
- slower drainage,
- some water may be strongly held,
- waterlogging risk can be greater.

Loam
Often provides a relatively balanced combination of:
- water storage,
- drainage,
- aeration.
But actual irrigation scheduling must be based on measured/estimated soil-water conditions.

37. Water Requirement and Soil Structure
Good soil structure can improve:
- infiltration,
- root growth,
- aeration,
- water storage,
- drainage.
Poor structure or compaction can restrict roots and alter water movement.
Soil Structure→Water Movement→Root Access→Plant Water Status 

38. Water Requirement and Soil Salinity
Salinity is particularly important because salts reduce the plant's ability to extract water.
Salinity↑→Soil Water Potential↓→Water Uptake Difficulty↑ 
Therefore a soil can contain substantial water while plants still experience physiological water stress.
This is sometimes described as physiological drought.

39. Water Requirement and Waterlogging
Too much water is also harmful.
Waterlogging→Air-Filled Pore Space↓→Soil O2↓→Root Respiration Stress Filled\ Pore\ Space  Soil\ O 2  Root\ Respiration\ Stress  
Therefore:
Plant Water Requirement≠Maximum Water Application 
Plants need an appropriate water-air balance.

40. Water Requirement and Root Aeration
Roots require oxygen for aerobic respiration.
When irrigation is excessive:
Water Filled Pores↑→Air Filled Pores↓Water\ Filled\ Pores  Air\ Filled\
Pores and:
O2↓→Root Respiration↓O 2  Root\ Respiration Thus irrigation management must prevent both:
- water deficit,
- excess water.

41. Consumptive Water Use
Consumptive use generally refers to water consumed through processes such as:
- transpiration,
- evaporation,
- plant growth-related use.
In practical irrigation studies, ET is often used as the principal measure of crop water use.
The terminology can vary depending on the water-accounting framework.

42. Water Requirement in Different Growth
Stages
A simplified conceptual table:
Stage
Canopy
Water Use Water Sensitivity

Seedling

Important: this is a general pattern, not a universal schedule for every crop.

43. Peak Water Requirement
The period when crop water demand is highest is often called the peak water-use period.
It commonly occurs when:
- canopy is large,
- radiation is high,
- temperature is high,
- VPD is high,
- wind is significant.

Conceptually:
Peak ET≈High Atmospheric Demand+Large Active Canopy 

44. Daily Water Requirement
If:
ETc=5 mm/dayET c=5\ mm/day
then the crop is using approximately:
5 L/m2/day 
because:
1 mm=1 L/m2 

45. Converting Water Depth to Volume
For an area AA:
V=P×A 
where PP is water depth in metres.
For one hectare:
1 ha=10,000 m21\,ha=10,000\,m 2
Therefore:
1 mm=0.001m1\,mm=0.001m V=0.001×10,000V=0.001 10,000 =10m3= 
So:
1mm water over 1ha=10m3 
- r:
10,000L 

46. Example of Daily Crop Water Use
Suppose:
ETc=5mm/dayET c=5mm/day

for:
10ha10ha
Then daily water use is:
5×10×105 10 10 =500m3/day= 
before considering additional system losses or other water-balance terms.

47. Net vs Gross Irrigation Requirement
The crop/root zone needs a certain net amount of water.
But irrigation systems are not perfectly efficient.
Therefore:
Gross Irrigation>Net Irrigation 
Conceptually:
Gross Irrigation=Net IrrigationApplication Efficiency 
 Application\ Efficiency   
where efficiency is expressed as a fraction.
For example, if:
Net=40mmNet=40mm
and:
Efficiency=0.80Efficiency=0.80
then:
Gross=400.80Gross=  0.80  =50mm= 
This is a simplified calculation; actual irrigation design can involve distribution, conveyance and
application components separately.

48. Irrigation Efficiency
Irrigation efficiency describes how effectively supplied irrigation water contributes to the intended
crop/root-zone use.
A general conceptual expression is:

Efficiency=Useful WaterWater Applied×100   Water\
Applied   100  
Water can be lost through:
- runoff,
- deep percolation,
- evaporation,
- leakage,
- poor distribution.

49. Water Requirement vs Water Application
These are not identical.

Water requirement
What the crop/root-zone system needs.

Water application
What the irrigation system actually delivers.
Therefore:
Applied Water=Useful Water+Losses 
Good irrigation management attempts to reduce unnecessary losses while maintaining the required
root-zone water status.

50. Plant Water Requirement and Irrigation
Scheduling
Knowing water requirement helps answer:

When to irrigate?
When soil water approaches the chosen management threshold.

How much to irrigate?
Enough to replenish the intended root-zone deficit, considering system efficiency and constraints.

Thus:
Water Requirement→Irrigation Scheduling 

51. Irrigation Scheduling
Irrigation scheduling involves deciding:
1. when to irrigate, and
2. how much water to apply.
It considers:
- crop ET,
- soil water,
- rainfall,
- root-zone depth,
- crop stage,
- irrigation method,
- irrigation system capacity,
- weather.

52. Soil-Water Depletion
Suppose the root zone contains available water.
As the crop uses water:
ET→Soil Water Depletion 
If depletion becomes too large:
Depletion↑→Plant Stress↑ 
Irrigation replenishes part or all of the deficit depending on the management strategy.

53. Field Capacity and Irrigation
Immediately after heavy irrigation:
Soil→Near Saturation  After drainage:
Soil→Field Capacity  Crop water is then gradually depleted through:
ET→Soil Water Depletion 
The goal is generally not to keep the soil continuously saturated.

54. Management Allowable Depletion
A common irrigation concept is allowable soil-water depletion.
The farmer allows some water to be depleted before irrigating.
Conceptually:
RAW=TAW×p 
where:
- RAWRAW = readily allowable water depletion,
- TAWTAW = total available water,
- pp = fraction allowed to be depleted before irrigation.
The value of pp is crop- and condition-dependent.

55. Crop Water Requirement and Rainfed
Agriculture
Rainfed farming does not mean the crop has no water requirement.
It means water is primarily supplied by precipitation rather than controlled irrigation.
Crop Water Requirement exists in both irrigated and rainfed systems   
In rainfed agriculture:

Rainfall Timing+Amount+Distribution 
determine whether crop demand can be met.

56. Water Requirement and Rainfall
Distribution
Suppose two seasons receive the same total rainfall.

Season A
Rain arrives near crop demand.

Season B
Much rainfall arrives when crop demand is low and much is lost through runoff/deep drainage.
Their effective crop water supply can be very different.
Therefore:
Total Rainfall≠Effective Crop Water Supply 

57. Water Requirement and Drought
Drought occurs when available water is insufficient relative to crop demand over time.
Water Demand>Water Supply→Drought Stress 
Drought risk increases with:
- low rainfall,
- high temperature,
- high VPD,
- high radiation,
- high wind,
- low soil storage,
- shallow roots.

58. Water Requirement and Heat Stress
Heat increases atmospheric demand.
If soil water is inadequate:
Heat→Water Demand↑Heat  Water\ Demand and:
Water Deficit→Transpiration↓→Cooling↓Water\ Deficit  Transpiration  Cooling Therefore:
Heat+Drought→Compounded Plant Stress 

59. Water Requirement and Fertilizer
Water and nutrients interact.
Adequate water supports:
- nutrient dissolution,
- movement toward roots,
- root activity,
- uptake.
But excessive irrigation can cause:
- nutrient leaching,
- runoff,
- denitrification,
- nutrient loss.
Therefore:
Water Management↔Nutrient Management 

60. Water Requirement and Root Development
Adequate water supports root growth, but excessive water can restrict oxygen.

Thus:
Optimal Water→Healthy Root Environment 
while:
Drought→Root Water Stress  and:
Waterlogging→Root Oxygen Stress  61. Water Requirement and Water-Use
Efficiency
Water-use efficiency (WUE) relates production to water use.
A simplified expression is:
WUE=Biomass or YieldWater Used   Water\ Used   
For example:
WUE=5,000kg5,000m3WUE=  5,000m 3  =1kg/m3= 
WUE should not be interpreted as meaning that using less water is always better. A crop can have high
WUE but still produce low yield if water supply is severely restricted.

62. Water Productivity
A related agricultural concept is water productivity:
Water Productivity=Crop OutputWater Used  Water\
Used   
Depending on the study, the denominator may refer to irrigation water, evapotranspiration, or total
water consumption.
Therefore the definition should always specify the water term being used.

63. Plant Water Requirement vs Water-Use
Efficiency
These answer different questions.

Water requirement
How much water does the crop need?

Water-use efficiency
How much output is produced per unit of water used?
Requirement≠Efficiency 

64. Methods for Estimating Water Requirement
Several approaches can be used.

1. Weather-based ET estimation
Uses:
- temperature,
- radiation,
- humidity,
- wind.
Example:
ET0→ETcET 0 ET c
using KcK c.

2. Soil-water measurement
Monitor soil moisture using:
- gravimetric sampling,
- tensiometers,
- capacitance sensors,
- TDR,

- FDR,
- other soil-moisture sensors.

3. Crop observation
Indicators include:
- wilting,
- leaf rolling,
- canopy temperature,
- stomatal behavior,
- growth reduction.
Plant symptoms are useful but are generally late indicators compared with direct soil/weather
monitoring.

## 4. Lysimeters
Lysimeters can directly measure water loss from a soil–plant system under controlled conditions.

5. Remote sensing
Satellite or drone measurements can help estimate:
- vegetation condition,
- canopy temperature,
- ET,
- crop coefficient,
- spatial water stress.

65. Water Requirement Measurement Hierarchy
A practical system can combine:
Weather+SoilMoisture+CropStage+ETModel+FieldObservation 

This is generally more informative than relying on a single indicator.

66. Daily, Seasonal and Total Water
Requirement
Water requirement can be considered at different time scales.

Daily
ETc(mm/day)ET c(mm/day)

Monthly
∑ETc ET c

Seasonal
Seasonal Water Requirement=∑day=1nETc 
  n ET c  
This is important for irrigation-system planning.

67. Example of Seasonal Water Requirement
Suppose simplified daily crop ET values over three days are:
4, 5, 6 mm/day4,\ 5,\ 6\ mm/day
Then:
ETseason=4+5+6ET  season =4+5+6 =15mm= 
For actual crop planning, all relevant days of the crop season should be included.

68. Crop Water Requirement and Growth
Duration
Long-duration crops generally have a longer period during which water is used.
Conceptually:

Longer Crop Duration→More Days of Water Use 
But seasonal water requirement cannot be determined from duration alone because daily ET changes
with weather and canopy.

69. Crop Water Requirement and Canopy
Canopy development is a major driver of transpiration.
Canopy Development→Leaf Area→Transpiration→ET 
This is why KcK c changes during the season.

70. Plant Water Requirement and Crop
Coefficient
The complete conceptual calculation is:
ET0→Kc→ETc→Water Balance→Irrigation Requirement 
This is one of the most important chains in irrigation management.

71. Water Requirement Is Not Constant
It changes with:
Weather+CropStage+Canopy+Soil+Rooting+WaterAvailability+Management 
Therefore:
Water Requirement=f(Crop,Stage,Climate,Soil,Management) 

72. Practical Irrigation Principle
A good irrigation system attempts to:

Supply water when needed+Supply the appropriate amount+Minimize unnecessary losses  +   +    
Not:
Irrigate whenever the field looks dry  
and not:
Apply maximum water  

73. Four Major Questions
Plant water requirement helps answer:

1. How much water?
ETcET c

2. When is water needed?
Soil-water depletion + crop stage 

3. Where is water needed?
Root zone 

4. How should it be supplied?
Irrigation method + scheduling

75. Common Misconceptions

❌ “Plants use all the irrigation water.”
No.
Some water may be lost through:
- runoff,
- deep drainage,
- evaporation,
- leakage,
- inefficient distribution.

❌ “More irrigation means better growth.”
No.
Excess water can cause:
Waterlogging→O2↓→Root Stress   O 2  Root\
Stress

❌ “Crop water requirement equals irrigation requirement.”
No.
Rainfall and stored soil water can contribute.

❌ “Water requirement is constant throughout the crop season.”
No.
It changes with crop stage, canopy and weather.

❌ “Hot weather alone determines water requirement.”
No.
Humidity, wind, radiation, crop canopy and soil water also matter.

❌ “All rainfall is effective.”
No.
Runoff, evaporation and deep drainage can reduce the useful fraction.

❌ “If soil is wet, plants cannot be water stressed.”
Not necessarily.
Salinity, root damage, waterlogging and poor aeration can cause physiological stress even when soil
contains substantial water.

78. Master Concept
The most important idea to remember is:
Crop Water Requirement=Atmospheric Water Demand+Crop Characteristics+Soil–Root Water Supply
Relationship  =   +

In practical irrigation management:
ET0×Kc→ETc→Water Balance→Irrigation Requirement 

79. Exam-Ready Answer
Plant water requirement is the quantity of water required by a crop during a specified
period to meet water losses through evapotranspiration and support normal
physiological processes, growth, development, yield and quality under specified soil,
climatic, crop and management conditions. Crop evapotranspiration (ETcET c) is the
principal component used for estimating crop water use and is commonly estimated
from reference evapotranspiration (ET0ET 0) and the crop coefficient (KcK c):
ETc=KcET0ET c=K cET 0.

80. Final One-Line Memory
Plant Water Requirement = The water the crop needs to replace water losses and maintain healthy grow
th and production.   

And remember the complete chain:
Weather→ET0→Kc→ETc→Water Balance→Irrigation Requirement→Irrigation Scheduling   ET 0  K c  ET c     
Most important distinction:
Crop Water Requirement≠Irrigation Requirement    
because rainfall, stored soil water and other water sources can contribute to meeting the crop's
requirement.

## Module 11 — Water & Irrigation
## Management
2. Evapotranspiration (ET)
## 1. Introduction
Evapotranspiration (ET) is the combined loss of water to the atmosphere through evaporation from
soil, water and plant surfaces and transpiration from plants.
In the simplest form:
ET = Evaporation + Transpiration  
It is one of the most important concepts in agriculture because it tells us how rapidly water is being
transferred from the soil–plant system to the atmosphere.
ET is therefore central to:
- crop water requirement,
- irrigation scheduling,
- drought monitoring,
- water budgeting,
- irrigation-system design,
- crop productivity,
- water-use efficiency.

## 2. Definition
Evapotranspiration is the combined process by which water is transferred from the
soil, water and plant surfaces to the atmosphere through evaporation and plant
transpiration.
Thus:
ET=E+T 
where:
- ETET = evapotranspiration,
- EE = evaporation,
- TT = transpiration.

3. Why Is ET Important?
Plants continuously exchange water with the atmosphere.
The basic pathway is:
Soil Water→Roots→Plant→Leaves→Atmosphere 
At the same time:
Soil/Water Surface→Evaporation→Atmosphere 
Together:
E+T=ET 
Therefore ET provides a practical estimate of crop water use.

## 4. Evaporation
Evaporation is the conversion of liquid water into water vapor from non-living surfaces.
Agricultural evaporation can occur from:
- exposed soil,
- irrigation water,

- ponds,
- puddles,
- wet crop surfaces,
- wet residue.
E=Water loss from non-plant surfaces   

## 5. Transpiration
Transpiration is the loss of water vapor from living plant tissues, mainly through stomata.
Water moves:
Soil→Roots→Xylem→Leaves→Stomata→Atmosphere 
Therefore:
T=Plant water loss to the atmosphere   

6. Difference Between Evaporation and
Transpiration
Feature
Evaporation
Source
Soil/water/wet surfaces
Biological process No
Surface energy + atmospheric
Main control
demand
Major location
Exposed soil/water
Crop canopy
Often decreases soil exposure
effect
Part of ET
Yes
Together:
ET=E+T 

Transpiration
Plants
Yes
Stomata + plant water status + atmospheric
demand
Mainly leaves
Usually increases with active canopy
Yes

7. Evapotranspiration Is Not Just “Plant Water
Loss”
This is an important distinction.
ET includes:
Evaporation from the soil+Transpiration from the crop  +
   
Therefore, saying:
“ET is the amount of water used by the plant”
is an oversimplification.
A portion of ET may come from soil evaporation, especially when the soil surface is exposed.

8. Units of ET
ET is commonly expressed as a depth of water:
mm/day 
- r:
- mm/week,
- mm/month,
- mm/season.
The depth representation makes ET easy to use in irrigation calculations.

9. Important Conversion
1 mm=1 L/m2 
For one hectare:
1 ha=10,000m21\,ha=10,000m 2
Therefore:
1mm×10,000m2=10,000L1mm 10,000m 2 = 10,000L
- r:

1mm over 1ha=10m3 
Thus:
1mm=10,000L/ha 

## 10. Example
If:
ET=5mm/dayET=5mm/day
then approximately:
5L/m2/day5L/m 2/day
- f water is transferred to the atmosphere.
For 1 hectare:
5×10=50m3/day5 10=50m 3/day
Therefore:
ET=5mm/day⇒50m3/ha/day 
before considering the distinction between ET, irrigation application and other water-balance
components.

11. Reference Evapotranspiration — ET0ET 0
One of the most important concepts in irrigation is:
ET0 
called reference evapotranspiration.
It represents the atmospheric evaporative demand from a standardized reference surface under defined
conditions.
It allows weather conditions to be separated from crop-specific characteristics.
Conceptually:
Weather→ET0 
Then:
ET0→Kc→ETc 

12. Why Do We Need ET0ET 0?
Different crops have different:
- canopy structures,
- leaf areas,
- rooting systems,
- stomatal behavior,
- growth stages.
Instead of calculating water use separately from scratch for every crop, we first estimate atmospheric
demand:
ET0ET 0
and then adjust it for the crop:
ETc=KcET0ET c=K cET 0

13. Crop Evapotranspiration — ETcET c
The water use of a specific crop under standard non-stressed conditions is commonly represented as:
ETc=Kc×ET0 
where:
- ETcET c = crop evapotranspiration,
- KcK c = crop coefficient,
- ET0ET 0 = reference evapotranspiration.
This is a fundamental irrigation-management relationship.

14. Crop Coefficient KcK c
The crop coefficient represents how the crop's water use differs from the reference surface.
It depends on:
- crop type,
- growth stage,
- canopy development,

- ground cover,
- crop architecture,
- aerodynamic characteristics.
Therefore:
Kc=f(Crop, Growth Stage, Canopy) 

15. Crop Growth Stages and KcK c
A typical conceptual pattern is:
Initial→Development→Mid−season→Late−season 

Initial stage
Small canopy:
Kc→relatively lowK c Development stage
Canopy expands:
Kc↑K c Mid-season
Large active canopy:
Kc→highK c Late season
Senescence and maturity:
Kc↓K c The exact values are crop- and method-specific.

16. Single Crop Coefficient Concept
A basic approach is:
ETc=KcET0 

This combines the crop's effects into one coefficient.
It is widely useful for practical irrigation planning.

17. Dual Crop Coefficient Concept
A more detailed approach separates soil evaporation from crop transpiration.
ETc=(Kcb+Ke)ET0 +K e)ET 0  
where:
- KcbK  cb  = basal crop coefficient representing crop transpiration under non-stressed
conditions,
- KeK e = soil evaporation coefficient.
This is particularly useful when the soil surface alternates between:
- wet,
- dry,
- partially covered,
- exposed.

18. Single vs Dual Crop Coefficient
Approach
Main idea
Single KcK c
Crop water use represented by one coefficient
Dual Kcb+KeK  cb +K e Separates crop transpiration and soil evaporation
Single approach
Simpler
Dual approach
More detailed, especially for variable soil wetness

19. ET and Crop Canopy
When the crop is young:
Small canopy→Lower transpiration   As canopy develops:
Leaf area↑→Transpiration potential↑    At full canopy:
Active leaf area→large  Later:
Senescence→Active leaf area↓    Therefore ET changes throughout the crop season.

20. Soil Evaporation During Crop Development
At early stages:
Exposed Soil↑→E↑ potential   E  
As canopy closes:
Soil Shading↑→E↓   E  
while:
T↑T Therefore the relative contributions of evaporation and transpiration change over time.

21. ET During Early and Late Crop Stages
Early stage
ET=E+TET=E+T
with evaporation potentially making a large contribution.

Full canopy
Transpiration often becomes the dominant component.

Senescence
Transpiration decreases as active leaf area declines.
Therefore:
E/T Ratio Changes During the Season 

22. Factors Affecting Evapotranspiration
ET is controlled by several interacting factors.
The major groups are:
Weather+Crop+Soil+Water+Management 

## 23. Temperature
Generally:
Temperature↑→Atmospheric Demand↑Temperature  Atmospheric\
Demand Higher temperature can increase:
- saturation vapor pressure,
- vapor pressure deficit,
- evaporative demand.
But temperature alone does not determine ET.

24. Relative Humidity
When relative humidity decreases:
RH↓→VPD↑RH  VPD and atmospheric drying demand generally increases.
VPD=es−ea 
Thus:
RH↓→ET Demand↑ 
when other conditions permit.

## 25. Wind
Wind removes humid air around the plant surface.

Wind↑→Boundary Layer↓→Vapor Transfer↑ 
Therefore wind can increase ET, particularly when water is available and atmospheric conditions
support evaporation.

26. Solar Radiation
Solar radiation provides energy for evaporation and transpiration.
Generally:
Radiation↑→ET Potential↑ 
Therefore sunny conditions can produce higher evaporative demand than cloudy conditions under
- therwise similar circumstances.

27. Soil Moisture
ET is not determined solely by atmospheric demand.
When soil water becomes severely limited:
Soil Water↓→Stomatal Closure→Transpiration↓ 
Therefore actual ET can become lower than potential/reference-driven demand under water
stress.
This distinction is extremely important.

28. Potential ET vs Actual ET
Potential/reference-driven demand
Describes how much water could be transferred under adequate water supply and specified conditions.

Actual ET
The amount actually transferred under the existing soil-water and crop conditions.
Therefore:
ETactual≤ETdemand  ET  demand   

conceptually when water stress limits transpiration.

29. Crop Stress Coefficient
Under water stress, crop evapotranspiration can be adjusted using a stress coefficient:
ETc=KsKcET0 
where:
- KsK s = crop water-stress coefficient,
- KcK c = crop coefficient,
- ET0ET 0 = reference ET.
For a non-stressed crop:
Ks≈1K s 1
Under stress:
Ks<1K s<1
The exact method and values depend on the water-balance framework.

30. Water Stress and ET
This creates an important feedback:
Soil Water↓  ↓
Plant Water Stress↑  ↓
Stomatal Closure↑  ↓
Transpiration↓  ↓
ETactual↓   
But lower ET in this situation does not mean the crop is healthier.
It can indicate severe water stress.

31. ET and Plant Water Requirement
Crop water requirement is closely related to ET.
Under standard non-stressed conditions:
Crop Water Use≈ETc 
This is why ET is the foundation of many irrigation calculations.

32. ET and Irrigation Requirement
ET tells us how much water has been lost from the crop–soil system.
The irrigation system must replace an appropriate portion of that loss while considering:
- rainfall,
- stored soil water,
- drainage,
- runoff,
- irrigation efficiency,
- groundwater contribution where relevant.
Conceptually:
ET→Soil Water Depletion→Irrigation Need 

33. Soil-Water Balance
A general water-balance equation is:
ΔS=P+I+CR−R−D−ET 
where:
- ΔS S = change in soil-water storage,
- PP = precipitation,
- II = irrigation,

- CRCR = capillary rise,
- RR = runoff,
- DD = drainage/deep percolation,
- ETET = evapotranspiration.
This connects ET with the rest of irrigation management.

34. ET and Rainfall
Suppose:
ET=6mm/dayET=6mm/day
and effective rainfall provides:
3mm3mm
Then the rainfall does not necessarily meet the entire daily crop water loss.
The remaining deficit may need to come from:
- soil-water storage,
- irrigation,
- other water sources.
Therefore:
ET≠Irrigation 

35. ET and Irrigation Scheduling
Irrigation scheduling can use accumulated ET.
For example:
ET1+ET2+ET3+⋯+ETnET 1+ET 2+ET 3+ +ET n
can estimate cumulative water depletion, after accounting for rainfall and other water inputs.
Thus:
Cumulative ET→Soil Water Depletion→Irrigation Decision 

36. Daily ET
Suppose:
ET0=5mm/dayET 0=5mm/day
and:
Kc=0.8K c=0.8
Then:
ETc=0.8×5ET c=0.8 5 ETc=4mm/day 
This means the estimated crop evapotranspiration under the assumptions of the method is
approximately 4 mm/day.

37. Water Balance Example
Suppose over one day:
P=2mmP=2mm I=3mmI=3mm ET=4mmET=4mm
Ignoring runoff, drainage and capillary rise for simplicity:
ΔS=2+3−4 S=2+3-4 ΔS=1mm 
So soil-water storage increases by approximately 1 mm.

38. ET and Soil Texture
Soil texture affects ET indirectly through:
- water storage,
- infiltration,
- drainage,
- root water availability.

Sandy soil
Rapid drainage and low storage can cause water stress sooner.

Clay soil
Greater storage but potentially poor aeration when saturated.

Loam
Often provides a balanced soil-water environment.
Thus:
Soil Properties→Water Availability→Actual ET 

39. ET and Soil Surface
Soil surface conditions strongly influence evaporation.

Bare wet soil
E↑E Mulched soil
E↓E Dense crop canopy
Soil exposure↓  and evaporation may decrease.

40. ET and Mulching
Mulch can reduce direct evaporation by:
- shading soil,
- reducing wind at the soil surface,
- changing soil temperature,
- limiting direct energy transfer.
Conceptually:
Mulch→Soil Evaporation↓→Water Conservation 
However, the overall crop water balance can vary with mulch type, rainfall, soil and climate.

41. ET and Plant Population
Higher plant population can increase total canopy development.
Population↑→Leaf Area↑→Transpiration Potential↑      But this relationship is not necessarily linear.
Very dense canopies can also:
- shade soil,
- reduce evaporation,
- increase competition.
Therefore total ET must be considered as:
ET=E+T 
rather than considering transpiration alone.

42. ET and LAI
Leaf Area Index:
LAI=Total one−sided leaf areaGround area  Ground\
area   
As LAI increases:
Transpiring Surface↑  until canopy effects approach a maximum or plateau.
Therefore:
LAI→Canopy Development→T→ET 

43. ET and Crop Growth Stage
A simplified relationship:
Growth Stage→Kc→ETc   K c  ET c  
As the crop progresses:

Kc,initial→Kc,development→Kc,mid→Kc,lateK  c,initial   K  c,development 
 K  c,mid   K  c,late 
Therefore irrigation demand changes with crop development.

44. ET and Critical Growth Stages
A crop may have:
- high ET demand,
- high water sensitivity,
- or both
at particular stages.
For example, reproductive stages are often important for yield formation.
Therefore irrigation management should consider both:
Water Demand   
and:
Water Sensitivity   
They are not identical.

45. ET and Heat Stress
Under high temperature:
Atmospheric Demand↑  If water is available:
ET Potential↑ET\ Potential If water becomes limited:
Stomatal Closure↑→T↓   T and the crop can become hotter.
Thus:
High ET Demand+Low Soil Water→Heat+Drought Stress 

46. ET and VPD
Vapor pressure deficit is:
VPD=es−ea 
where:
- ese s = saturation vapor pressure,
- eae a = actual vapor pressure.
Higher VPD generally means stronger atmospheric drying demand.
VPD↑→Transpiration Demand↑ 
provided stomata and soil water allow it.

47. ET and Wind–Humidity Interaction
Consider:

High temperature + low RH + high wind
These conditions can produce:
High Atmospheric Evaporative Demand 
But:

High humidity + low wind
can produce lower atmospheric drying demand.
Therefore ET is controlled by multiple weather variables together.

48. ET and Waterlogging
ET may behave differently under waterlogging.
Waterlogging causes:
Soil Oxygen↓  ↓
Root Function↓  ↓

Water Uptake↓  So despite abundant water:
Plant Transpiration can decrease 
This is why:
More Soil Water≠More Healthy ET 

49. ET and Salinity
Salinity reduces plant water uptake.
Salinity↑→Osmotic Stress↑    ↓
Water Uptake↓  ↓
Stomatal Closure↑  ↓
T↓ 
Thus salinity can reduce actual crop ET even though soil contains water.

50. Potential ET, Reference ET and Crop ET
These terms should not be mixed.
Term
Meaning
Evaporation Water loss from surfaces
Transpiration Water loss through plants
ET
Evaporation + transpiration
ET0ET 0
Reference-surface evapotranspiration
ETcET c
Crop evapotranspiration
Atmospheric-demand concept under adequate water conditions; terminology varies by
Potential ET
method
Actual ET
ET occurring under actual soil/crop conditions
The most useful standard irrigation chain is:
ET0→Kc→ETc 

51. Methods of Estimating ET
1. Weather-based methods
Use weather data such as:
- temperature,
- radiation,
- humidity,
- wind.
The most widely used standardized approach is FAO-56 Penman–Monteith.
ET0=0.408Δ(Rn−G)+γ900T+273u2(es−ea)Δ+γ(1+0.34u2)  T+273 u 2(e s-e a)     + (1+0.34u 2)    

52. Lysimeter Method
A lysimeter is an instrument/system that can measure water loss from a defined soil–plant system,
- ften through changes in mass or water balance.
Conceptually:
Water Balance Measurement→ET Estimate 
Lysimeters are useful for research and calibration.

53. Soil-Water Balance Method
ET can be estimated by monitoring:
- rainfall,
- irrigation,
- soil-water storage,
- runoff,
- drainage,
- groundwater contribution.
Using:

ΔS=P+I+CR−R−D−ET 
ET can be inferred if the other terms are measured or estimated.

54. Remote Sensing
Satellite and drone observations can help estimate ET using information related to:
- land surface temperature,
- vegetation,
- radiation,
- canopy condition,
- surface energy balance.
This is useful for spatially variable irrigation management.

55. Soil Moisture Sensors
Sensors such as:
- TDR,
- FDR/capacitance sensors,
- other moisture probes
can monitor soil water.
They do not directly measure ET in the simplest sense, but they can support:
ET/Water Balance→Irrigation Scheduling 

56. ET and Irrigation Methods
Different irrigation systems influence water distribution and evaporation.

Flood irrigation
Potentially greater surface wetting and evaporation.

Furrow irrigation
Water is concentrated in furrows; evaporation depends on wetting pattern.

Sprinkler irrigation
Water is exposed to air during application, creating potential interception/evaporation losses.

Drip irrigation
Applies water to localized soil zones and can reduce unnecessary wetting of exposed soil in suitable
systems.
However:
Irrigation Method≠ET 
ET is primarily a crop–soil–atmosphere process; irrigation method influences how efficiently water is
supplied to meet it.

57. ET and Water-Use Efficiency
Water-use efficiency may be represented as:
WUE=Biomass or YieldWater Used   Water\ Used   
ET is often used as part of water-productivity calculations.
A crop with lower ET does not automatically have higher productivity.

58. ET and Crop Water Productivity
A common conceptual relationship is:
Water Productivity=Crop OutputWater Used  Water\
Used   
The precise denominator must be defined:
- irrigation water,
- evapotranspiration,
- total water consumed,
- etc.

59. ET and Climate Change
Changes in:
- temperature,
- humidity,
- wind,
- radiation,
- rainfall patterns
can alter atmospheric evaporative demand and therefore crop water requirements.
A warming climate can increase water demand in many situations, but actual ET also depends on water
availability and plant response.
Thus:
Climate→Atmospheric Demand→ET→Irrigation Requirement 

60. ET and Microclimate
Field microclimate affects ET through:
- canopy temperature,
- wind,
- humidity,
- radiation,
- soil moisture.
For example:
Dense Canopy→Wind↓   Wind and:
Soil Shading↑→Evaporation↓   Evaporation Thus microclimate can significantly modify local ET.

61. Complete ET Concept
The entire process can be summarized as:
Solar Energy+Temperature+Humidity+Wind  +   +
  +    
↓
Atmospheric Evaporative Demand   
↓
Evaporation + Transpiration   
↓
ET 
while soil and crop conditions determine how much of that atmospheric demand can actually occur.

62. Complete Irrigation Chain
This is the most important chain for Module 11:
Weather 
↓
ET0 
↓
Kc 
↓
ETc 
↓
Rainfall+Soil Water+Groundwater 
↓
Water Balance 
↓
Soil Water Depletion 
↓
Irrigation Requirement 

↓
Irrigation Scheduling 

63. Most Important Relationships
Relationship 1
ET=E+T 

Relationship 2
ETc=KcET0 

Relationship 3
ETc=KsKcET0 
when a crop stress coefficient is used.

Relationship 4
ΔS=P+I+CR−R−D−ET 

Relationship 5
VPD=es−ea 

64. Common Misconceptions

❌ ET means only transpiration.
No.
ET=E+T 

❌ ET and irrigation requirement are the same.
No.
Irrigation must account for rainfall, soil-water storage and other water-balance terms.

❌ Higher ET always means healthier crop.
No.

High ET may indicate high atmospheric demand, while low ET may result from severe water stress.

❌ More soil water always increases ET.
No.
Waterlogging can restrict roots and reduce transpiration.

❌ Temperature alone determines ET.
No.
Radiation, humidity, wind, crop and soil conditions also matter.

❌ ET0ET 0 is the water requirement of every crop.
No.
It is reference-surface evapotranspiration.
Crop-specific ET is estimated using crop coefficients:
ETc=KcET0 

❌ ET remains constant throughout the season.
No.
Crop canopy and weather change continuously.

65. Exam-Ready Definition
Evapotranspiration (ET) is the combined loss of water from the soil, water and plant
surfaces to the atmosphere through evaporation and plant transpiration. It is a major
component of crop water use and is widely used for estimating crop water
requirements and irrigation needs. Crop evapotranspiration is commonly estimated
from reference evapotranspiration and the crop coefficient using
ETc=KcET0ET c=K cET 0.

66. Final Master Summary
Evapotranspiration = Evaporation + Transpiration   

Evaporation
Soil/Water Surface→Atmosphere   

Transpiration
Plant→Atmosphere   

Total ET
ET=E+T 

Reference ET
ET0=Atmospheric demand from a standardized reference surface  

Crop ET
ETc=KcET0 

Under water stress
ETc=KsKcET0 

Water balance
ΔS=P+I+CR−R−D−ET 

The complete concept
Weather→ET0→Kc→ETc→Water Balance→Irrigation Requirement→Scheduling   ET 0  K c  ET c     
One-line memory:
ET is the water leaving the soil–plant system through evaporation and transpiration, and it is the key qu
antity used to determine how much water a crop is using and when irrigation may be needed.

## Module 11 — Water & Irrigation
## Management
3. Soil Moisture
## 1. Introduction
Soil moisture is the amount of water present in the soil pores at a particular time.
It is one of the most important components of agricultural water management because soil acts as a
water reservoir for plant roots.
The basic pathway is:
Rainfall/Irrigation→Soil→Water Storage→Roots→Plant→Atmosphere 
Soil moisture determines whether water is:
- available to roots,
- too strongly held for roots to extract,
- moving through the soil,
- draining away,
- evaporating,
- causing waterlogging.

## 2. Definition
Soil moisture is the water contained within the pore spaces of soil, including water
available to plants and water held too strongly or weakly for effective plant uptake.
In agriculture, we are particularly interested in:
How much soil water is available to the crop root system?  

3. Why Soil Moisture Is Important
Soil moisture affects:
1. seed germination,

2. seedling establishment,
3. root growth,
4. nutrient availability,
5. nutrient transport,
6. photosynthesis,
7. transpiration,
8. plant water status,
9. microbial activity,
10.fertilizer efficiency,
11.crop growth,
12.flowering,
13.fruit/grain/pod development,
14.yield,
15.irrigation scheduling.
The fundamental relationship is:
Soil Moisture→Root Water Uptake→Plant Water Status→Growth→Yield 

4. Where Is Water Stored in Soil?
Soil consists broadly of:
Solid particles+Water+Air + +   
The spaces between soil particles are called pores.
These pores may contain:
- water,
- air,
- or both.
Therefore:
Soil Pores=Water-filled pores+Air-filled pores = +
   

5. Soil Moisture and Soil Porosity
Recall:
n=VpVt×100  V t  100  
where:
- nn = total porosity,
- VpV p = pore volume,
- VtV t = total soil volume.
The pore space determines the maximum potential space available for water and air.
But:
High Porosity≠High Plant-Available Water   
because pore size and connectivity matter.

6. Macropores and Micropores
Macropores
Large pores generally facilitate:
- drainage,
- air movement,
- rapid water movement.

Micropores
Small pores generally retain:
- capillary water,
- plant-available water,
- strongly held water.
Thus:
Macropores→Drainage + Aeration   
Micropores→Water Retention   
A good agricultural soil needs an appropriate balance.

7. Soil Water Is Not All Equally Available
This is one of the most important concepts.
Water in soil can be broadly classified as:
1. gravitational water,
2. capillary water,
3. hygroscopic water.

8. Gravitational Water
Gravitational water is water that drains downward under gravity after the soil becomes wet.
It is mainly associated with larger pores.
Immediately after heavy rainfall or irrigation:
Gravitational Water→Drainage   
It is generally not the main long-term source of plant-available water.
Excess gravitational water can cause:
Waterlogging→O2↓→Root Stress 

9. Capillary Water
Capillary water is held in smaller soil pores by capillary forces.
It is an important source of plant-available water.
Conceptually:
Capillary Water→Main Plant Water Source   
However, as the soil dries, water becomes increasingly difficult for roots to extract.

10. Hygroscopic Water
Hygroscopic water is held very tightly around soil particles, especially clay particles.
Plants generally cannot extract most of it effectively.
Thus:

Strongly Held Water→Low Plant Availability   

11. Field Capacity
Field capacity (FC) is the amount of water remaining in soil after excess gravitational water has
drained under defined conditions.
Conceptually:
Saturation→Drainage→Field Capacity 
Field capacity is an important reference point for irrigation management.

## 12. Saturation
A soil is approximately saturated when most or essentially all soil pores are filled with water.
Therefore:
Water-Filled Pores↑→Air-Filled Pores↓ Filled\ Pores  Air Filled\ Pores  
and:
O2 Availability↓ 
This is why prolonged saturation can damage many crops.

13. Permanent Wilting Point
Permanent wilting point (PWP) is a reference soil-water condition at which a plant remains wilted
and cannot recover its water status under the specified reference conditions.
It represents a very dry soil-water condition.
Therefore:
FC>PWP 
in terms of volumetric or gravimetric water content under the same measurement basis.

14. Available Water
The simplest important equation is:

AWC≈FC−PWP 
where:
- AWCAWC = available water capacity,
- FCFC = water content at field capacity,
- PWPPWP = water content at permanent wilting point.
This represents the approximate range of soil water between these two reference states.

15. Example of Available Water
Suppose:
FC=30%FC=30\%
and:
PWP=15%PWP=15\%
Then:
AWC=30−15AWC=30-15 AWC=15% 
The meaning depends on whether these values are expressed on a volumetric or gravimetric basis.

16. Total Available Water in the Root Zone
For a root zone:
TAW≈AWC×Zr 
where:
- TAWTAW = total available water in the root zone,
- ZrZ r = effective root-zone depth.
If:
AWC=0.15 m3/m3AWC=0.15\,m 3/m 3
and:
Zr=0.60mZ r=0.60m
then:
TAW=0.15×0.60TAW=0.15 0.60 =0.09m=0.09m
- r:

90mm 
- f approximate available water storage.

17. Root Zone
The root zone is the portion of soil from which the crop obtains significant water and nutrients.
Its depth depends on:
- crop,
- variety,
- soil,
- compaction,
- water table,
- soil moisture,
- growth stage.
Thus:
Root Zone=f(Crop+Soil+Growth+Environment) 

18. Soil Moisture Profile
Soil moisture is not necessarily uniform throughout the soil profile.
For example:
0−15cm:wet0-15cm:  15−30cm:moderately wet15-30cm: 
30−60cm:dry30-60cm: 
The crop's actual water supply depends on:
- root distribution,
- water content at each depth,
- soil texture,
- soil structure,
- hydraulic connectivity.
Therefore:

Average Soil Moisture≠Complete Root-Zone Water Status 
  

19. Soil Moisture and Texture
Sandy Soil
Generally:
- large pores,
- rapid infiltration,
- rapid drainage,
- low water-storage capacity.
Therefore:
Sandy Soil→Frequent Irrigation May Be Needed 
under suitable climatic conditions.

Clay Soil
Generally:
- many small pores,
- high water retention,
- slower drainage,
- some water held very tightly.
Therefore:
Clay Soil→High Retention but Potentially Difficult Drainage 

Loam Soil
Often provides a relatively balanced combination of:
- water retention,
- drainage,
- aeration.

But actual behavior depends on structure and organic matter as well as texture.

20. Soil Moisture and Organic Matter
Organic matter can influence:
- aggregation,
- pore structure,
- water retention,
- infiltration,
- microbial activity.
Conceptually:
Organic Matter→Better Soil Structure→Improved Water Storage and Movement 
The magnitude of the effect depends on soil, amendment type, amount and management.

21. Soil Moisture and Soil Structure
Good aggregation can create a useful mixture of:
- large pores,
- medium pores,
- small pores.
This can improve the balance between:
Water Storage+Drainage+Aeration 
Poor structure can cause:
- crusting,
- compaction,
- poor infiltration,
- poor aeration,
- runoff.

22. Soil Moisture and Bulk Density
Bulk density is:
ρb=Dry Soil MassBulk Soil Volume  Bulk\ Soil\ Volume   
As compaction increases:
Bulk Density↑Bulk\ Density - ften accompanied by:
Porosity↓Porosity particularly loss of large pores.
This can reduce:
- infiltration,
- drainage,
- root penetration,
- aeration.

23. Soil Moisture and Air-Filled Porosity
A useful relationship is:
AFP=n−θv 
where:
- AFPAFP = air-filled porosity,
- nn = total porosity,
- θv  v = volumetric water content.
As soil moisture increases:
θv↑  v therefore:
AFP↓AFP and oxygen availability can decline.

## 24. Waterlogging
When excessive soil moisture fills most pores:
Soil Moisture↑→AFP↓→O2↓ 
This can cause:
- root respiration problems,
- nutrient imbalance,
- denitrification,
- toxic reduced compounds,
- root disease,
- stunted growth.
Thus:
Too Much Water Can Be Harmful 

25. Soil Moisture and Root Water Uptake
Roots absorb water mainly from the soil solution.
Water movement toward roots occurs through:
- mass flow,
- root uptake,
- soil-water redistribution.
A simplified pathway is:
Soil→RootSurface→Root→Xylem→Shoot 

26. Soil Water Potential
Water movement is driven by differences in water potential.
Water tends to move from higher water potential toward lower water potential.
Conceptually:
Soil→Root→Xylem→Leaf→Atmosphere 

as water potential generally becomes progressively more negative along the transpiration pathway.

27. Components of Soil Water Potential
Soil water potential can involve several components, including:
- matric potential,
- osmotic potential,
- gravitational potential,
- pressure potential in saturated conditions.
A conceptual representation is:
Ψ=Ψm+Ψs+Ψg+Ψp 
The importance of each component depends on the soil and measurement conditions.

28. Matric Potential
Matric potential describes the effect of attraction between water and soil particles and the capillary
forces within pores.
As soil dries:
∣Ψm∣↑→Water becomes harder to extract 
This is why soil water content alone does not fully describe plant availability.

29. Salinity and Soil Moisture
A saline soil may contain considerable water but still make water difficult for plants to absorb.
Salinity↑→Osmotic Potential More Negative→Water Uptake Difficulty↑ 
Therefore:
Soil Water Content≠Plant Water Availability 

30. Soil Moisture and Evapotranspiration
Recall:

ET=E+T 
When soil water is adequate:
Water Supply→Transpiration can meet atmospheric demand 
 When soil water becomes limiting:
Soil Moisture↓→Stomatal Closure→Transpiration↓ 
Therefore soil moisture is a major control on actual ET.

31. Soil Moisture and Drought
When water availability decreases:
Soil Moisture↓→Plant Water Status↓ 
Then:
- stomata close,
- photosynthesis decreases,
- leaf expansion decreases,
- roots may change allocation,
- flowering/fruiting may be affected,
- yield may decline.

32. Soil Moisture and Germination
Seeds require sufficient water for imbibition.
Dry Seed→Water Uptake→Metabolic Activation→Germination 
Too little moisture:
Imbibition↓→Germination failure/delay   Too much water:
Oxygen availability↓→Germination problems   Thus successful germination requires an appropriate balance of:
Water+O2+Temperature 

33. Soil Moisture and Nutrient Availability
Water is necessary for:
- dissolving nutrients,
- transporting nutrients,
- root uptake,
- microbial processes.
But excessive water can cause nutrient losses through:
- leaching,
- runoff,
- denitrification.
Therefore:
Water Management↔Nutrient Management 

34. Soil Moisture and Nitrogen
Nitrogen transformations are strongly influenced by soil moisture.
Under excessive wetness:
O2↓O 2 and denitrification can increase under suitable conditions.
Under very dry conditions:
- microbial activity can decline,
- nutrient movement toward roots can decrease.
Thus appropriate soil moisture supports effective nitrogen cycling.

35. Soil Moisture and Phosphorus
Phosphorus movement toward roots occurs mainly through diffusion.

Adequate soil moisture supports diffusion.
When soil becomes extremely dry:
P Diffusion↓→P Supply to Roots↓ 
Therefore drought can create nutrient stress even when soil contains phosphorus.

36. Soil Moisture and Potassium
Potassium movement to roots occurs through:
- diffusion,
- mass flow.
Soil moisture affects both processes.
Dry soil can reduce nutrient movement and root activity.

## 37. Infiltration
Infiltration is the entry of water from the soil surface into the soil.
Rainfall/Irrigation→Soil Surface→Infiltration 
The rate depends on:
- texture,
- structure,
- initial moisture,
- compaction,
- crusting,
- organic matter,
- vegetation,
- rainfall intensity.

38. Infiltration vs Soil Moisture
These are different concepts.

Infiltration
How water enters the soil.

Soil moisture
How much water is currently stored in the soil.
Therefore:
Infiltration≠Soil Moisture 

## 39. Percolation
Percolation is downward movement of water through the soil profile.
Surface→Root Zone→Deeper Soil 
Excessive percolation below the root zone can cause:
Water+Nutrients→Deep Drainage/Loss 

## 40. Runoff
When rainfall/application exceeds the soil's infiltration capacity or surface storage:
Runoff↑ 
Runoff can transport:
- soil,
- nutrients,
- pesticides,
- organic matter.
Thus poor soil structure can reduce effective soil-water storage by increasing runoff.

41. Capillary Rise
Water can sometimes move upward from a shallow water table through capillary forces.
Water Table→Capillary Rise→Root Zone 
This can contribute useful water under some conditions but can also bring salts into the root zone.

42. Soil Moisture Cycle
A simplified agricultural soil-water cycle is:
Rainfall+Irrigation 
↓
Infiltration 
↓
Soil Water Storage 
↓
Root Uptake+Evaporation 
↓
ET 
with additional pathways:
Runoff+Deep Percolation+Drainage+Capillary Rise 

43. Soil Water Balance
The overall balance can be expressed as:
ΔS=P+I+CR−R−D−ET 
where:
- ΔS S = change in soil-water storage,
- PP = precipitation,
- II = irrigation,
- CRCR = capillary rise,
- RR = runoff,
- DD = drainage/deep percolation,
- ETET = evapotranspiration.
This is one of the most important equations in irrigation management.

44. Positive Soil-Water Balance
If:
P+I+CR>R+D+ETP+I+CR>R+D+ET
then:
ΔS>0 
So soil moisture increases.

45. Negative Soil-Water Balance
If:
P+I+CR<R+D+ETP+I+CR<R+D+ET
then:
ΔS<0 
So stored soil moisture decreases.
If depletion becomes severe:
Plant Water Stress↑ 

46. Soil Moisture and Irrigation Scheduling
This is one of the biggest practical applications.
The farmer needs to determine:

When to irrigate?
When soil water depletion reaches a chosen management threshold.

How much to irrigate?
Enough to replenish the intended root-zone deficit, while accounting for irrigation-system efficiency
and losses.
Thus:
Soil Moisture→Water Depletion→Irrigation Timing 

47. Total Available Water — TAW
Total available water in the effective root zone can be approximated as:
TAW=(θFC−θPWP)Zr - )Z r  
where:
- θFC  = volumetric water content at field capacity,
- θPWP  = volumetric water content at permanent wilting point,
- ZrZ r = root-zone depth.

48. Readily Available Water — RAW
Plants can experience increasing stress before all theoretical available water is exhausted.
A common irrigation concept is:
RAW=p×TAW 
where:
- RAWRAW = readily available water,
- pp = allowable depletion fraction.
When the soil-water depletion approaches the management threshold, irrigation may be scheduled.
The value of pp depends on crop, atmospheric demand and management conditions.

49. Soil Moisture Measurement
Soil moisture can be measured using several methods.

1. Gravimetric method
A soil sample is:
1. collected,
2. weighed wet,
3. dried,
4. weighed again.
Water mass:
Mw=Mwet−MdryM w=M  wet -M  dry 

Gravimetric water content:
θg=Mwet−MdryMdry -M  dry    M  dry    

50. Volumetric Water Content
Volumetric water content is:
θv=VwVt  V t   
where:
- VwV w = volume of water,
- VtV t = total soil volume.
It is commonly expressed as:
m3/m3m 3/m 3
- r percentage.

51. Gravimetric vs Volumetric Water Content
Feature

Gravimetric

Volumetric

Based on
Mass
Volume
Formula
Water mass / dry soil mass Water volume / total soil volume
Unit
kg/kg or %
m³/m³ or %
Field irrigation use
Useful
Very useful
Requires bulk density for conversion Yes, generally
No for definition
Relationship:
θv=θg×ρb/ρw 
where:
- ρb  b = bulk density,
- ρw  w = water density.

52. Soil Moisture Sensors
Modern agriculture can use:

TDR
Time Domain Reflectometry

Measures electromagnetic signal behavior related to soil dielectric properties.

FDR/Capacitance
Measures dielectric properties using electromagnetic techniques.

Tensiometers
Measure soil-water suction/matric potential over an applicable range.

Neutron moisture meters
Can estimate soil water through neutron moderation, but require specialized equipment and regulatory
considerations.

53. Soil Moisture Content vs Soil Water
Potential
These are different.

Soil moisture content
How much water is present.

Soil-water potential
How strongly that water is held and its energetic state.
Thus:
Amount of Water≠Ease of Water Extraction 
This distinction is crucial.

54. Soil Moisture and Irrigation Methods
Different irrigation methods distribute water differently.

Flood irrigation
Large surface area may be wetted.

Furrow irrigation
Water moves through furrows and infiltrates into surrounding soil.

Sprinkler
Water is applied over the soil/crop surface.

Drip
Water is applied locally near the root zone.
Therefore irrigation method affects:
- wetting pattern,
- evaporation,
- infiltration,
- runoff,
- deep percolation,
- root-zone moisture distribution.

55. Drip Irrigation and Soil Moisture
With drip irrigation, soil moisture often forms a localized wetted zone.
Conceptually:
Emitter→Localized Wetting Pattern→Root Zone 
The shape depends on:
- soil texture,
- discharge rate,
- application duration,
- spacing,
- soil structure.

56. Soil Moisture and Mulching
Mulch can reduce soil evaporation.
Mulch→Evaporation↓→Soil Moisture Conservation 
It can also affect:

- soil temperature,
- infiltration,
- runoff,
- weed growth.

57. Soil Moisture and Conservation Tillage
Reduced soil disturbance and residue retention can help maintain soil structure and surface cover.
Conceptually:
Residue Cover→Evaporation↓+Raindrop Impact↓ 
This can support soil-water conservation, although actual outcomes depend on climate, soil and
management.

58. Soil Moisture and Weeds
Weeds also consume soil water.
Crop+Weed→Shared Soil Water 
Therefore:
Poor Weed Control→Water Competition↑ 
Effective weed management can improve water availability to the crop.

59. Soil Moisture and Plant Population
Plant population influences total water demand.
Population↑→Canopy↑→Transpiration Potential↑Population  Canopy  Transpiration\ Potential At the same time, higher population increases competition for available soil moisture.
Thus:
Plant Population↔Soil Water Demand 

60. Soil Moisture and Root Architecture
Root depth and distribution determine how much soil water can be accessed.
A crop with deeper roots may access deeper soil moisture if those layers are:
- physically accessible,
- adequately aerated,
- non-saline,
- suitable for root growth.
Therefore:
Root Architecture→Soil Volume Explored→Water Access 

61. Soil Moisture and Soil Compaction
Compaction can produce a difficult combination:
Compaction→Macropores↓→Infiltration/Aeration↓ 
Roots may also encounter greater mechanical resistance.
Therefore a soil can contain water but still have poor crop water availability because roots cannot
effectively explore it.

62. Soil Moisture and Salinity
This relationship is especially important in irrigated agriculture.
Salinity↑→Osmotic Stress↑→Effective Water Availability↓ 
Therefore irrigation management must consider both:
Water Quantity+Water Quality 

63. Soil Moisture and Water Quality
Poor-quality irrigation water may introduce:
- salts,
- sodium,

- chloride,
- bicarbonate,
- other ions.
Repeated application can alter soil properties.
Thus:
Irrigation Water Quality→Soil Salinity/Sodicity→Future Water Availability 

64. Soil Moisture and Rainfall
Rainfall does not instantly become available to plants.
The pathway is:
Rainfall→Interception→Infiltration→Soil Storage→Root Uptake 
But some rainfall can become:
Runoff+Evaporation+Deep Drainage 
Therefore:
Total Rainfall≠Soil Moisture Increase 

65. Soil Moisture and Rainfall Intensity
If rainfall intensity exceeds infiltration capacity:
Rainfall Intensity>Infiltration Capacity→Runoff Potential↑ 
This is particularly important on:
- compacted soils,
- crusted soils,
- bare soils,
- steep slopes.

66. Soil Moisture and Drainage
Good drainage removes excess water.

Excess Soil Water→Drainage→Aeration Recovery 
But excessive drainage can remove too much useful water.
Therefore:
Good Drainage≠Maximum Drainage 
The objective is:
Remove Excess Water+Retain Useful Water 

67. Soil Moisture and ET — Complete
Relationship
The relationship between the two previous Module 11 topics is:
Soil Moisture→Root Water Availability→Transpiration 
and:
ET=Evaporation+Transpiration 
Therefore:
Soil Moisture→Actual ET→Water Depletion→Irrigation Need 

68. Soil Moisture Monitoring for Irrigation
A practical monitoring system can combine:

Soil
- volumetric water content,
- matric potential,
- depth-wise moisture.

Crop
- wilting,
- leaf rolling,
- canopy temperature,
- growth.

Weather
- temperature,
- humidity,
- radiation,
- wind,
- rainfall.

ET
ET0,ETcET 0, ET c
Together:
Weather+Soil+Crop→Better Irrigation Decision 

69. Soil Moisture Sensors — Placement
Sensors should represent the root zone, not simply the soil surface.
Multiple depths can be useful:
10−20cm,20−40cm,40−60cm 
as illustrative monitoring depths, with actual depths selected according to crop and rooting pattern.
A single shallow sensor may give a misleading picture if roots extend much deeper.

70. Soil Moisture Thresholds
An irrigation system can use predefined thresholds.
For example:
If soil moisture<management threshold→Irrigate <    
The threshold should be based on:
- crop,
- soil,
- root zone,
- growth stage,
- atmospheric demand,

- irrigation system.
There is no single universal moisture percentage that means “irrigate now” for every soil and crop.

71. Example of Irrigation Decision
Suppose root-zone water storage is:
100mm100mm
and the management strategy permits depletion of:
40mm40mm
Then irrigation may be scheduled when approximately:
40mm 
- f usable depletion has accumulated, subject to the crop and irrigation strategy.
The irrigation amount is then determined from the desired replenishment level and system efficiency.

72. Soil Moisture and Crop Water Requirement
The relationship can be summarized:
Crop Water Requirement→ET 
while:
Soil Moisture→Water Supply 
Therefore:
Crop Water Demand↔Soil Water Supply 
If demand exceeds supply:
Water Stress 

73. Soil Moisture and Irrigation Requirement
The irrigation requirement depends on how much of the crop's water use can be supplied by existing
soil water and rainfall.
Conceptually:
Irrigation Need=Crop Demand−Available Natural/Stored Water 

with runoff, drainage, efficiency and other water-balance components considered.

74. Soil Moisture Management
Good soil moisture management aims to maintain the root zone in a suitable range.

Practices include:
- irrigation scheduling,
- drip irrigation,
- sprinkler irrigation where appropriate,
- mulching,
- residue retention,
- conservation tillage,
- cover crops,
- organic matter management,
- drainage,
- weed management,
- suitable planting density,
- rainwater harvesting,
- soil structure improvement.
The objective is:
Avoid Both Water Deficit and Waterlogging 

75. Poor Soil Moisture Management
Too little water
Drought→Stomatal Closure→Photosynthesis↓→Growth/Yield↓       Too much water
Waterlogging→O2↓→Root Respiration↓→Growth/Yield↓   O 2     Thus the target is a favorable soil-water range, not maximum moisture.

76. Soil Moisture vs Related Terms
Term
Meaning
Soil moisture
Water currently contained in soil
Soil water content
Quantitative expression of soil moisture
Field capacity
Water remaining after excess drainage
Permanent wilting point Reference lower limit of plant water availability

Soil-water potential
Energy state/availability of soil water
Infiltration
Entry of water into soil
Percolation
Downward movement through soil
Drainage
Removal of excess water
Evapotranspiration
Evaporation + transpiration
Irrigation
Artificial application of water

77. Most Important Formulas
Volumetric water content
θv=VwVt  V t   

Gravimetric water content
θg=MwMdry  M  dry    

Available water
AWC≈FC−PWP 

Total available water
TAW=(θFC−θPWP)Zr - )Z r  

Readily available water
RAW=p×TAW 

Air-filled porosity
AFP=n−θv 

Soil-water balance
ΔS=P+I+CR−R−D−ET 

Bulk density
ρb=Dry Soil MassBulk Volume  Bulk\ Volume   

Volumetric–gravimetric conversion
