78. Master Soil-Moisture Cycle
Rainfall/Irrigation   
↓
Infiltration  
↓
Soil-Water Storage  
↓
Root UptakeEvaporationDeep PercolationDrainage  c   \\
 \\  \\      
↓
Water Balance  
↓
Plant Water Status  
↓
Growth + Yield  

79. Master Irrigation Relationship
The three Module 11 concepts now connect directly:

Plant Water Requirement
How much water does the crop need?
↓

Evapotranspiration
How much water is being transferred to the atmosphere?
↓

Soil Moisture
How much water is stored and potentially available to roots?
↓

Irrigation
How much additional water should be supplied, and when?
Therefore:
Crop Demand↔Soil-Water Supply↔Irrigation     

80. Common Misconceptions

❌ “More soil moisture is always better.”
No.

Excess moisture can cause waterlogging and oxygen deficiency.

❌ “All soil water is available to plants.”
No.
Some water is gravitational and drains quickly; some is held too tightly for effective extraction.

❌ “Field capacity means the soil is completely full of water.”
No.
At field capacity, excess gravitational water has drained under defined conditions.

❌ “Soil moisture percentage tells everything about plant water availability.”
No.
Water potential, salinity, texture, root distribution and soil structure also matter.

❌ “Dry soil always means the crop needs irrigation.”
Not necessarily.
Irrigation should consider root-zone moisture, crop stage, weather, rainfall and soil-water status.

❌ “Waterlogging is caused only by too much rainfall.”
No.
It can result from:
- excessive irrigation,
- poor drainage,
- compacted soil,
- high water table,
- slow infiltration,
- flooding.

❌ “A moisture sensor near the surface represents the whole root zone.”
Not necessarily.
Moisture should ideally be monitored at representative root-zone depths.

81. Exam-Ready Definition
Soil moisture is the water contained within the pore spaces of soil and serves as the
principal water reservoir for crop roots. Its availability depends not only on the
quantity of water present but also on soil texture, structure, pore size, water potential,
salinity, root distribution and aeration. Important reference points include field
capacity and permanent wilting point, with approximate available water expressed as
AWC=FC−PWPAWC=FC-PWP. Soil moisture monitoring is essential for efficient
irrigation scheduling and prevention of both drought stress and waterlogging.

82. Final One-Line Memory
Soil Moisture = Water stored in the soil that forms the crop’s root-zone water supply.
   
The most important chain to remember is:
Rainfall/Irrigation→Infiltration→Soil-Water Storage→Root Uptake→Plant→Transpiration→ET d           

And for irrigation:
Soil Moisture→Water Depletion→Irrigation Timing→Appropriate Water Application      
Core principle:
Good irrigation management does not aim for maximum soil moisture; it aims for an appropriate rootzone water–air balance.   

## Module 11 — Water & Irrigation
## Management
5. Permanent Wilting Point (PWP)
## 1. Introduction
Permanent Wilting Point (PWP) is a fundamental soil-water concept that represents the lower
reference limit of plant-available water.
As a soil dries, plants initially have plenty of water available. As water is continuously removed
through evapotranspiration, the remaining water is held increasingly tightly by soil particles.
Eventually, the plant can no longer extract enough water to maintain its water status and remains wilted
even when placed under the standard recovery conditions used to define PWP.
The overall sequence is:
Saturation→Field Capacity→Water Depletion→Permanent Wilting Point 
    
genui "learning viz":
 "type id":"SOIL FIELD CAPACITY AND WILTING POINT","locale override":"en-IN"  

## 2. Definition
Permanent wilting point is the soil-water condition at which a plant remains
permanently wilted and cannot regain its normal water status under the specified
reference conditions.
It is commonly represented as:
PWP 

- r:
θPWP  
when expressed as soil-water content.

3. Why Is It Called "Permanent"?
The word permanent does not mean that every plant literally dies immediately at this point.
It means that under the standardized/reference conditions used for the concept, the plant does not
recover from wilting.
Therefore:
PWP=reference condition of persistent plant wilting   
rather than simply:
"plant has died" 

4. Position of PWP in Soil-Water Storage
Think of soil as a water reservoir:
WET
│
│

## Saturation
│

↓ excess water drains

│
│

## Field Capacity
│

↓ crop extracts water

│
│

## Plant-Available Water
│

↓ increasing difficulty of extraction

│
│

## Permanent Wilting Point
│
DRY

Thus:
FC>PWP 

when both are expressed on the same water-content basis.

5. Relationship Between FC and PWP
The two concepts are directly connected.

Field Capacity
Upper reference point:
FC 

Permanent Wilting Point
Lower reference point:
PWP 
Therefore:
Available Water≈FC−PWP 
This is one of the most important relationships in soil-water management.

6. Plant-Available Water
The approximate plant-available water capacity is:
AWC=θFC−θPWP -   
where:
- AWCAWC = available water capacity,
- θFC  = volumetric water content at field capacity,
- θPWP  = volumetric water content at permanent wilting point.

## 7. Example
Suppose:
θFC=0.30 =0.30
and:
θPWP=0.15 =0.15
Then:

AWC=0.30−0.15AWC=0.30-0.15 AWC=0.15m3/m3 
So approximately 15% of soil volume represents the difference between the two reference water
contents.

8. Total Available Water in the Root Zone
For a crop root zone:
TAW=(θFC−θPWP)Zr - )Z r  
where:
- TAWTAW = total available water,
- ZrZ r = effective root-zone depth.

Example
Suppose:
θFC=0.32 =0.32 θPWP=0.16 =0.16
and:
Zr=0.75mZ r=0.75m
Then:
TAW=(0.32−0.16)×0.75TAW=(0.32-0.16) 0.75 =0.12m=0.12m
Therefore:
TAW=120mm 
approximately.

9. What Happens as Soil Approaches PWP?
As soil dries:
Soil Moisture↓ 
↓
Soil Water Potential becomes More Negative 
↓
Water becomes harder for roots to extract 

↓
Plant Water Stress↑ 
↓
Wilting 
↓
PWP 

10. Why Can't Plants Extract All Soil Water?
Water is held by the soil through forces such as:
- adhesion,
- cohesion,
- capillary forces,
- adsorption onto soil particles.
As soil becomes drier, water films become thinner and are held more strongly.
Therefore:
Soil Drying→Water Holding Force↑→Root Extraction Difficulty↑ 

11. Soil-Water Potential at PWP
A traditional reference value for PWP is approximately:
−1500 kPa 
- r:
−1.5 MPa 
This is a conventional reference, not a universal physiological threshold for every plant and soil.
The actual water status at which plants wilt depends on:
- crop species,
- genotype,
- atmospheric conditions,
- root system,
- soil,

- duration of stress,
- plant acclimation.
Therefore:
PWP≠an absolute universal biological limit   

12. Why −1500 kPa Should Be Treated as a
Reference
The traditional −1500 kPa value is useful for comparing soils and estimating available water.
However, plants differ greatly in their ability to extract water.
Therefore:
Reference PWP≠Exact wilting threshold of every crop   
This distinction becomes especially important in advanced soil-water studies.

13. PWP and Soil Texture
Soil texture strongly influences PWP.

Sandy Soil
Water is held relatively weakly in many larger pores.
As the soil dries:
Water content decreases rapidly 
PWP is generally reached at a relatively low water content.

Clay Soil
Clay has many small pores and a large surface area.
It can retain considerable water even when very dry.
However:
Much of that water is strongly held   
Therefore clay can have:

- high water content at PWP,
- but relatively low accessibility of that water.

Loam
Loam commonly has an intermediate behavior.
The actual PWP depends on the specific soil characteristics.

14. Texture Comparison
Soil Typical PWP water content
Water behavior
Sandy Relatively low
Less water retained at very dry condition
Loamy Intermediate
Moderate retention
Clayey Relatively high
More strongly retained water
These are general tendencies rather than universal numerical values.

15. Why Clay Can Have More Water but Less
Available Water
This is an important concept.
Imagine:

Sandy soil
FC = relatively low
PWP = very low

Clay soil
FC = relatively high
PWP = also relatively high
Available water depends on the difference:
AWC=FC−PWP 
Therefore:
High FC≠High Available Water 
The difference between FC and PWP is what matters.

16. PWP and Soil-Water Retention Curve
A soil-water retention curve relates:
Water Content↔Soil−Water Potential 
As soil dries:
Water Content↓ 
while:
Water Potential becomes more Negative 
PWP represents a lower reference point on this relationship.

17. PWP and Matric Potential
In unsaturated soil, matric forces are particularly important.
The matric component of water potential becomes increasingly negative as soil dries.
Conceptually:
Drying→Matric Potential More Negative→Root Water Extraction Difficulty↑ 

18. PWP and Root Water Uptake
Water movement into roots depends on a water-potential gradient.
A simplified pathway is:
Soil→Root→Xylem→Leaf→Atmosphere 
As the soil becomes very dry, the gradient available for root uptake becomes less favorable.
Eventually:
Root Water Uptake<Plant Water Loss 
and plant water stress becomes severe.

19. PWP and Transpiration
Recall:

ET=E+T 
As soil dries:
Soil water↓  ↓
Root water uptake↓  ↓
Stomatal closure↑  ↓
Transpiration↓  ↓
Photosynthesis↓  Thus PWP represents a very severe soil-water condition, well beyond the point at which many crops
begin experiencing water stress.

20. PWP Does Not Mean Plants Are Stress-Free
Above It
This is extremely important.
A crop can experience water stress before reaching PWP.
Therefore:
Plant Stress Begins Before PWP   
PWP is better viewed as a lower reference boundary, not as the point at which drought suddenly
begins.

21. Readily Available Water
Because crops may become stressed before PWP, irrigation scheduling usually does not wait until PWP.
First calculate:
TAW=(FC−PWP)Zr 
Then a fraction may be considered readily available:
RAW=p×TAW 

where pp is the allowable depletion fraction.
Therefore:
FC→TAW→RAW→Irrigation Timing 

22. Why Irrigation Is Normally Scheduled
Before PWP
Suppose:
FC=30%FC=30\%
and:
PWP=15%PWP=15\%
Theoretically:
AWC=15%AWC=15\%
But allowing the soil to reach 15% before irrigating may cause significant crop stress.
Instead, irrigation can be scheduled when a suitable portion of available water has been depleted.
Conceptually:
FC→Allowed Depletion→Irrigation   Irrigation  
rather than:
FC→PWP→IrrigationFC PWP Irrigation

23. PWP and Drought
As drought develops:
Rainfall↓+ET demand↑ 
can lead to:
Soil Moisture↓ 
↓
Water Potential↓ 
↓
Plant Stress↑ 

↓
Wilting 
If severe and persistent:
PWP Condition 
can be approached.

24. PWP and Plant Water Stress
Water stress can cause:
- reduced cell expansion,
- leaf rolling,
- stomatal closure,
- reduced photosynthesis,
- reduced nutrient transport,
- reduced growth,
- flower abortion,
- reduced fruit/grain/pod formation,
- premature senescence,
- yield loss.
Therefore:
Soil Drying→Plant Water Stress→Growth/Yield Loss 

25. PWP and Crop Growth Stage
The impact of low soil moisture depends strongly on growth stage.
Sensitive stages may include:
- flowering,
- pollination,
- fertilization,
- fruit set,

- grain formation,
- grain filling.
Thus the same soil moisture condition can have different consequences at different crop stages.
Stress Effect=f(Soil Moisture,Duration,Crop,Stage,Atmospheric Demand) 

26. PWP and Root Depth
Two crops growing in the same soil can have different practical access to water because their root
systems differ.
A deeper-rooted crop may access:
Deeper Soil Water 
provided those layers are suitable for root growth.
Therefore:
PWP of Soil≠Complete Description of Crop Water Stress 
Root distribution must also be considered.

27. PWP and Salinity
Salinity can make water more difficult for plants to extract.
So even when soil moisture content is relatively high:
Salinity↑→Osmotic Stress↑→Effective Water Availability↓ 
Therefore saline soils demonstrate why:
Water Content≠Complete Water Availability 

28. PWP and Soil Moisture
These terms should not be confused.

Soil moisture
The amount of water currently present.

PWP
A reference condition representing severe depletion where plants remain wilted under standardized
conditions.
Therefore:
PWP is a particular soil-water state, not a synonym for soil moisture.   

29. PWP vs Field Capacity
Feature

Field Capacity
Permanent Wilting Point
Position
Upper reference
Lower reference
Soil condition
Relatively wet
Very dry
Water availability
Relatively high
Very low
Water potential
Less negative
Much more negative
Plant condition
Generally favorable
Persistent wilting
Irrigation role
Starting/reference point
Lower boundary
Approximate traditional potential  −33 kPa in many textbook treatments  −1500 kPa
The numerical potential values are reference conventions, not universal constants.

30. PWP vs Saturation
Feature
Soil water
Air-filled pores
Oxygen
availability
Main problem

Saturation
Very high
Very low

PWP

Very low
High
Generally high in terms of air-filled pores, though roots may be
Potentially low
stressed by dryness
Waterlogging Severe water deficit
Oxygen
Root problem
Insufficient water uptake
shortage
Thus the crop can be damaged at both extremes.
Too Wet↔Too Dry 

31. PWP vs Drought Stress
PWP is not identical to drought.

Drought
A period of water deficit relative to plant demand.

PWP
A standardized/reference soil-water condition associated with persistent plant wilting.
Therefore:
Drought≠PWP 
A crop can experience serious drought stress well before reaching PWP.

32. PWP and Irrigation Management
The irrigation-management framework is:
FC−PWP→Available Water 
then:
Available Water→Allowable Depletion 
then:
Allowable Depletion→Irrigation Timing 
Therefore PWP is essential for estimating the water-storage range of a root zone.

33. Measuring PWP
PWP can be estimated using:

Laboratory approaches
Soil samples can be equilibrated at a specified water potential and their water content measured.

Soil-water retention methods
A retention curve can be developed:
Ψm↔θ 
and water content at a selected reference potential can be estimated.

Plant-based methods
Historical definitions are associated with plant wilting experiments.
Modern soil-water studies often use controlled soil-water potential measurements rather than assuming
- ne universal value.

34. Gravimetric and Volumetric PWP
PWP can be expressed as:

Gravimetric
θg=MwaterMdry soil   M  dry\ soil    

Volumetric
θv=VwaterVsoil   V  soil    
Conversion:
θv=θgρbρw    w   
Always check the measurement basis before comparing values.

35. PWP and Soil Bulk Density
Bulk density affects pore structure and therefore soil-water relationships.
ρb=Dry Soil MassBulk Volume  Bulk\ Volume   
Changes in bulk density can change:
- total pore space,
- pore-size distribution,
- water retention,
- root penetration.
Therefore:
Compaction→Pore Structure Change→Soil Water Behavior Change 

36. PWP and Organic Matter
Organic matter can influence:
- aggregation,
- pore structure,
- water retention,
- water movement.
Therefore organic matter management can influence the soil-water retention characteristics of a soil.

But:
More Organic Matter≠Automatically More Plant Available Water 

37. PWP and Soil Texture — Core Concept
The most important relationship is:
Texture→Pore Size Distribution→Water Retention→FC/PWP→Available Water 

38. Example Comparing Two Soils
Suppose:

Soil A
FC=20%FC=20\% PWP=8%PWP=8\%
Then:
AWC=12%AWC=12\%

Soil B
FC=40%FC=40\% PWP=25%PWP=25\%
Then:
AWC=15%AWC=15\%
Although Soil B has much more water at both FC and PWP, its plant-available water is only moderately
greater because PWP is also high.
This illustrates:
Available Water Depends on the Difference, Not FC Alone   

39. PWP and Root-Zone Water Balance
The complete root-zone water system can be represented as:
P+I+CR→Root-Zone Storage   
while:
ET+R+D→Water Losses   

and:
FC↔PWP 
defines an important reference range for that storage.
The general balance is:
ΔS=P+I+CR−R−D−ET 

40. Practical Management to Avoid Approaching
PWP
Farmers can reduce severe soil-water depletion through:
- timely irrigation,
- drip irrigation,
- appropriate irrigation scheduling,
- mulching,
- residue retention,
- conservation tillage,
- rainwater harvesting,
- suitable crop selection,
- drought-tolerant varieties,
- appropriate plant population,
- weed management,
- improved soil structure,
- organic matter management.
The objective is:
Keep Root Zone Above Severe Water Stress 

41. Common Misconceptions

❌ "PWP means the plant has already died."
No.

It is a reference condition of persistent wilting under specified conditions.

❌ "PWP is exactly −1500 kPa for every soil and crop."
No.
−1500 kPa is a traditional reference value, not a universal biological constant.

❌ "Plants have no stress until PWP."
No.
Plants can experience substantial stress well before PWP.

❌ "All water above PWP is equally available."
No.
Water becomes progressively harder to extract as soil dries.

❌ "Higher PWP is always bad."
Not necessarily.
A high PWP water content often occurs in fine-textured soils that also have high FC. What matters for
available water is:
## Fc−Pwpfc-Pwp
❌ "PWP is the same as drought."
No.
Drought is a water-deficit condition; PWP is a soil-water reference state.

❌ "PWP alone determines irrigation timing."
No.
Irrigation timing also depends on:
- FC,
- available water,
- allowable depletion,
- root depth,
- crop,
- growth stage,

- ET,
- rainfall,
- soil variability.

42. The Three Most Important Soil-Water
Levels
Condition
Meaning
Saturation
Soil pores are almost completely water-filled
Field Capacity
Excess gravitational water has drained
Permanent Wilting Point Plant cannot recover from wilting under reference conditions
The useful range is approximately:
FC→PWP 

43. The Complete Soil-Water Spectrum
Saturation   
↓

↓

↓

↓

↓

↓

↓
Water held extremely strongly

44. Master Formula Set
Available water
AWC=θFC−θPWP -   

Root-zone available water
TAW=(θFC−θPWP)Zr - )Z r  

Readily available water
RAW=p×TAW 

Root-zone deficit relative to FC
D=(θFC−θcurrent)Zr - )Z r  

Volumetric water content
θv=VwVt  V t   

Gravimetric water content
θg=MwMdry  M  dry    

Conversion
θv=θgρbρw    w   

45. Exam-Ready Definition
Permanent wilting point (PWP) is the soil-water condition at which a plant remains
wilted and cannot recover its normal water status under specified reference
conditions. It represents an important lower reference limit of plant-available soil
water. PWP is influenced by soil texture, structure, water-retention characteristics and
plant properties. Together with field capacity, it is used to estimate available water
capacity as AWC=θFC−θPWPAWC= -  and total root-zone
available water as TAW=(θFC−θPWP)ZrTAW=( - )Z r.

46. Final One-Line Memory
Permanent Wilting Point = The lower reference soil-water condition at which a plant remains wilted be
cause the remaining soil water is too difficult to extract.   

Remember these three:
Saturation = Too Wet   
Field Capacity = After Excess Water Drains    Permanent Wilting Point = Too Dry for Effective Recovery   
And the most important relationship in Module 11 so far:
Available Water≈Field Capacity−Permanent Wilting Point 
 -   
FC→Water Depletion→Irrigation Decision→PWP     
In practical irrigation management, you generally want to irrigate before the crop approaches PWP,
because significant water stress can occur well before that reference point.

## Module 11 — Water & Irrigation
## Management
6. Critical Moisture Stages
## 1. Introduction
Critical moisture stages are the specific stages of crop growth during which adequate soil moisture is
particularly important for maintaining normal growth, development, reproductive success, and yield.
A crop does not have the same sensitivity to water shortage throughout its entire life cycle.
For example, a short period of drought during early vegetative growth may sometimes be partly
compensated for later, whereas water stress during flowering, pollination, fruit set, or grain filling
can cause much larger and sometimes irreversible yield losses.
The basic concept is:
Crop Stage+Water Stress→Stage-specific Effect on Yield  +     

## 2. Definition
Critical moisture stages are crop-growth stages at which adequate soil moisture is
especially important because water stress during those stages can cause
disproportionately large effects on reproductive development, yield formation, quality,
- r crop establishment.
They are also commonly called:

- critical growth stages,
- critical irrigation stages,
- critical water-requiring stages,
- moisture-sensitive stages.
The exact stages differ among:
- crops,
- varieties,
- climates,
- soils,
- production systems.
Therefore:
Critical Stage≠Universal Stage for Every Crop   

3. Why Are They Important?
Water stress can occur at any time, but its consequences differ by growth stage.
A simplified relationship is:
Yield Loss=f(Stress Intensity,Duration,Timing,Crop,Genotype) , , , , )  
Two identical droughts can therefore produce different yield losses if they occur at different stages.

4. Crop Water Demand Changes With Growth
Crop water use generally changes as the crop develops.
A simplified pattern is:
Early Growth→Vegetative Expansion→Peak Canopy→Reproductive Development→Maturity       
Water demand is often relatively low during early establishment, rises as canopy and atmospheric
demand increase, and may decline toward maturity.
However, water demand and water sensitivity are not exactly the same thing.
A stage may have moderate water use but still be highly sensitive to water stress.

5. Critical Moisture Stage vs Maximum Water
Requirement
These concepts must be distinguished.

Maximum water requirement
The stage when crop water consumption/ET may be relatively high.

Critical moisture stage
The stage when water stress can cause particularly serious consequences for yield or quality.
Therefore:
Highest ET Stage≠Always Most Critical Stage   
Some reproductive stages are highly sensitive even if their water use is not the absolute maximum.

6. The Main Critical Stages
Across many crops, important moisture-sensitive periods commonly include:
1. germination and establishment,
2. early seedling establishment,
3. rapid vegetative growth,
4. flowering,
5. pollination,
6. fruit set,
7. pod formation,
8. grain formation,
9. grain filling,
10.tuber/root/bulb development,
11.early reproductive development.
The exact critical stages are crop-specific.

7. Stage 1 — Germination
Adequate moisture is essential for:
Seed Imbibition   
The sequence is:
Dry Seed→Water Uptake→Metabolic Activation→Radicle Emergence     If soil moisture is insufficient:
Imbibition↓→Germination↓     
Consequences include:
- delayed emergence,
- poor stand,
- uneven crop,
- reduced plant population.

8. Stage 2 — Seedling Establishment
After germination, the young plant has a relatively small root system.
Water stress can cause:
- wilting,
- poor root development,
- reduced leaf expansion,
- mortality,
- poor establishment.
Therefore:
Seedling Stage→Adequate Moisture Important for Establishment 
  
A poor stand can have effects throughout the season.

9. Stage 3 — Vegetative Growth
During vegetative growth, plants develop:
- roots,
- leaves,
- stems,
- branches,
- tillers.
Water stress can reduce:
Leaf Area  Root/Shoot Growth 
Canopy Development 
and therefore reduce photosynthetic capacity.

10. Soil Moisture → Leaf Area
Under water stress:
Soil Moisture↓→Cell Expansion↓→Leaf Expansion↓ 
Consequently:
Leaf Area↓→Light Interception↓→Photosynthesis Potential↓ 

11. Stage 4 — Flower Initiation
Water stress around reproductive transition can affect:
- reproductive development,
- number of reproductive structures,
- flowering timing,
- plant architecture.
Severe stress may disrupt normal development.
However, the exact response is crop- and genotype-dependent.

12. Stage 5 — Flowering
Flowering is one of the most important moisture-sensitive stages for many crops.
Water stress can cause:
- reduced flower production,
- flower abortion,
- reduced pollen viability,
- reduced stigma receptivity,
- poor pollination,
- reduced fertilization,
- reduced fruit/grain/pod set.
The pathway is:
Water Stress→Flower/Pollen/Stigma Problems→Pollination/Fertilization Problems→Yield Loss   Water\ Stress  Flower/Pollen/Stigma\ Problems  Pollination/Fertilization\
Problems  Yield\ Loss  

13. Stage 6 — Pollination
Successful pollination requires appropriate:
- pollen viability,
- pollen release,
- pollen transfer,
- stigma receptivity,
- reproductive timing.
Water stress can disrupt these processes directly or indirectly.
Therefore:
Adequate Moisture→Better Reproductive Conditions→Pollination Success 

14. Stage 7 — Fertilization
After pollination:

Pollen Germination→Pollen Tube→Ovule→Fertilization 
Water stress can interfere with reproductive processes and increase abortion.
Consequently:
Fertilization Success↓→Seed/Fruit Set↓ 

15. Stage 8 — Fruit Set
For fruit crops:
Flower→Pollination→Fertilization→Fruit Set 
Water stress around flowering and fruit set can increase:
- flower drop,
- fruit abortion,
- poor fruit set,
- reduced number of fruits.
Therefore fruit set is a major moisture-sensitive period in many fruit and vegetable crops.

16. Stage 9 — Pod Formation
In pulse crops:
Flowering→Pod Set→Pod Development→Seed Filling 
Water stress can cause:
- flower abortion,
- pod abortion,
- fewer pods,
- reduced seed number.
Thus flowering and pod formation are commonly important moisture-sensitive stages in pulses.

17. Stage 10 — Grain Formation
In cereals:
Flowering→Fertilization→Grain Set→Grain Development 
Water stress can reduce the number of grains that successfully develop.
Therefore:
Grain Number 
can be strongly affected by moisture availability around reproductive stages.

18. Stage 11 — Grain Filling
Grain filling is another highly important moisture-sensitive period for many cereal crops.
During grain filling:
Photosynthetic Assimilates+Remobilized Reserves→Developing Grain 
Water stress can cause:
- reduced photosynthesis,
- premature senescence,
- shorter grain-filling duration,
- reduced assimilate supply,
- lower grain weight.
Therefore:
Water Stress→Grain Filling Rate/Duration↓→Grain Weight↓ 

19. Yield Formation and Critical Moisture
A simplified cereal yield relationship is:
Yield≈Plants/Area×Grains/Plant×Grain Weight 
Different moisture stresses affect different yield components.

Early stress
May reduce:
Plants/AreaPlants/Area

Flowering stress
May reduce:
Grains/PlantGrains/Plant

Grain-filling stress
May reduce:
Grain WeightGrain\ Weight
This is a very useful way to understand critical moisture stages.

20. Stage-specific Yield Response
A conceptual relationship is:
Water Stress×Stage Sensitivity→Yield Loss 
For example:
Stage
Germination
Seedling
Vegetative
Flower initiation
Flowering
Fruit/pod/grain
set
Grain filling
Maturity

Possible major effect
Poor emergence
Poor establishment
Reduced canopy
Reproductive development
Flower abortion/pollination problems
Reduced reproductive units
Reduced grain weight
Usually lower sensitivity to severe stress than earlier critical reproductive stages,
but crop-specific

21. Critical Moisture Stages Are Crop-Specific
There is no single universal list.
For example:

Rice
Important moisture-sensitive periods commonly include:
- establishment,
- tillering/vegetative development,
- panicle initiation,
- flowering/anthesis,
- grain filling.

Wheat
Important periods commonly include:
- crown-root establishment,
- tillering,
- jointing,
- booting,
- flowering/anthesis,
- grain filling.

Maize
Important periods commonly include:
- establishment,
- rapid vegetative growth,
- tasseling,
- silking,
- pollination,
- early grain development.

Pulses
Important periods commonly include:
- establishment,
- flowering,
- pod formation,
- seed filling.

These are broad examples; exact irrigation recommendations depend on crop, variety, climate, soil and
production system.

22. Why Flowering Is Often Highly Critical
Flowering involves several linked processes:
Flowering→Pollen Release→Pollination→Fertilization→Reproductive Set 
Water stress can affect several of these simultaneously.
Therefore a short stress event at flowering can sometimes produce a large yield effect.

23. Why Grain Filling Is Critical
During grain filling:
Source→Assimilates→Sink 
The source includes:
- leaves,
- stems,
- stored carbohydrates.
The sink is:
- developing grain.
Water stress reduces photosynthetic activity and may accelerate senescence.
Thus:
Water Stress→Source Activity↓→Assimilate Supply↓→Grain Weight↓ 

24. Critical Moisture and Root Zone
Critical moisture refers to the water condition experienced by the active root zone, not simply surface
soil.
The relevant question is:
Is sufficient water available in the soil volume being explored by active roots?

Thus:
Root Zone Moisture>Surface Moisture Alone 
for many irrigation decisions.

25. Soil Moisture Depletion
As plants consume water:
ET→Soil Water Depletion 
Starting from field capacity:
FC→depletion→PWPFC  PWP
But irrigation should generally be scheduled before severe stress develops.
A common concept is:
RAW=p×TAW 
where:
- RAWRAW = readily available water,
- TAWTAW = total available water,
- pp = allowable depletion fraction.

26. Critical Moisture and Irrigation Scheduling
The objective is to prevent soil moisture from falling too far during sensitive stages.
Conceptually:
Monitor Soil Moisture→Estimate Depletion→Identify Crop Stage→Irrigate When Required      
During highly sensitive stages, the allowable depletion may be managed more conservatively
depending on crop and irrigation strategy.

27. Critical Moisture ≠ Continuous Flooding
Maintaining adequate moisture does not mean keeping soil saturated.
Remember:

Saturation→O2↓→Root Stress 
The goal is:
Adequate Moisture+Adequate Aeration 

28. Critical Moisture and Field Capacity
Field capacity is an upper reference point:
FC 
The crop then consumes water:
FC→depletionFC As depletion increases:
Water stress risk↑  Therefore:
FC→Available Water→Allowable Depletion→Irrigation 
   

29. Critical Moisture and Permanent Wilting
Point
PWP is a lower reference point:
PWP 
However:
Do not wait until PWP to irrigate   
because crop stress generally begins before PWP.
Thus:
FC→Allowable Depletion→Irrigation→PWP 
  PWP  
not:
FC→PWP→IrrigationFC PWP Irrigation

30. Critical Moisture and Evapotranspiration
Recall:
ET=E+T 
As soil moisture declines:
Soil Water Availability↓→Transpiration Limitation↑ 
Eventually:
Actual ET<Potential ET 
because the crop cannot obtain enough water to meet atmospheric demand.

31. Soil Moisture Stress and Stomata
Water stress causes plants to regulate stomatal opening.
Soil Moisture↓→Root/Plant Water Status↓→Stomatal Closure 
Then:
CO2 Entry↓CO 2\ Entry ↓
Photosynthesis↓Photosynthesis This is one of the major physiological pathways through which drought affects yield.

32. Critical Moisture and Photosynthesis
The chain is:
Soil Moisture↓→Stomatal Conductance↓→CO2 Uptake↓→Photosynthesis↓ 
Severe stress can additionally cause:
- chlorophyll damage,
- oxidative stress,
- premature senescence,

- reduced leaf area.

33. Critical Moisture and Root Growth
Moderate water limitation can sometimes stimulate greater relative root allocation, but severe drought
generally restricts root growth and function.
Therefore:
Water Stress→Root Architecture/Function Changes 
Crop response depends strongly on:
- genotype,
- soil,
- stress intensity,
- stress duration,
- developmental stage.

34. Critical Moisture and Nutrient Uptake
Water supports nutrient movement and root activity.
Under drought:
Soil Moisture↓→Root Activity↓+Nutrient Movement↓→Nutrient Uptake↓ 
Thus apparent nutrient deficiency can sometimes be associated with water stress even when nutrients
are present in soil.

35. Critical Moisture and Heat Stress
Water stress becomes particularly dangerous under high temperature.
Normally:
Transpiration→Evaporative CoolingTranspiration  Evaporative\ Cooling
But drought causes:

Soil Moisture↓→Stomatal Closure→Transpiration↓→Cooling↓Soil\ Moisture  Stomatal\ Closure  Transpiration  Cooling Therefore:
Drought+Heat→Compounded Stress 

36. Critical Moisture and Humidity/VPD
High atmospheric demand can increase water loss.
Vapor pressure deficit is:
VPD=es−ea 
High VPD generally increases atmospheric demand for transpiration.
Therefore the same soil moisture content may produce different crop stress under different atmospheric
conditions.
Stress=f(Soil Moisture+VPD+Temperature+Stage) 

37. Critical Moisture and Crop Yield
A useful conceptual model is:
Yield=f(G,E,G×E,Water,Management) 
Water stress reduces yield through several pathways:
Water Stress→Growth↓+Reproduction↓+Filling↓→Yield↓ 

38. Early vs Late Moisture Stress
Early stress
Can reduce:
- stand,
- root development,
- leaf area,
- tillers/branches.

Reproductive stress
Can reduce:
- flowers,
- pollination,
- fertilization,
- fruit/pod/grain number.

Filling-stage stress
Can reduce:
- seed/grain/fruit size,
- filling duration,
- final weight.
Thus:
Timing Determines Which Yield Component Is Damaged   

39. Critical Moisture and Yield Components
For many crops:
Yield≈Population×Reproductive Units/Plant×Weight/Unit 
Therefore:
Stress timing
Potentially affected component
Establishment
Plant population
Vegetative
Canopy/biomass
Flowering
Reproductive-unit number
Fruit/pod/grain set Number
Filling
Unit weight
Maturity
Quality/completion, crop-specific

40. Critical Moisture vs Critical Period
These terms are related but not identical.

Critical moisture stage
A particular developmental stage highly sensitive to water shortage.

Critical period
A broader time interval during which stress has particularly strong consequences.
Therefore:
Stage=specific developmental point   
Period=time interval   

41. Critical Moisture vs Crop Water
Requirement
Crop water requirement
Total water needed to support crop evapotranspiration under specified conditions.

Critical moisture stage
Stage when water availability is especially important for yield.
Therefore:
Water Requirement≠Critical Stage 

42. Critical Moisture vs Irrigation Schedule
Critical stages
Tell us when the crop is particularly sensitive.

Irrigation schedule
Determines when and how much water to apply.
Therefore:
Critical Stages+Soil Moisture+ET+Weather→Irrigation Schedule 

43. Practical Identification of Critical Moisture
Stages
A farmer or agronomist can combine:

Crop development
- emergence,
- tillering,
- branching,
- flowering,
- fruit set,
- pod formation,
- grain filling.

Soil moisture
- sensor readings,
- gravimetric sampling,
- volumetric water content,
- soil-water potential.

Weather
- rainfall,
- temperature,
- humidity,
- VPD,
- ET.

Crop indicators
- wilting,
- leaf rolling,
- canopy temperature,
- growth rate,
- flowering/fruit set.

44. Soil-Moisture-Based Decision
A simplified decision framework:
Crop Stage   

↓
Root-Zone Moisture   
↓
Water Depletion   
↓
Stage Sensitivity   
↓
Irrigation Decision   
This is much better than irrigating simply according to a fixed calendar interval.

45. Critical Moisture Stage Table
Growth stage
Why moisture matters
Possible effect of stress
Germination
Imbibition
Poor/delayed germination
Seedling
Establishment
Mortality, weak stand
Vegetative
Leaf/root/stem growth
Reduced biomass/canopy
Flower initiation Reproductive transition
Altered reproductive development
Flowering
Reproductive processes
Flower abortion
Pollination
Pollen/stigma function
Poor pollination
Fertilization
Successful reproduction
Reduced set
Fruit set
Retention of reproductive organs Fruit abortion
Pod formation Pod establishment
Fewer pods
Grain set
Grain establishment
Fewer grains
Grain filling
Assimilate accumulation
Lower grain weight
Maturity
Completion/drying
Quality/harvest effects

46. Crop-Specific Examples
Rice
Important moisture-sensitive periods commonly include:
Panicle Initiation→Flowering 
and grain development/filling.
Water management is especially important because rice production systems differ substantially, and
continuous flooding is not universally required for every production system.

Wheat
Important stages commonly include:
Crown Root→Tillering→Jointing→Booting→Flowering→Grain Filling 
Water stress around reproductive development can strongly affect yield components.

Maize
A particularly important period is around:
Tasseling→Silking→Pollination→Early Grain Development 
Water stress here can cause poor synchronization between pollen shedding and silk receptivity and
reduce kernel set.

Pulses
Commonly sensitive stages include:
Flowering→Pod Formation→Seed Filling 
Water stress can increase flower and pod abortion.

Fruit Crops
Important periods may include:
Flowering→Fruit Set→Fruit Enlargement 
Water stress can affect:
- fruit set,
- fruit growth,
- fruit size,
- quality.

47. Why Critical Stages Differ Between Crops
Different crops have different:
- root systems,
- growth cycles,
- reproductive biology,
- canopy development,
- yield components,
- drought sensitivity.
For example:
Cereal→Grain Number + Grain Weight   
while:
Pulse→Pod Number + Seed Number + Seed Weight   
and:
Fruit Crop→Fruit Set + Fruit Number + Fruit Size   
Therefore their critical moisture stages differ.

48. Drought Escape and Critical Stages
Some varieties escape drought by completing sensitive development before severe drought occurs.
For example:
Early Maturity→Reproductive Stage Completed→Drought Avoidance/Escape 
This is one reason maturity duration matters when selecting varieties for water-limited environments.

49. Critical Moisture and Drought-Resistant
Varieties
Drought-adapted varieties may maintain performance better during critical stages through:

- deeper roots,
- efficient water use,
- osmotic adjustment,
- stomatal regulation,
- reproductive resilience,
- early maturity.
But:
Drought-resistant≠Drought-proof   

50. Irrigation Strategy During Critical Stages
The general principle is:
Prioritize Adequate Root-Zone Moisture During Sensitive Stages   
This does not necessarily mean increasing total seasonal irrigation.
Efficient management may involve:
- prioritizing irrigation at sensitive stages,
- reducing unnecessary irrigation during less sensitive periods,
- monitoring soil moisture,
- accounting for rainfall,
- considering ET,
- avoiding excessive irrigation.
Thus:
Right Water+Right Amount+Right Time  +   +
   

51. Critical Moisture and Water-Use Efficiency
Water-use efficiency can be expressed conceptually as:
WUE=Biomass or YieldWater Used  Water\ Used   
Efficient irrigation does not mean eliminating water stress at every moment regardless of cost.
It means using water strategically while protecting important yield-forming stages.

52. Critical Moisture and Deficit Irrigation
Deficit irrigation intentionally supplies less than full crop water requirement during selected periods.
The principle is:
Full Water Supply→Less Sensitive Stages 
and:
Priority Water→Critical Stages 
This can be useful in water-limited environments, but the strategy must be crop- and stage-specific.

53. Critical Moisture and Root-Zone Storage
The root-zone water reservoir can be represented by:
TAW=(θFC−θPWP)Zr - )Z r  
The crop gradually depletes this reservoir.
Therefore:
TAW→Depletion→Critical Threshold→Irrigation 

54. Critical Moisture and Field Capacity
A simplified example:
Suppose:
FC=30%FC=30\%
and:
PWP=15%PWP=15\%
Then:
AWC=15%AWC=15\%
If the crop is entering a highly moisture-sensitive reproductive stage, the irrigation strategy may avoid
allowing excessive depletion of this available-water reservoir.
The exact allowable depletion must be determined from the crop, soil, climate and irrigation method.

55. Critical Moisture and Waterlogging
Critical moisture does not only mean preventing drought.
Some crops are sensitive to excess moisture during critical stages.
Excess Moisture→O2↓→Root Stress→Growth/Reproduction Problems 
Therefore:
Critical Moisture=Avoid Both Severe Deficit and Harmful Excess 

56. Critical Moisture Management Practices
Before the critical stage
- ensure adequate root-zone moisture,
- monitor soil water,
- control weeds,
- maintain appropriate soil structure.

During the critical stage
- monitor moisture frequently,
- account for rainfall,
- irrigate when required,
- avoid both drought and waterlogging.

After the critical stage
- adjust irrigation according to crop demand and remaining yield formation.

57. Common Mistakes

❌ "Every crop needs irrigation at the same stages."
No.
Critical stages are crop-specific.

❌ "Flowering is the only critical stage."
No.

Germination, establishment, reproductive development, filling and other stages can be important.

❌ "Critical stage means maximum water requirement."
No.
Sensitivity to stress and water consumption are related but different.

❌ "Wait until the crop wilts before irrigating."
❌ "More irrigation always gives higher yield."
Generally not desirable, especially during sensitive stages.

No.
Excess irrigation can cause:
- waterlogging,
- nutrient losses,
- root problems,
- salinity risks,
- unnecessary cost.

❌ "PWP is the irrigation point."
No.
PWP is a lower reference boundary; irrigation is generally scheduled before severe stress develops.

58. Most Important Relationships
Soil water and crop stage
Soil Moisture+Crop Stage→Water Stress Risk 

Water stress and yield
Stress Intensity×Duration×Timing→Yield Effect 

Reproductive stage
Water Stress→Pollination/Fertilization/Set↓→Yield↓ 

Filling stage
Water Stress→Assimilate Supply↓→Unit Weight↓ 

Irrigation
Critical Stage+Root Zone Moisture+ET+Weather→Irrigation Decision 

59. Complete Critical-Moisture Framework
Crop Establishment   
↓
Maintain adequate moisture
↓
Vegetative Growth   
↓
Maintain adequate root-zone water
↓
Flowering   
↓
Protect reproductive processes
↓
Fruit/Pod/Grain Set   
↓
Protect reproductive-unit number
↓
Filling   
↓
Protect unit weight
↓
Maturity   
↓

Adjust irrigation according to remaining crop demand

60. Exam-Ready Definition
Critical moisture stages are the specific stages of crop development during which
adequate soil moisture is particularly important because water stress during these
stages can cause disproportionately large reductions in establishment, reproductive
success, yield components, quality or final yield. Important stages may include
germination, seedling establishment, flowering, pollination, fruit/pod/grain formation
and grain or seed filling, but the exact critical stages vary with crop, genotype,
environment and production system.

61. Final One-Line Memory
Critical Moisture Stages = Growth stages when avoiding water stress is especially important because th
e crop is highly sensitive and yield formation can be strongly affected.   

The easiest way to remember:
Establishment → Flowering → Set → Filling   
Think of the yield components:
Early Water→Plant Population    
Flowering Water→Reproductive Unit Number     Set-stage Water→Fruit/Pod/Grain Number    
Filling Water→Fruit/Seed/Grain Weight    
And the master principle:
The effect of drought depends not only on how much water is missing, but also on WHEN the water is
missing.   

## Module 11 — Water & Irrigation
## Management
7. Irrigation Scheduling
## 1. Introduction
Irrigation scheduling is the process of deciding when to irrigate and how much water to apply to a
crop so that sufficient moisture is maintained in the root zone without unnecessary water application.
In simple terms:
Irrigation Scheduling=When to irrigate+How much to irrigate 
= +  
It connects:
Crop Water Requirement→Soil Moisture→Weather/ET→Irrigation Timing→Irrigation Amount          
The objective is not to keep soil continuously wet. The objective is to maintain adequate root-zone
moisture while avoiding harmful water stress, waterlogging, and unnecessary water use.

## 2. Definition
Irrigation scheduling is the planning and determination of the timing, frequency, and
amount of irrigation water to be applied to a crop based on crop water requirements,
soil-water availability, weather conditions, growth stage, root-zone characteristics, and
irrigation-system efficiency.
It answers two fundamental questions:

Question 1 — When?
When should irrigation be applied?  

Question 2 — How much?
How much water should be applied?  

3. Main Objectives
Irrigation scheduling aims to:

1. maintain adequate soil moisture,
2. prevent severe water stress,
3. protect critical moisture stages,
4. maximize water-use efficiency,
5. maintain yield and quality,
6. avoid over-irrigation,
7. reduce waterlogging,
8. reduce nutrient leaching,
9. reduce pumping/energy costs,
10.improve irrigation efficiency,
11.conserve limited water resources,
12.coordinate irrigation with rainfall,
13.optimize crop production under available water.
The overall objective is:
Right Amount of Water + Right Time + Right Root Zone   

4. Why Irrigation Scheduling Is Necessary
Crop water demand is not constant.
It changes with:
- crop growth stage,
- temperature,
- solar radiation,
- humidity,
- wind,
- canopy size,
- soil moisture,
- root depth,
- crop variety,
- rainfall.

Therefore, applying the same amount of water every fixed number of days is not always appropriate.
For example:
EThot,dry,windy>ETcool,humid,calm  > ET  cool,humid,calm   
So irrigation demand can be very different even for the same crop.

5. Basic Concept
Imagine the root zone as a water reservoir.
After sufficient drainage:
FC 
represents an upper reference point.
As the crop consumes water:
FC→Water DepletionFC Eventually:
PWP 
represents a lower reference point associated with persistent wilting under standardized conditions.
Thus:
FC→Available Water→Depletion→Irrigation 
   
Good scheduling generally irrigates before severe stress develops, rather than waiting for PWP.

6. Field Capacity, PWP and Irrigation
Scheduling
Available water capacity is approximately:
AWC=θFC−θPWP -   
For a root zone:
TAW=(θFC−θPWP)Zr - )Z r  
where:
- TAWTAW = total available water,
- ZrZ r = effective root-zone depth.

7. Readily Available Water
Not all available water is normally allowed to be depleted before irrigation.
A commonly used concept is:
RAW=p×TAW 
where:
- RAWRAW = readily available water,
- pp = allowable depletion fraction,
- TAWTAW = total available water.
When depletion approaches the selected threshold, irrigation is triggered.
Conceptually:
Field Capacity→Depletion→RAW Threshold→Irrigation      

8. Irrigation Scheduling and Crop
Evapotranspiration
One of the most important quantities is:
ET=E+T 
where:
- EE = evaporation,
- TT = transpiration.
Reference evapotranspiration:
ET0 
is commonly used to represent atmospheric evaporative demand.
Crop evapotranspiration is estimated using:
ETc=KcET0 
where:
- ETcET c = crop evapotranspiration,
- KcK c = crop coefficient,

- ET0ET 0 = reference evapotranspiration.

9. Growth Stage and Irrigation Scheduling
The crop coefficient changes with growth stage.
Conceptually:
Kc,initial→Kc,development→Kc,mid→Kc,end   K  c,development 
 K  c,mid   K  c,end   
As canopy develops, transpiration generally becomes increasingly important.
Therefore:
ETc=KcET0 
changes through the season.

10. Critical Moisture Stages
Scheduling should give particular attention to moisture-sensitive stages such as:
- germination,
- seedling establishment,
- flowering,
- pollination,
- fruit set,
- pod formation,
- grain formation,
- grain filling,
- seed filling.
A simplified principle is:
Critical Stage+Water Stress→Potentially Large Yield Loss 
Therefore irrigation scheduling is not simply about total seasonal water—it is also about timing.

11. Soil-Water Balance Approach
A fundamental equation is:
ΔS=P+I+CR−R−D−ET 
where:
- ΔS S = change in soil-water storage,
- PP = precipitation,
- II = irrigation,
- CRCR = capillary rise,
- RR = runoff,
- DD = deep drainage,
- ETET = evapotranspiration.
This is the foundation of water-balance-based irrigation scheduling.

12. Simple Daily Water Balance
Suppose:
- soil loses 5 mm through ET,
- rainfall provides 2 mm,
- no runoff or drainage.
Then:
ΔS=2−5=−3mm S=2-5=-3mm
The root-zone storage decreases by approximately:
3mm 
This depletion accumulates until irrigation becomes necessary.

13. Irrigation Trigger
A simple conceptual trigger is:
D≥RAW⇒Irrigate   
where:

- DD = root-zone depletion,
- RAWRAW = readily available water.
Thus:
Measure/Estimate Depletion→Compare with Threshold→Irrigate     

14. How Much Water to Apply?
Once irrigation is required, the amount should generally replenish the appropriate portion of the root
zone.
The net irrigation requirement can be conceptually expressed as the amount needed to restore desired
soil-water status, after accounting for effective rainfall and other useful water inputs.
A simplified expression is:
NIR≈D−Eff. Rainfall 
subject to the actual water balance and soil conditions.

15. Net vs Gross Irrigation
The water needed in the root zone is not necessarily equal to the amount that must be delivered to the
field.

Net irrigation requirement
Water actually needed in the crop root zone.

Gross irrigation requirement
Water that must be applied because some water is lost during application.
Therefore:
Gross Irrigation=Net IrrigationApplication Efficiency 
 Application\ Efficiency   
If:
NIR=40mmNIR=40mm
and:
Ea=0.80E a=0.80
then:

Gross=400.80=50mmGross=  0.80 =50mm
So approximately:
50mm 
must be applied to deliver 40 mm effectively under this simplified efficiency assumption.

16. Application Efficiency
Ea=Water stored/usefully delivered in root zoneWater applied×100        100  
Lower efficiency means more gross water must be applied to deliver the same net amount.

17. Frequency of Irrigation
Irrigation frequency means:
How often irrigation should be applied.
It depends on:
- ET rate,
- soil water-holding capacity,
- root depth,
- crop stage,
- allowable depletion,
- rainfall,
- irrigation method,
- weather.
A sandy soil with low storage may require more frequent irrigation than a loamy soil with greater
available water.
But frequency should not be determined from soil texture alone.

18. Irrigation Interval
A simplified conceptual relationship is:

Irrigation Interval≈Allowable Soil Water DepletionDaily Crop Water Use   Daily\ Crop\ Water\ Use   
For example, if allowable depletion is 30 mm and crop water use is 5 mm/day:
Interval≈305=6 daysInterval  5 =6 
This is an illustrative calculation, not a universal recommendation.

19. Irrigation Scheduling Methods
There are several major approaches.

1. Calendar-based scheduling
Irrigation is applied at predetermined intervals.
Example:
Irrigate every 7 days.

Advantages
- simple,
- easy to implement.

Limitations
It does not automatically account for changes in:
- rainfall,
- temperature,
- wind,
- ET,
- soil moisture,
- crop stage.
Therefore:
Calendar Scheduling=Simple but less responsive   

20. Soil-Moisture-Based Scheduling
Irrigation is triggered using actual soil moisture.

Methods include:
- gravimetric sampling,
- tensiometers,
- capacitance sensors,
- TDR,
- FDR,
- soil-water potential sensors.
Concept:
Measure Soil Moisture→Compare with Threshold→Irrigate 
This can be more responsive to field conditions.

21. Weather-Based Scheduling
Weather information can be used to estimate crop water use.
The basic sequence is:
Weather→ET0→Kc→ETc→Water Balance→Irrigation 
Weather variables include:
- temperature,
- solar radiation,
- humidity,
- wind speed,
- rainfall.

22. ET-Based Scheduling
This approach estimates water consumption from evapotranspiration.
ETc=KcET0 
Daily crop water use is accumulated:
Cumulative ET=∑ETc 

Irrigation can then be scheduled when cumulative depletion reaches the selected threshold.

23. Plant-Based Scheduling
The plant itself can provide indicators of water stress.
Examples:
- leaf wilting,
- leaf rolling,
- stomatal conductance,
- canopy temperature,
- stem/leaf water potential,
- growth rate.
However, visible wilting often indicates that stress has already developed.
Therefore:
Plant Symptoms 
are useful indicators, but ideally should be combined with soil and weather measurements.

24. Remote-Sensing-Based Scheduling
Modern systems can use:
- satellite imagery,
- thermal imagery,
- drones,
- canopy temperature,
- vegetation indices,
- soil-moisture products.
Potential workflow:
Remote Sensing→Crop/Water Status→Spatial Variability→Irrigation Decision 
This is particularly useful for large or spatially variable fields.

25. Irrigation Scheduling Using Soil Moisture
Sensors
A sensor may measure:

Volumetric water content
θv 
- r:

Soil-water potential
Ψ 
The manager compares the reading with an appropriate crop-soil threshold.
For example:
θcurrent<θtrigger⇒Irrigation <   Irrigation  
The actual threshold must be crop-, soil- and system-specific.

26. Irrigation Scheduling by Growth Stage
A useful practical framework is:
Stage
Scheduling priority
Germination
Prevent moisture deficit during emergence
Seedling
Maintain establishment
Vegetative
Support canopy/root development
Flower initiation Avoid severe stress
Flowering
High attention to moisture
Pollination
Protect reproductive processes
Fruit/pod/grain set Protect reproductive-unit formation
Filling
Support size/weight development
Maturity
Adjust according to crop and harvest objective

27. Irrigation Scheduling and Crop Water
Requirement
The complete relationship is:

Crop Water Requirement→ETc→Soil Water Depletion→Irrigation Requirement 
Crop water requirement tells us approximately how much water the crop uses.
Scheduling determines when that water should be supplied.

28. Irrigation Scheduling and Rainfall
Rainfall should be included in the water balance.
Suppose:
ETc=6mm/dayET c=6mm/day
and rainfall supplies:
P=10mmP=10mm
Some or all rainfall may replenish soil water, depending on:
- rainfall intensity,
- infiltration,
- runoff,
- soil moisture,
- drainage,
- root-zone storage.
Therefore:
Rainfall≠Automatically Effective Rainfall 

29. Effective Rainfall
Effective rainfall is the fraction of precipitation that contributes usefully to crop water
requirements/root-zone storage.
Conceptually:
Effective Rainfall=Rainfall−Runoff−Deep Drainage−Other Losses 
The exact calculation depends on the method used.

30. Irrigation Scheduling and Soil Texture
Sandy soil
Generally:
- lower water storage,
- faster drainage,
- potentially faster depletion.
Therefore irrigation may need to be more frequent with smaller applications.

Clay soil
Generally:
- higher water retention,
- slower infiltration/drainage,
- potentially longer intervals,
- greater waterlogging risk.

Loam
Often intermediate.
But:
Texture Alone≠Irrigation Schedule 
Actual scheduling also depends on root depth, crop, climate, structure and management.

31. Irrigation Scheduling and Root Depth
A deeper root system can access a larger volume of soil.
Conceptually:
Root Depth↑→Potential Root−Zone Water Storage↑ 
Therefore two crops with identical soil and weather can have different irrigation intervals.

32. Irrigation Scheduling and Plant Population
Plant population affects canopy development and water use.

Higher population can increase:
- total leaf area,
- canopy interception,
- transpiration,
- crop water demand.
Thus:
Plant Population→Canopy→ET→Irrigation Demand 

33. Irrigation Scheduling and Crop Coefficient
The crop coefficient reflects crop-stage effects on ET.
ETc=KcET0 
As crop canopy develops:
KcK c
usually changes.
Therefore irrigation scheduling should account for crop stage rather than using one constant seasonal
value.

34. Irrigation Scheduling and Water Stress
If:
Crop Water Demand>Effective Water Supply 
then soil-water depletion increases.
Eventually:
Water Stress↑ 
which can cause:
- stomatal closure,
- reduced photosynthesis,
- reduced growth,
- reproductive failure,

- premature senescence,
- yield loss.
Scheduling attempts to prevent unacceptable levels of stress, especially at critical stages.

35. Irrigation Scheduling and Waterlogging
Over-irrigation is also a scheduling failure.
Excessive Irrigation→Saturation→Air−Filled Porosity↓→O2↓→Root Stress 
Therefore:
Good Scheduling=Avoid Both Drought and Waterlogging 

36. Irrigation Scheduling and Soil Aeration
Recall:
AFP=n−θv 
where:
- AFPAFP = air-filled porosity,
- nn = total porosity,
- θv  v = volumetric water content.
As soil water increases toward saturation:
θv↑  v and:
AFP↓AFP Thus excessive irrigation can reduce soil oxygen.

37. Irrigation Scheduling and Salinity
In saline soils, irrigation scheduling becomes more complex.
Water must be sufficient for crop needs while sometimes providing appropriate leaching to manage
salts.

Conceptually:
Salinity+Irrigation Water Quality+Drainage→Scheduling Strategy 
Simply increasing irrigation without adequate drainage may not solve salinity problems.

38. Irrigation Scheduling Under Drought
When water is limited, the objective may shift from:
meeting full crop water requirement at all times
toward:
protecting yield-forming stages and maximizing water productivity.
This can involve:
- deficit irrigation,
- prioritizing critical stages,
- improved application efficiency,
- mulching,
- conservation agriculture,
- drought-adapted varieties,
- accurate ET estimation.

39. Deficit Irrigation
Deficit irrigation deliberately supplies less than full crop water demand during selected periods.
Conceptually:
Water Supply<Full ETc 
during selected less-sensitive periods.
Water can be prioritized for:
Critical Moisture Stages 
This requires crop-specific knowledge because excessive deficit during sensitive stages can cause
major yield loss.

40. Irrigation Scheduling and Irrigation
Methods
Different methods have different application characteristics.

Surface irrigation
Water moves over the soil surface.
Scheduling must consider:
- field slope,
- infiltration,
- distribution,
- runoff,
- deep drainage.

Sprinkler
Water is applied through sprayed droplets.
Scheduling must consider:
- application rate,
- wind,
- evaporation,
- system efficiency.

Drip
Water is applied near the root zone.
Scheduling can involve:
- shorter intervals,
- smaller applications,
- frequent monitoring.
However:
Irrigation Method≠Irrigation Schedule 
The method determines how water is delivered; scheduling determines when and how much.

41. Irrigation Duration
For a system with known application rate:
Time=Required Water DepthApplication Rate 
 Application\ Rate   
For example, if a system applies:
5mm/hour5mm/hour
and 20 mm is required:
Time=205=4 hoursTime=  5 =4\ hours
Again, actual calculations must account for system uniformity and efficiency.

42. Irrigation Volume
Water depth can be converted to volume.
1mm=1L/m2 
and:
1mm over 1ha=10m3 
Therefore:
V=P×A 
when consistent units are used.
Example:
50 mm over 2 ha:
V=50×2×10V=50 2 10 V=1000m3 
before accounting for application efficiency if this is the required net depth.

43. Irrigation Scheduling Example
Suppose:
TAW=100mmTAW=100mm
and the selected allowable depletion is:
p=0.40p=0.40

Then:
RAW=0.40×100RAW=0.40 100 RAW=40mm 
If current depletion reaches approximately 40 mm, irrigation may be triggered under this simplified
scheduling framework.
If application efficiency is 80%:
Gross=400.80Gross=  0.80  Gross=50mm 
This demonstrates the sequence:
TAW→RAW→Trigger→Net Irrigation→Gross Application 

44. Another Example Using ET
Suppose crop ET is:
ETc=5mm/dayET c=5mm/day
and allowable depletion is:
25mm25mm
Ignoring rainfall and other inputs:
Interval=255Interval=  5  5 days 
If 8 mm effective rainfall occurs during that period, depletion is reduced and the next irrigation would
be delayed.
This demonstrates why fixed-calendar irrigation can be inferior to responsive scheduling under variable
weather.

45. Irrigation Scheduling Workflow
A practical scheduling system can follow:
1. Identify Crop 
↓
2. Identify Growth Stage 
↓
3. Determine Root Zone 
↓

4. Determine FC and PWP 
↓
5. Calculate TAW 
↓
6. Select Allowable Depletion 
↓
7. Monitor Rainfall/ET/Soil Moisture 
↓
8. Estimate Depletion 
↓
9. Trigger Irrigation 
↓
10. Calculate Net Water 
↓
11. Adjust for Efficiency 
↓
12. Apply Water 
↓
13. Monitor Soil and Crop Response 

46. The Three Main Scheduling Approaches
Method

Main information

Main advantage

Main limitation
Ignores changing
conditions

Calendar

Fixed interval

Simple

Soil moisture

Actual soil water

Directly observes root-zone
Sensors/sampling required
status

Atmospheric demand + crop
Responsive to weather
stage
Direct physiological
Plant-based
Crop response
indication
Remote
Spatial crop/water
Large-area monitoring
sensing
information
The strongest modern approach can combine multiple sources.
ET/weather

Requires data/calculation
Stress may already be
- ccurring
Technology/data
requirements

47. Integrated Irrigation Scheduling
A robust approach combines:
Soil Moisture+Weather+ET+Crop Stage+Rainfall+Root Zone 
to make the irrigation decision.
For example:
ETc+Rainfall+Soil Moisture+Critical Stage→Irrigation Decision 

48. Smart Irrigation Scheduling
Modern agriculture can automate scheduling using:
- soil-moisture sensors,
- weather stations,
- IoT systems,
- automated valves,
- flow meters,
- satellite data,
- drones,
- AI/ML,
- mobile applications,
- variable-rate irrigation.
A simplified automated system:
Sensor→Controller→Decision Rule→Valve→Irrigation 
Advanced systems can incorporate:
Weather Forecast+ET+Soil Moisture+Crop Stage→Predictive Irrigation 

49. Irrigation Scheduling and Weather Forecasts
Forecast information can improve decisions.
For example, if significant rainfall is forecast soon, irrigation may be postponed where agronomically
appropriate.
Conversely:
High ET+No Rainfall+Low Soil Moisture→Higher Irrigation Priority 
Forecasts are not perfect, so decisions should account for uncertainty.

50. Irrigation Scheduling and Microclimate
Field microclimate affects water use.
Important factors include:
- temperature,
- humidity,
- wind,
- radiation,
- canopy density.
For example:
Wind↑→ET Demand↑Wind  ET\ Demand and:
RH↓→VPD↑→Atmospheric Drying Demand↑RH  VPD  Atmospheric\ Drying\ Demand Thus irrigation scheduling should ideally use field-relevant weather information.

51. Irrigation Scheduling and Water-Use
Efficiency
Water-use efficiency:
WUE=Biomass or YieldWater Used  Water\ Used   
Good scheduling can improve WUE by reducing:

- unnecessary evaporation,
- runoff,
- deep drainage,
- excessive vegetative growth,
- water stress during important stages.
But maximizing WUE does not automatically mean maximizing yield.

52. Irrigation Scheduling and Nutrient Use
Over-irrigation can increase:
- nitrate leaching,
- nutrient movement below the root zone,
- fertilizer losses.
Therefore:
Irrigation Management↔Nutrient Management 
Efficient irrigation can improve nutrient-use efficiency.

53. Irrigation Scheduling and Weed
Management
Excess water can support weed growth, while poorly timed irrigation can create favorable conditions
for some weeds.
Therefore irrigation interacts with:
- weed emergence,
- crop competition,
- herbicide performance,
- crop canopy.
This is one reason irrigation should be considered as part of integrated crop management, not as an
isolated activity.

54. Irrigation Scheduling and Disease
Excess irrigation can create:
- high humidity,
- prolonged leaf wetness,
- waterlogging,
- favorable pathogen conditions.
Therefore:
Over−Irrigation→Microclimate/Disease Risk↑ 
Especially in crops and environments sensitive to excessive moisture.

55. Common Irrigation Scheduling Mistakes
1. Irrigating only according to calendar
Every 7 days 
may be inappropriate when rainfall and ET change.

2. Waiting for severe wilting
This can allow avoidable stress.

3. Irrigating too frequently
May cause:
- waterlogging,
- shallow rooting,
- nutrient loss,
- disease risk.

4. Applying too much water
More water does not necessarily mean more yield.

5. Ignoring rainfall
Rainfall should be incorporated into the water balance.

6. Ignoring soil differences
Different fields may have different water-storage capacities.

7. Ignoring growth stage
Flowering and filling may require different management from maturity.

8. Ignoring irrigation efficiency
Gross water application can be substantially larger than net root-zone requirement.

56. Irrigation Scheduling vs Irrigation
Management
Irrigation scheduling
Focuses primarily on:
When+HowMuch 

Irrigation management
Broader concept including:
- water source,
- method,
- scheduling,
- efficiency,
- distribution,
- drainage,
- water quality,
- energy,
- salinity,
- infrastructure.
Thus:
Scheduling⊆Irrigation Management 

57. Irrigation Scheduling vs Water Requirement
Concept
Main question
Crop water requirement How much water does the crop need?
ET
How much water is leaving through evaporation + transpiration?
Irrigation requirement How much water must irrigation supply?
Irrigation scheduling
When and how much should irrigation be applied?
Irrigation method
How should the water be delivered?
These concepts are related but not interchangeable.

58. Complete Mathematical Framework
Reference ET
ET0 

Crop ET
ETc=KcET0 

Available water
AWC=θFC−θPWP -   

Total available water
TAW=(θFC−θPWP)Zr - )Z r  

Readily available water
RAW=p×TAW 

Soil-water balance
ΔS=P+I+CR−R−D−ET 

Net-to-gross irrigation
Gross=NetApplication Efficiency  Application\ Efficiency   

Water volume
V=P×A 
when units are consistent.

59. Master Irrigation Scheduling Model
The entire concept can be summarized as:
Weather→ET0   ET 0   ET0+Kc→ETc  ETc+Rainfall+Irrigation→Soil Water Balance  Soil Water Balance→Root−Zone Depletion 
Depletion+Crop Stage→Irrigation Trigger  Irrigation Trigger→Net Water→Gross Application 

60. Practical Example — Complete Calculation
Assume:
- root-zone depth = 0.6 m,
- FC = 0.30 m3/m3m 3/m 3,
- PWP = 0.15 m3/m3m 3/m 3,
- allowable depletion p=0.40p=0.40,
- crop ET = 5 mm/day,
- no effective rainfall.

Step 1 — Available water
AWC=0.30−0.15=0.15AWC=0.30-0.15=0.15

Step 2 — Root-zone TAW
TAW=0.15×0.6TAW=0.15 0.6 =0.09m=0.09m TAW=90mm 

Step 3 — Readily available water
RAW=0.40×90RAW=0.40 90 RAW=36mm 

Step 4 — Approximate interval
Interval=365Interval=  5  7.2 days 
So under these simplified assumptions, irrigation would be triggered after approximately 7 days.
If application efficiency is 75%:
Gross=360.75Gross=  0.75  Gross=48mm 
This illustrates the complete calculation:

FC→PWP→TAW→RAW→ET→Irrigation Interval→Gross Water 

61. Important Limitation of the Example
The above calculation is educational and simplified.
Real scheduling may require:
- variable root depth,
- changing KcK c,
- actual daily ET,
- rainfall,
- effective rainfall,
- capillary rise,
- runoff,
- drainage,
- soil-layer differences,
- irrigation-system uniformity,
- crop stage,
- salinity,
- forecast weather.
Therefore:
   

62. Best Conceptual Strategy
For practical agriculture, the strongest general framework is:
Measure + Estimate + Observe + Adjust   

Measure
Soil moisture and irrigation flow.

Estimate
ET, rainfall contribution and water balance.

Observe
Crop stage and stress.

Adjust
Irrigation timing and amount.

63. Exam-Ready Definition
Irrigation scheduling is the systematic determination of when irrigation should be
applied and how much water should be supplied to a crop based on crop water
requirement, evapotranspiration, soil-water availability, root-zone depth, rainfall,
crop growth stage, weather conditions and irrigation-system efficiency. Its objective is
to maintain adequate soil moisture, prevent excessive water stress and waterlogging,
protect critical moisture stages, conserve water and achieve efficient crop production.

64. Final One-Line Memory
Irrigation Scheduling = Decide WHEN to irrigate + Decide HOW MUCH water to apply.
   

Remember the complete chain:
Weather→ET0→Kc→ETc→Soil-Water Depletion→Critical Stage→Irrigation Trigger→Net Water→G
ross Application   ET 0  K c  ET c
      
And the three most important principles:
Don’t irrigate simply because the calendar says so.   Don’t wait until severe wilting/PWP.   Protect critical moisture stages while avoiding over-irrigation.  

## Module 11 — Water & Irrigation
## Management
8. Flood Irrigation
## 1. Introduction
Flood irrigation is a traditional surface-irrigation method in which water is released onto a field and
allowed to spread over a large portion or the entire field surface, mainly under gravity.
The basic principle is:
Water Source→Field Surface→Spreading→Infiltration→Root Zone 
     
It is one of the simplest forms of irrigation because it generally requires relatively simple field
infrastructure and uses gravity rather than pressurized pipes to distribute water.

## 2. Definition
Flood irrigation is a surface-irrigation method in which irrigation water is released
- nto the field and allowed to flow and spread over the soil surface, often covering a
substantial portion of the field, before infiltrating into the root zone.

One-line definition
Flood Irrigation = Allowing irrigation water to spread over the field surface by gravity.
   

3. Basic Principle
Flood irrigation depends primarily on:
- gravity,
- field slope,
- field elevation,
- soil infiltration,
- water flow rate,

- field surface condition.
The basic process is:
Water Application→Surface Flow→Infiltration→Root-Zone Storage 
    
Unlike drip or sprinkler irrigation, water is not individually delivered to each plant.

4. How Flood Irrigation Works
The general sequence is:

Step 1 — Water source
Water comes from:
- canal,
- reservoir,
- pond,
- farm water storage,
- tube well,
- river or other irrigation source where appropriate.

Step 2 — Field entry
Water enters through:
- field channel,
- inlet,
- pipe,
- opening in a bund,
- irrigation furrow/channel.

Step 3 — Surface spreading
Water moves across the field under gravity.

Step 4 — Infiltration
Water enters the soil:
Surface Water→Soil Pores   

Step 5 — Root-zone storage
Water becomes available to crop roots.

Step 6 — Drainage
Excess water may:
- infiltrate deeper,
- run off,
- collect in low areas,
- leave through drainage.

5. Simple Diagram
Canal/Pond/Well  
↓
Field Inlet  
↓
Water Spreads Across Field  
↓
Surface Flow  
↓
Infiltration  
↓
Root Zone  
↓
Crop Water Uptake  

6. Why Is It Called "Flood" Irrigation?
It is called flood irrigation because water is allowed to spread over a broad area of the field surface
rather than being delivered only to individual plants or narrow irrigation zones.
However, flood irrigation does not necessarily mean permanently submerging the crop.
This is an important distinction:

Flood Irrigation≠Permanent Flooding   
Irrigation water is applied and allowed to infiltrate or drain.

7. Main Characteristics
Flood irrigation generally has:
- surface water application,
- gravity-driven movement,
- relatively simple infrastructure,
- low pressure requirements,
- large water application areas,
- relatively limited control over exact water distribution compared with drip systems.

8. Field Requirements
Good flood irrigation generally benefits from:

1. Suitable land slope
Excessive slope can cause:
Runoff↑→Erosion↑ 
Very uneven land can cause:
High Areas→Under−irrigation 
Low Areas→Over−irrigation 
Therefore land levelling is important.

9. Role of Land Levelling
Recall:
High Areas→Cut  Low Areas→Fill 
A relatively uniform field surface allows water to distribute more evenly.
Thus:

Better Levelling→More Uniform Surface Irrigation 
Precision land levelling can improve surface-irrigation performance.

10. Soil Requirements
Soil infiltration characteristics strongly affect flood irrigation.

Sandy soils
Usually:
- rapid infiltration,
- lower water-holding capacity,
- potentially greater deep percolation.

Clay soils
Usually:
- slower infiltration,
- higher water retention,
- greater potential for prolonged ponding/waterlogging.

Loam soils
Often provide intermediate characteristics.
But:
Soil Texture Alone≠Irrigation Suitability 
Structure, compaction, organic matter, slope and drainage also matter.

11. Flood Irrigation and Soil Infiltration
Water moves downward into the soil according to infiltration characteristics.
Conceptually:
Surface Water→Infiltration→Root−Zone Moisture 
If water is applied faster than the soil can infiltrate it:

Application Rate>Infiltration Capacity→Runoff/Ponding 

12. Root-Zone Water
The purpose of irrigation is ultimately to supply useful water to the crop root zone.
Therefore:
Flooding the Surface≠Automatically Supplying the Root Zone Efficiently    
Water may be lost through:
- runoff,
- evaporation,
- deep percolation,
- leakage,
- poor distribution.

13. Water Movement
Flood irrigation involves several water pathways:
Applied Water=Infiltrated+Runoff+Deep Percolation+Evaporation+Other Losses 
The exact balance depends on field conditions.
The objective is to maximize the useful fraction reaching the root zone.

14. Irrigation Efficiency
A simplified application-efficiency concept is:
Ea=Water stored in the effective root zoneWater applied×100        100  
Flood irrigation can have relatively lower application efficiency than well-designed pressurized
systems, but performance varies enormously with:
- field preparation,
- land levelling,

- soil,
- operator skill,
- water management,
- field size,
- irrigation design.
Therefore it is incorrect to assume that every flood-irrigated field has the same efficiency.

15. Advantages of Flood Irrigation
1. Simple system
It can require relatively simple field infrastructure.

2. Low pressure requirement
Water generally moves using gravity.

3. Lower equipment complexity
Compared with pressurized systems such as drip or sprinkler irrigation, specialized equipment can be
less extensive.

4. Suitable where water is readily available
It can be practical where:
- surface water is available,
- land is reasonably suitable,
- labor and management systems support it.

5. Large-area application
Water can be distributed over substantial field areas.

6. Can be integrated with field channels
Existing surface-water infrastructure can sometimes be used.

7. Useful for some traditional production systems
It remains widely used in many agricultural regions.

16. Disadvantages of Flood Irrigation
The major limitation is limited control over where the water goes compared with localized irrigation.
Potential problems include:

1. Water loss
Through:
- runoff,
- evaporation,
- deep percolation.

2. Uneven distribution
High and low parts of an uneven field may receive different amounts.

## 3. Waterlogging
Excessive application can cause:
Saturation→Air-filled Porosity↓→O2↓→Root Stress filled\
Porosity  O 2  Root\ Stress  

4. Nutrient losses
Excess water can contribute to:
- nitrate leaching,
- nutrient movement,
- runoff losses.

5. Soil erosion
Especially on sloping fields or when flow velocity is high.

6. Salinity problems
Repeated irrigation followed by evaporation can contribute to salt accumulation where drainage is
inadequate.

7. Labor requirements
Water may need to be manually controlled and redirected.

17. Flood Irrigation and Waterlogging
One of the most important risks is over-irrigation.
When soil becomes saturated:
Water Filled Pores↑→Air Filled Pores↓ 
Therefore:
O2 Availability↓ 
Root respiration can become limited.
Root Respiration↓→Root Function↓→Crop Stress 

18. Flood Irrigation and Soil Aeration
Recall:
AFP=n−θv 
where:
- AFPAFP = air-filled porosity,
- nn = total porosity,
- θv  v = volumetric water content.
When water content increases toward saturation:
θv↑  v therefore:
AFP↓AFP So excessive flood irrigation can reduce soil aeration.

19. Flood Irrigation and Salinity
Salinity is particularly important in areas with:
- high evaporation,
- saline irrigation water,
- poor drainage,

- shallow water tables.
The general pathway can be:
Irrigation→Water Added→Evaporation→Salt Concentration 
If drainage is insufficient:
Salt Accumulation↑ 
Therefore:
Irrigation+Drainage+Water Quality 
must be considered together.

20. Flood Irrigation and Drainage
Good irrigation and good drainage are complementary.

Irrigation:
Water→Field 

Drainage:
Excess Water→Outlet 
Therefore:
Irrigation+Drainage→Controlled Root-Zone Water + 
  

21. Flood Irrigation vs Flooding the Crop
These terms should not be confused.

Flood irrigation
Water is deliberately applied and allowed to spread over the field.

Uncontrolled flooding
Excess water enters the field because of:
- heavy rainfall,
- river overflow,
- drainage failure,

- storm events.
Thus:
Flood Irrigation = Controlled Agricultural Water Application   
while:
Flooding = Excess/Uncontrolled Water Accumulation   

22. Flood Irrigation vs Basin Irrigation
These terms are sometimes used differently in agricultural practice.

Basin irrigation
The field is divided into relatively enclosed basins using bunds, and water is applied to each basin.

Flood irrigation
A broader term for allowing water to spread over the field surface.
Thus basin irrigation can be considered a more controlled form of surface flooding in many
classifications.

23. Common Surface-Irrigation Forms
Surface irrigation includes several approaches.

1. Wild/free flooding
Water is released and allowed to spread relatively freely.

2. Border irrigation
Field is divided into long strips/borders.

3. Basin irrigation
Water is contained within bunded basins.

4. Furrow irrigation
Water flows through furrows between crop rows.
These systems differ in how water is controlled.

24. Flood Irrigation vs Furrow Irrigation

Flood irrigation
Furrow irrigation

25. Flood Irrigation vs Drip Irrigation
