26. Flood Irrigation vs Sprinkler
Flood
Water moves across soil surface  

Sprinkler
Water applied through pressurized spray  
Sprinklers can provide more controlled application, but they require pressurized equipment and can be
affected by wind and evaporation.

27. Flood Irrigation and Land Preparation
The success of surface irrigation is strongly affected by field preparation.
Important operations can include:
Field Clearing→Tillage→Levelling→Field Layout→Irrigation 
Uneven land can result in uneven water distribution.

28. Role of Bunds
Bunds or field borders can help:
- contain water,
- divide fields,
- control flow,
- reduce uncontrolled runoff.
Conceptually:
Bund→Water Containment→Better Control 
Poorly maintained bunds can leak or fail.

29. Flood Irrigation and Irrigation Scheduling
Flood irrigation still requires proper scheduling.
It should not mean:
"Put as much water as possible on the field."
Instead:
Scheduling=When to Irrigate+How Much to Apply 
The same principles discussed earlier apply:
ETc=KcET0ET c=K cET 0
and:
ΔS=P+I+CR−R−D−ET S=P+I+CR-R-D-ET

30. Flood Irrigation and Critical Moisture
Stages
Irrigation timing should consider crop-sensitive stages.
For example:
Flowering→Pollination→Fruit/Grain/Pod Set→Filling 
Adequate moisture during important stages can protect yield formation.
However, excessive water during these stages can also cause problems.
Therefore:
Adequate Moisture≠Maximum Water 

31. Flood Irrigation and Soil Moisture
The objective is to replenish soil water within the root zone.
A simplified conceptual sequence:
Soil Moisture Depletion→Irrigation→Root−Zone Replenishment 
The amount should be based on:
- soil water deficit,
- root-zone depth,
- crop stage,
- rainfall,
- ET,
- infiltration,
- application efficiency.

32. Flood Irrigation and Field Capacity
After irrigation and drainage, the soil may approach field capacity.
The goal is generally not to maintain saturation indefinitely.

Irrigation→Infiltration→Drainage→Desired Root−Zone Moisture 
Excess application beyond the useful storage capacity may simply move below the root zone or run off.

33. Flood Irrigation and Water-Holding
Capacity
Consider two soils.

Soil A
Low available water capacity.
AWC↓AWC It may require more frequent irrigation.

Soil B
Higher available water capacity.
AWC↑AWC It may store more water in the root zone.
Therefore:
AWC→Irrigation Interval 
But crop and weather must also be considered.

34. Water Application Depth
If a known water depth is applied:
V=P×A 
For example:
50 mm over 1 hectare:
50×10=500m350 10=500m 3
Therefore:
50mm=500m3/ha 
before accounting for whether 50 mm represents net or gross application.

35. Net and Gross Water
Suppose the crop needs:
40mm40mm
in the effective root zone.
If application efficiency is:
80%80\%
then:
Gross=400.80Gross=  0.80  Gross=50mm 
Thus 50 mm may need to be applied to deliver approximately 40 mm effectively under the simplified
assumption.

36. Water Distribution Problem
One major issue with flood irrigation is uneven distribution.
Suppose:
- high area receives 20 mm,
- low area receives 60 mm.
The low area may become over-irrigated while the high area remains under-irrigated.
This can produce:
Uneven Crop Growth 
and:
Uneven Yield 

37. Improving Flood-Irrigation Performance
Flood irrigation can be improved through:

1. Proper land levelling
Better Surface→Better Distribution  2. Appropriate field size
Smaller/manageable units can improve control.

3. Proper inlet design
Controls the rate and direction of water entry.

4. Appropriate irrigation timing
Avoid excessive depletion or excess application.

5. Controlled flow
Avoid excessively high flow rates.

6. Good drainage
Prevents prolonged saturation.

7. Soil-moisture monitoring
Improves scheduling.

8. Bund maintenance
Reduces leakage and uncontrolled runoff.

38. Flood Irrigation and Conservation
Traditional flood irrigation can be made more efficient through:
- precision land levelling,
- improved field channels,
- appropriate basin/border design,
- better scheduling,
- soil-moisture monitoring,
- reduced runoff,
- proper drainage,
- appropriate irrigation depth.
Thus:
Traditional Method+Better Management→Improved Water Productivity 

39. Where Flood Irrigation May Be Suitable
It may be suitable where:

- water is reasonably available,
- land can be adequately levelled,
- soil infiltration is manageable,
- drainage is adequate,
- gravity irrigation is feasible,
- crops tolerate the application pattern,
- pressurized systems are not economically or technically appropriate.
Suitability is site-specific.

40. Where It Can Be Problematic
Potentially problematic situations include:
- highly uneven land,
- steep slopes,
- highly permeable soils with excessive deep drainage,
- poorly drained soils,
- saline soils with inadequate drainage,
- water-scarce environments where losses must be minimized,
- crops highly sensitive to prolonged wetness.

41. Flood Irrigation and Water Scarcity
When water is limited, uncontrolled flooding can result in substantial losses.
The goal should shift toward:
Maximum Useful Root−Zone Storage 
rather than:
Maximum Surface Water 
This is why improved surface-irrigation design and scheduling can be important even when the basic
irrigation method remains unchanged.

42. Flood Irrigation and Nutrient Management
Excess water can increase nutrient losses.
For nitrogen:
Excess Water→Leaching/Denitrification/Runoff Risk↑ 
Therefore irrigation should be coordinated with fertilizer application.

43. Flood Irrigation and Soil Erosion
If water moves rapidly over the surface:
Flow Velocity↑→Soil Detachment/Transport↑ 
Risk increases with:
- slope,
- bare soil,
- weak aggregation,
- excessive flow,
- poor field design.
Conservation practices can reduce erosion.

44. Flood Irrigation and Soil Structure
Repeated excessive wetting and drying can influence soil structure, while poorly managed surface flow
can cause:
- crusting,
- erosion,
- structural degradation.
Good soil structure helps:
Infiltration+Aeration+Root Growth 

45. Flood Irrigation and Crop Health
Poorly managed flood irrigation can contribute to:
- root diseases,
- waterlogging,
- nutrient deficiency,
- salt accumulation,
- uneven crop growth.
Therefore:
Irrigation Management→Soil Health→Root Health→Crop Performance 

46. Flood Irrigation and Mechanization
Uneven or poorly designed fields can interfere with:
- machinery movement,
- planting,
- harvesting,
- drainage,
- field traffic.
Good land levelling and field layout can improve both irrigation and mechanization.

47. Advantages vs Disadvantages — Quick

Simple
Water losses can be high

Lower equipment complexity
Runoff

Can use surface-water infrastructure Nutrient losses
Familiar traditional method
Salinity risk under poor drainage

Advantages
Potentially low capital cost

Disadvantages
More difficult precise control

48. Flood Irrigation vs Other Methods

Basin

Flood irrigation is therefore a surface-irrigation method.

49. Key Formulae
Crop ET

Soil-water balance

AWC=θFC−θPWP -

50. Practical Flood-Irrigation Workflow
1. Assess Crop Stage 
↓
2. Check Root−Zone Moisture 
↓
3. Estimate Water Requirement 
↓
4. Check Rainfall 
↓
5. Determine Net Irrigation Requirement 
↓
6. Check Field Levelling/Drainage 
↓
7. Apply Controlled Flow 
↓
8. Allow Infiltration 
↓
9. Stop Before Excessive Runoff/Waterlogging 
↓
10. Monitor Root−Zone Moisture 

51. Most Important Concept
The purpose of flood irrigation is not to maximize the amount of water on the soil surface.
The objective is:
Move sufficient water into the effective root zone   

while minimizing:
Runoff+Evaporation+Deep Percolation+Waterlogging 
Therefore:
Good Flood Irrigation=Controlled Surface Flow+Uniform Infiltration+Proper Scheduling+Adequate Dr
ainage  =   +   +   +    

52. Exam-Ready Definition
Flood irrigation is a surface-irrigation method in which water is released onto the
agricultural field and allowed to spread over a substantial portion or the entire soil
surface under gravity, from where it infiltrates into the soil and replenishes the crop
root zone. Its effectiveness depends on land levelling, soil infiltration, field slope, flow
rate, irrigation scheduling, drainage and water management. Poorly managed flood
irrigation can cause runoff, deep percolation, uneven distribution, waterlogging,
nutrient loss and salinity problems.

53. Final One-Line Memory
Flood Irrigation = Apply water to the field surface → let gravity spread it → allow infiltration into the r
- ot zone.   

Remember:
Flood = Broad Surface   
Furrow = Channel   
Drip = Localized Root Zone   
Sprinkler = Pressurized Droplets   
And the central principle:
Irrigate the root zone, not merely the soil surface.   

## Module 11 — Water & Irrigation
## Management
9. Furrow Irrigation
## 1. Introduction
Furrow irrigation is a type of surface irrigation in which water is conveyed through small, elongated
channels called furrows, usually located between crop rows. Water moves along the furrow by gravity
and infiltrates laterally and downward into the soil, supplying the crop root zone.
The basic principle is:
Water Source→Furrow→Surface Flow→Infiltration→Root Zone 
     
In contrast to flood irrigation, where water may spread broadly over the field surface, furrow irrigation
concentrates water into specific channels.

## 2. Definition
Furrow irrigation is a surface-irrigation method in which water is applied through
small, elongated channels or furrows between crop rows and allowed to move along
the field by gravity while infiltrating into the soil and supplying water to the crop root
zone.

One-line definition
Furrow Irrigation = Supplying irrigation water through channels between crop rows.
  

3. Basic Principle
A furrow is essentially a small channel created in the soil.
Ridge/BedFurrowRidge/Bed     
Water flows through the depressed portion:
Furrow Water→Lateral Infiltration+Downward Infiltration→Root Zone 
 +     
The crop itself does not necessarily need to be directly submerged.

4. How Furrow Irrigation Works
Step 1 — Furrows are prepared
Furrows are created between crop rows.

Step 2 — Water enters the furrow
Water is supplied from:
- field channel,
- irrigation pipe,
- canal,
- pump,
- reservoir,
- other suitable irrigation source.

Step 3 — Water moves along the furrow
Gravity causes water to flow from the upper end toward the lower end.

Step 4 — Water infiltrates
Water moves:
- downward,
- sideways.

Step 5 — Root zone is replenished
Roots absorb the available soil water.

Step 6 — Irrigation is stopped
The inflow is stopped when the required amount of water has been delivered.

5. Simple Diagram
Crop Row

Crop Row

│

│

│

│

🌱

🌱

──────────┴──────────────────────┴──────────

\

/
\

## Water
/

\

## Furrow
/

\ /
↓
Infiltration

↙

↘

Root-zone water

The actual furrow geometry varies with crop, soil and irrigation system.

6. Main Components
A furrow-irrigation system generally involves:
1. water source,
2. field channel or supply pipe,
3. field inlet,
4. furrows,
5. crop rows,
6. field slope/grade,
7. cutoff point,
8. drainage or runoff arrangement.

7. Important Furrow Characteristics
Important design parameters include:
- furrow spacing,
- furrow depth,
- furrow width,
- furrow length,
- longitudinal slope,
- inflow rate,
- soil infiltration rate,

- irrigation duration,
- crop-row arrangement.
There is no single universal furrow size.
Furrow Design=f(Crop+Soil+Slope+Water+Management) 

8. Furrow Spacing
Furrow spacing is the distance between adjacent furrows.
It is closely related to crop-row spacing.
For example:
60 cm row spacing   
may have a furrow positioned between rows, depending on the production system.
Spacing affects:
- wetted soil area,
- lateral water movement,
- root-zone wetting,
- plant population,
- machinery,
- irrigation time.

9. Furrow Depth
Furrow depth determines the amount of water that can temporarily flow through the channel without
excessive overflow.
If too shallow:
Overflow risk↑  If excessively deep:
- soil disturbance may increase,
- erosion risk can increase,
- planting geometry may become unsuitable.
Therefore depth should be crop- and soil-specific.

10. Furrow Length
Furrow length is the distance water travels along the furrow.
If the furrow is too long:
Infiltration Time Variation↑→Distribution Uniformity↓ 
The upper part may receive substantially more water than the lower part.
If furrows are too short:
- more field channels/inlets may be needed,
- management and labor can increase.
Thus furrow length is a design trade-off.

11. Furrow Slope
A suitable slope is important.
If slope is too high:
Flow Velocity↑→Erosion Risk↑ 
If slope is too low:
Water Movement↓→Poor Advance/Drainage Potential 
Therefore the field should be graded appropriately.

12. Furrow Inflow Rate
Water entering the furrow must be controlled.
If inflow is too high:
- erosion,
- overtopping,
- runoff,
- soil disturbance.
If inflow is too low:

- slow advance,
- excessive infiltration near the inlet,
- longer irrigation time.
Therefore:
Inflow Rate→Water Advance→Infiltration Pattern     

13. Water Movement in a Furrow
Water movement occurs in two important directions.

Along the furrow
Longitudinal Flow   

Into the soil
Vertical + Lateral Infiltration   
Therefore:
Furrow Flow→Soil Infiltration→Root-Zone Wetting     

14. Lateral Infiltration
This is an important advantage of furrow irrigation.
Water does not only move downward.
It also moves sideways:
Crop

Crop

│

│

🌱

🌱

─────────┴────────────────────┴──────
## ↓ Water ↓
\ /

↙
↙

↓
↓

↘
↘

Root zone becomes wetted

This allows roots located beside the furrow to access water.

15. Wetting Pattern
The soil-wetting pattern depends on:
- soil texture,
- structure,
- infiltration rate,
- furrow spacing,
- irrigation duration,
- water amount.

Sandy soil
Water tends to move downward relatively rapidly.

Clay soil
Lateral movement can be relatively important because infiltration is slower.

Loam
Often intermediate.
Thus:
Wetting Pattern=f(Soil+Furrow+Time) 

16. Furrow Irrigation and Soil Texture
Sandy soil
Potential problems:
- rapid infiltration,
- deep percolation,
- insufficient lateral wetting.
Possible management:
- shorter furrows,
- appropriate inflow,
- more frequent/smaller irrigation where justified.

Clay soil
Potential problems:
- slow infiltration,
- prolonged ponding,
- waterlogging.

Loam soil
Often well suited to many furrow systems, depending on field conditions.
But:
Soil Texture Alone Does Not Determine Suitability   

17. Types of Furrow Irrigation
Several arrangements can be used.

1. Straight furrows
Furrows run relatively straight along the field.

2. Contour furrows
Furrows follow approximately contour lines.
They can reduce runoff velocity on suitable slopes.
Contour Furrows→Runoff Velocity↓ 

3. Graded furrows
Furrows have a controlled longitudinal grade.

## 4. Corrugations
Small shallow channels are used, particularly in some closely spaced crops.

5. Alternate furrow irrigation
Only alternate furrows are irrigated during an irrigation event.

6. Every-furrow irrigation
Water is supplied to essentially every furrow.

7. Surge irrigation
Water is delivered intermittently in cycles or surges rather than as one continuous flow.

18. Alternate Furrow Irrigation
In alternate furrow irrigation:
Furrow 1 = Irrigated   
Furrow 2 = Not Irrigated   
Then the pattern can be changed during subsequent irrigation events.
The purpose can include:
- reducing wetted area,
- reducing evaporation,
- improving water-use efficiency,
- controlling excessive wetness.
Its suitability depends strongly on soil and crop.

19. Surge Furrow Irrigation
In surge irrigation, water is supplied intermittently.
Conceptually:
Flow→Stop→Flow→Stop 
rather than:
Continuous Flow 
This can alter infiltration and water advance and may improve distribution under suitable conditions.

20. Contour Furrow Irrigation
Contour furrows are laid approximately along the contour.
They can be useful where controlled surface flow is needed on gently sloping land.
Potential effect:
Contour Layout→Runoff Speed↓→Erosion Risk↓ 

However, poor design on slopes can still result in water concentration and erosion.

21. Furrow Irrigation and Planting Position
Crops may be planted:
- on ridge tops,
- on furrow sides,
- between furrows,
- on beds with furrows between beds,
depending on crop and production system.
Thus:
Furrow Irrigation≠One Fixed Planting Arrangement 

22. Furrow Irrigation and Raised Beds
A common arrangement is:
Raised Bed→Crop Rows    
with:
Furrow   
between beds.
The furrow can serve as the water-conveyance channel.
This connects:
Raised Beds+Furrows+Irrigation 

23. Furrow Irrigation and Ridging
Recall:
Ridge=Raised Soil =   
Furrow=Depressed Channel =   
Therefore:
Ridge↔Furrow   
A ridging system naturally creates furrows that can be used for irrigation.

24. Furrow Irrigation vs Flood Irrigation
Feature
Flood irrigation
Furrow irrigation
Water application
Broad surface
Specific channels
Water control
Relatively lower Relatively greater
Crop-row relation
Less dependent Strongly related
Root-zone targeting Less localized
Better targeted
Soil surface wetted Larger area
Mainly furrow area
Evaporation potential Often higher
Can be lower
Erosion
Possible
Possible if flow is excessive
Field layout
Simple
Requires furrows

25. Furrow Irrigation vs Basin Irrigation
Basin irrigation
Water is contained within a relatively enclosed basin.

Furrow irrigation
Water flows through elongated channels.
Basin=Contained Area   
Furrow=Linear Channel   

26. Furrow Irrigation vs Drip Irrigation
Feature
Furrow
Drip
System
Surface
Pressurized/localized
Water delivery Furrow
Emitters
Main force
Gravity
Pressure
Wetted pattern Along furrow Localized
Infrastructure Usually simpler More complex
Filtration
Less central
Important
Automation
Possible
Highly adaptable
Water control Moderate
High

27. Furrow Irrigation vs Sprinkler
Furrow
Water→Soil Surface Channel 

Sprinkler
Pressurized Water→Air→Soil 
Sprinklers wet the crop canopy and soil surface depending on system, whereas furrow irrigation
primarily conveys water through soil channels.

28. Advantages of Furrow Irrigation
1. Relatively simple
The system can be constructed with basic field-shaping equipment.

## 2. Gravity-based
Usually does not require field-wide pressurization.

3. Lower surface wetting
Compared with broad flooding, only the furrow area is intentionally wetted.

4. Suitable for row crops
Particularly useful where crops are planted in distinct rows.

5. Reduced direct wetting of foliage
Water primarily contacts the soil rather than being sprayed over the canopy.

6. Can be mechanized
Furrows can be formed using suitable implements.

7. Can be integrated with raised beds
This provides combined planting and water-management geometry.

8. Fertilizer placement can be coordinated
In suitable systems, fertilizers can be placed appropriately relative to the furrow and crop.

29. Disadvantages of Furrow Irrigation
1. Uneven distribution
Upper furrow sections may receive more water than lower sections.

## 2. Runoff
Excess flow can reach the lower end and leave the field.

## 3. Erosion
High flow velocity can detach and transport soil.

4. Deep percolation
Long infiltration times can move water below the effective root zone.

## 5. Labor
Manual control may be required in traditional systems.

6. Field levelling requirement
Uneven fields can reduce uniformity.

7. Salt accumulation
Salts can accumulate in certain parts of the wetted/unwetted soil pattern under saline conditions.

## 8. Waterlogging
Poorly drained heavy soils can remain excessively wet.

30. Furrow Irrigation and Soil Erosion
This is especially important on sloping fields.
If:
Flow Velocity↑Flow\ Velocity then:
Soil Detachment and Transport Risk↑ 
Management includes:
- suitable furrow slope,
- controlled inflow,
- shorter furrows where appropriate,

- contour layout where suitable,
- good soil structure,
- residue/cover management.

31. Furrow Irrigation and Waterlogging
If too much water is applied:
Water Application↑→Soil Saturation↑ 
Then:
AFP↓→O2↓→Root Respiration↓ 
Therefore proper scheduling is essential.

32. Furrow Irrigation and Drainage
Furrows can sometimes help convey excess surface water, but an irrigation furrow should not
automatically be considered a complete drainage system.

Irrigation furrow
Water→Root Zone 

Drainage channel
Excess Water→Outlet 
Their functions may overlap in some layouts, but their primary purposes differ.

33. Furrow Irrigation and Salinity
Water moves from the furrow into surrounding soil.
As water is removed through crop uptake and evaporation, salts may become concentrated in portions
- f the soil.
Under saline conditions:
Irrigation Water→Salt Input→Evaporation/Water Uptake→Salt Accumulation 
Adequate drainage and appropriate water quality are therefore important.

34. Furrow Irrigation and Irrigation Scheduling
Furrow irrigation requires proper scheduling.
The important questions are:

When should irrigation begin?
Based on:
- soil-water depletion,
- crop stage,
- ET,
- rainfall,
- soil characteristics.

How long should water flow?
Based on:
- furrow length,
- infiltration,
- inflow rate,
- desired root-zone replenishment.
Thus:
Furrow Design+Irrigation Scheduling +   
must work together.

35. Advance and Recession
Two important concepts in surface irrigation are:

Advance
The time required for water to travel from the inlet toward the end of the furrow.
ta=Advance time   

Recession
The time at which water disappears from a particular point after inflow is stopped.

tr=Recession time   
The difference between these helps determine how long a point remains wetted.
Conceptually:
Opportunity Time=tr−ta 
Longer opportunity time generally means more infiltration, though the exact relationship depends on
soil infiltration behavior.

36. Opportunity Time
Opportunity time is the length of time water remains available at a location on the soil surface for
infiltration.
Opportunity Time=Recession Time−Advance Time 
If opportunity time differs greatly along the furrow:
Infiltration Distribution becomes Uneven 
This is a central design problem in furrow irrigation.

37. Irrigation Uniformity
Good furrow irrigation attempts to provide reasonably uniform root-zone water.
If:
Upper Furrow Water≫Lower Furrow Water   then:
- upper area may be over-irrigated,
- lower area may be under-irrigated.
Therefore:
Good Furrow Design→Better Distribution Uniformity 

38. Factors Affecting Furrow Irrigation
Performance
Performance depends on:
P=f(Soil,Slope,Furrow Length,Furrow Spacing,Inflow,Infiltration,Crop,Root Depth,Water Quality,Sche
duling) 
Important factors include:
- soil texture,
- infiltration rate,
- soil structure,
- field slope,
- field levelling,
- furrow length,
- furrow spacing,
- inflow rate,
- irrigation duration,
- crop root distribution,
- rainfall,
- drainage.

39. Furrow Irrigation and Root Distribution
Root distribution determines where water needs to reach.
If roots occupy:
0−60cm0-60cm
then irrigation should replenish an appropriate portion of this effective root zone.
The objective is:
Furrow Water→Root−Zone Wetting 
rather than unnecessary deep percolation.

40. Furrow Irrigation and Plant Population
Plant spacing determines where plants are located relative to the furrow.
For example:
Row Spacing+Plant Spacing→Planting Geometry 
and:
Furrow Position↔Crop Row 
Therefore irrigation layout and planting layout should be designed together.

41. Furrow Irrigation and Fertilizer
Fertilizer placement can influence nutrient availability.
For example, fertilizer may be placed:
- beside the crop row,
- below/near the root zone,
- in appropriate bands.
However, excessive irrigation can cause:
Nutrient Leaching↑ 
Therefore:
Fertilizer Management+Irrigation Management   
should be coordinated.

42. Improving Furrow-Irrigation Efficiency
Performance can be improved through:

1. Proper land levelling
Uniform Field Surface→Uniform Water Advance 

2. Appropriate furrow length
Avoid excessively long furrows where distribution becomes poor.

3. Controlled inflow
Avoid excessive flow and erosion.

4. Proper irrigation timing
Use soil moisture/ET/crop stage.

5. Appropriate furrow spacing
Ensure adequate lateral wetting.

6. Good drainage
Prevent prolonged saturation.

7. Surge irrigation
Can improve distribution in suitable conditions.

8. Alternate furrow irrigation
Can reduce water application in appropriate situations.

43. Furrow Irrigation and Water-Use Efficiency
Water-use efficiency can be expressed conceptually as:
WUE=YieldWater Used  Water\ Used   
Efficient furrow irrigation attempts to reduce:
- runoff,
- deep percolation,
- unnecessary evaporation,
- excessive wetting.

## 44. Example
Suppose:
- furrow length = 100 m,
- water enters at the upper end,
- infiltration is relatively rapid near the inlet.
The upper part may remain wet longer than the lower part.

Therefore:
Opportunity Timeupper>Opportunity Timelower  > Opportunity\
Time  lower   
This can cause:
Infiltrationupper>Infiltrationlower  > Infiltration  lower   
If severe, the upper end can be over-irrigated while the lower end is under-irrigated.
This is why furrow length and inflow rate matter.

45. Furrow Irrigation — Complete Water
Pathway
Water Source   
↓
Field Inlet   
↓
Furrow   
↓
Gravity Flow   
↓
Lateral + Vertical Infiltration   
↓
Root-Zone Moisture   
↓
Root Uptake   
↓
Plant Growth   

46. Furrow Irrigation and Water Balance
The field water balance can be represented as:
ΔS=P+I+CR−R−D−ET 

where:
- PP = precipitation,
- II = irrigation,
- CRCR = capillary rise,
- RR = runoff,
- DD = deep drainage,
- ETET = evapotranspiration.
Furrow irrigation primarily contributes to:
I 
while poor management can increase:
R+D 

47. Furrow Irrigation and Field Capacity
The objective is generally to replenish an appropriate amount of root-zone water.
Available water:
AWC=θFC−θPWP -   
Total available water:
TAW=(θFC−θPWP)Zr - )Z r  
Irrigation should be scheduled before unacceptable depletion occurs.

48. Furrow Irrigation and Critical Moisture
Stages
Special attention should be given to moisture-sensitive stages such as:
- flowering,
- pollination,
- fruit set,
- pod formation,
- grain formation,
- grain filling,

- seed filling.
Thus:
Crop Stage+Soil Moisture→Irrigation Priority 

49. Furrow Irrigation and Water-Saving
Strategies
Potential strategies include:
Better Levelling+Shorter/Optimized Furrows+Controlled Inflow+Alternate Furrows+Surge Flow+Bett
er Scheduling 
These should be selected according to crop, soil, slope and local conditions.

50. Common Problems
Problem
Possible cause
Upper end too wet
Low advance rate / excessive opportunity time
Lower end too dry
Poor water advance
Erosion
Excessive inflow or slope
Runoff
Excessive inflow/application
Deep percolation
Excessive opportunity time
Waterlogging
Poor drainage/excessive application
Uneven crop growth
Uneven water distribution
Salt accumulation
Saline water/poor drainage
Furrow collapse
Weak/unstable soil structure or excessive flow
Poor wetting between furrows Wide spacing/soil-dependent lateral movement

51. Common Mistakes
Mistake 1: Treating all furrows identically
Different soils require different designs.

Mistake 2: Excessive inflow
More flow is not necessarily better.

Mistake 3: Excessively long furrows
Can create poor distribution.

Mistake 4: Ignoring field slope
Can cause erosion or poor advance.

Mistake 5: Ignoring soil infiltration
Can cause over- or under-irrigation.

Mistake 6: Irrigating only by calendar
Rainfall and ET change.

Mistake 7: Ignoring drainage
Can create waterlogging and salinity problems.

Mistake 8: Confusing furrow with drainage channel
A furrow primarily serves crop-row water management; a drainage channel primarily conveys excess
water away.

52. Key Comparison
Term
Meaning
Flood irrigation
Water spreads broadly over field surface
Furrow irrigation Water flows through channels between crop rows
Basin irrigation
Water is contained in bunded basins
Border irrigation
Water flows across long strips
Raised bed
Elevated broad planting area
Ridge
Raised linear soil structure
Furrow
Depressed linear channel
Drip irrigation
Localized water through emitters
Sprinkler irrigation Pressurized water applied as spray

53. Most Important Distinctions
Furrow vs Ridge
Ridge=Raised =    Furrow=Depressed 
=   

Furrow irrigation vs flood irrigation
Flood=Broad surface application =   
Furrow=Channel-based surface application =   

Furrow vs drainage channel
Furrow→Irrigation/root-zone management    Drainage channel→Removal of excess water 
  

54. Exam-Ready Definition
Furrow irrigation is a surface-irrigation method in which water is conveyed through
small, elongated channels called furrows, usually located between crop rows. Water
moves along the furrows under gravity and infiltrates vertically and laterally into the
soil to replenish the crop root zone. Its performance depends on furrow spacing and
length, field slope, soil infiltration characteristics, inflow rate, irrigation duration,
land levelling, crop geometry and drainage.

55. Final One-Line Memory
Furrow Irrigation = Water flows through furrows between crop rows → infiltrates sideways and downw
ard → supplies the root zone.   

Remember the entire concept:
Furrow=Channel  =    
Ridge=Raised Soil  =    
Furrow Irrigation=Water Through Channel  =     Flood Irrigation=Broad Surface Water  =    
And the most important principle:
Good Furrow Irrigation = Controlled Flow + Uniform Infiltration + Proper Scheduling + Adequate Dra
inage   

## Module 11 — Water & Irrigation
## Management
10. Basin Irrigation
## 1. Introduction
Basin irrigation is a type of surface irrigation in which the field is divided into relatively level,
enclosed areas called basins using bunds or small embankments. Water is released into a basin and
allowed to spread over the enclosed surface by gravity before infiltrating into the soil.
The basic principle is:
Water Source→Basin Inlet→Water Spreads in Basin→Infiltration→Root Zone       
Unlike furrow irrigation, water does not primarily travel through narrow channels between crop rows.
Instead, it is temporarily contained within a basin.

## 2. Definition
Basin irrigation is a surface-irrigation method in which a field is divided into
relatively level, enclosed areas by bunds, and water is applied to each basin and
allowed to spread over the soil surface under gravity before infiltrating into the root
zone.

One-line definition
Basin Irrigation = Irrigating an enclosed, level field area by temporarily ponding water within it.
  

3. Basic Principle
A basin is essentially:
Bunds + Relatively Level Surface + Water Inlet   
The bunds prevent water from freely escaping.
Bund
┌─────────────────┐

│
│

│
## Water
│

│ │
│
│

🌱🌱

│

│
│
│

└─────────────────┘
Bund

Water is retained long enough for the required amount to infiltrate.

4. How Basin Irrigation Works
Step 1 — Field preparation
The field is divided into suitable units.

Step 2 — Bund construction
Small embankments are constructed around each basin.

Step 3 — Levelling
The basin surface is made relatively uniform.

Step 4 — Water application
Water enters through an inlet.

Step 5 — Water spreading
Water spreads across the basin.

Step 6 — Infiltration
Water enters the soil.
Surface Water→Root-Zone Water   

Step 7 — Inlet closure
Water supply is stopped when sufficient water has been applied.

Step 8 — Drainage/infiltration
Water infiltrates and the surface eventually becomes non-ponded.

5. Main Components
A basin irrigation system generally consists of:
1. water source,
2. main/submain field channel or pipe,
3. basin inlet,
4. basin bunds,
5. relatively level basin surface,
6. crop/planting area,
7. drainage or overflow arrangement where required.

6. Basin Structure
The basic arrangement is:
Basin=Enclosed Area+Bunds+Water Inlet  =   +  
+    
A field can contain multiple basins:
┌───────────┬───────────┬───────────┐
│
│
│

Basin 1

🌱🌱

│
│
│

Basin 2

🌱🌱

│
│
│

Basin 3

🌱🌱

│
│
│

├───────────┼───────────┼───────────┤
│
│
│

Basin 4

🌱🌱

│
│

│
│
│

Basin 6

│
│
│

└───────────┴───────────┴───────────┘

The internal bunds help keep water within each unit.

7. Why Land Levelling Is Important
Basin irrigation works best when the basin surface is reasonably level.
If the basin is uneven:

High Areas→Less Water 
Low Areas→More Water 
This creates uneven infiltration and crop growth.
Therefore:
Better Levelling→More Uniform Water Distribution 
Precision land levelling can improve basin-irrigation performance.

8. Basin Size
There is no single universal basin size.
Size depends on:
- soil infiltration rate,
- field slope,
- crop,
- water availability,
- water application rate,
- land levelling,
- machinery,
- labor,
- basin purpose.
Conceptually:
Basin Size=f(Soil+Slope+Crop+Water+Management) 

9. Small vs Large Basins
Small basins
Advantages:
- easier water control,
- potentially more uniform application,
- useful where land is not perfectly uniform.

Disadvantages:
- more bunds,
- more construction,
- more field-management work.

Large basins
Advantages:
- fewer bunds,
- simpler layout.
Disadvantages:
- greater risk of uneven water distribution if the surface is not sufficiently uniform.

10. Basin Irrigation and Soil Texture
Sandy soils
Generally:
- rapid infiltration,
- relatively low water-holding capacity.
Water may infiltrate quickly before the entire basin receives adequate water.

Clay soils
Generally:
- slower infiltration,
- greater water retention,
- greater potential for prolonged surface ponding.

Loam soils
Often intermediate.
Therefore:
Basin Design Must Match Soil Infiltration 

11. Water Movement
Unlike furrow irrigation, water spreads mainly across the basin surface.

The sequence is:
Water Entry→Surface Spreading→Ponding→Infiltration      
Water then moves through the soil:
Vertical + Lateral Movement→Root Zone    

12. Basin Irrigation and Infiltration
Infiltration determines how quickly water leaves the basin surface and enters the soil.
If infiltration is very rapid:
Water Disappears Quickly 
If infiltration is very slow:
Ponding Duration↑ 
Excessive ponding can create waterlogging problems if drainage is poor.

13. Basin Irrigation and Root-Zone Moisture
The goal is not simply to fill the basin with water.
The objective is:
Apply Enough Water→Replenish Appropriate Root-Zone Moisture    
Water that moves below the effective root zone may become deep drainage rather than useful crop
water.

14. Types of Basin Irrigation
Basin irrigation can be broadly arranged into different forms depending on crop and field design.

1. Small/individual basins
Small enclosed areas are irrigated separately.

2. Large basins
Larger areas are enclosed and irrigated as a unit.

3. Ring basins
Circular or ring-shaped basins are commonly used around individual trees or groups of trees.

4. Check basins
The field is divided into relatively level compartments using check bunds.

15. Check Basin Irrigation
Check basin irrigation involves dividing the field into small, nearly level compartments surrounded
by low bunds.
Water is introduced into each compartment.
Conceptually:
Field→Check Bunds→Individual Basins     
This provides greater control than uncontrolled flooding.

16. Ring Basin Irrigation
Ring basins are particularly useful in some orchards.
A ring or circular basin is formed around the tree:
Tree

🌳

┌─────────┐
│

## Water
│

│

## Ring
│

└─────────┘

The water is confined around the desired root-zone area.
However, the exact design should account for tree age, root distribution, soil and irrigation
requirements.

17. Basin Irrigation for Orchards
For trees, basins can be designed:

- around individual trees,
- around groups of trees,
- as larger rectangular/square units.
As the tree grows:
Root Zone Expands→Basin Design May Need Adjustment 
Water should not automatically be concentrated only around the trunk.

18. Basin Irrigation for Field Crops
Check basins can be used for various field crops where the field surface and crop arrangement make
surface irrigation appropriate.
The exact suitability depends on:
- crop tolerance to temporary wetting,
- soil,
- slope,
- drainage,
- field preparation.

19. Advantages of Basin Irrigation
1. Simple principle
The system is relatively easy to understand and operate.

## 2. Gravity-based
Water can be distributed without pressurization across the basin.

3. Good control compared with free flooding
Bunds restrict water movement.

4. Suitable for relatively level land
It works particularly well when basins are properly levelled.

5. Useful for orchards
Ring and basin arrangements can direct water toward the desired root zone.

6. Can accommodate different field sizes
Basins can be designed according to field and crop conditions.

7. Simple infrastructure
The basic system can require relatively simple structures.

20. Disadvantages of Basin Irrigation
1. Land levelling requirement
Uneven basins can produce uneven water distribution.

2. Bund construction and maintenance
Bunds occupy some field area and require maintenance.

3. Waterlogging risk
Excessive application on poorly drained soils can saturate the root zone.

4. Deep percolation
Long ponding can move water below the effective root zone.

## 5. Runoff/overflow
Poorly controlled application can exceed basin storage.

6. Soil erosion
Excessive inflow can disturb soil.

## 7. Labor
Traditional systems may require manual opening and closing of inlets.

8. Not ideal for steep land
Water can become difficult to control on steep slopes.

21. Basin Irrigation and Waterlogging
This is a particularly important consideration.

Excess irrigation can cause:
Soil Saturation↑→AFP↓→O2↓→Root Stress↑ 
Recall:
AFP=n−θv 
where:
- nn = total soil porosity,
- θv  v = volumetric water content.
As soil becomes saturated:
AFP→0AFP 0
and soil aeration decreases.

22. Basin Irrigation and Soil Aeration
Prolonged ponding can reduce oxygen diffusion through soil.
Therefore:
Excessive Ponding→O2 Supply↓→Root Respiration↓ 
This can negatively affect crops that are sensitive to prolonged waterlogging.

23. Basin Irrigation and Drainage
Good drainage is essential where soils or climate create waterlogging risk.

Irrigation
Water→Basin 

Drainage
Excess Water→Outlet 
Therefore:
Basin Irrigation+Adequate Drainage   
is much safer than simply retaining water without an outlet strategy.

24. Basin Irrigation and Salinity
Repeated irrigation can introduce salts through irrigation water.
If water evaporates and drainage is poor:
Evaporation→Salt Concentration→Salt Accumulation 
Therefore saline conditions require attention to:
- irrigation-water quality,
- soil EC,
- drainage,
- leaching requirements,
- crop salt tolerance.

25. Basin Irrigation and Irrigation Scheduling
Basin irrigation still requires proper scheduling.
The important questions are:

When should the basin be irrigated?
Consider:
- soil moisture,
- crop stage,
- ET,
- rainfall,
- root depth.

How much water should be applied?
Consider:
- root-zone deficit,
- soil water-holding capacity,
- effective rainfall,
- application efficiency.
Thus:

Basin Irrigation+Irrigation Scheduling 
must be considered together.

26. Soil-Water Balance
The general field water balance is:
ΔS=P+I+CR−R−D−ET 
where:
- PP = precipitation,
- II = irrigation,
- CRCR = capillary rise,
- RR = runoff,
- DD = deep drainage,
- ETET = evapotranspiration.
Basin irrigation contributes to:
II
while poor management may increase:
R+DR+D

27. Available Soil Water
Available water:
AWC=θFC−θPWP -   
Root-zone storage:
TAW=(θFC−θPWP)Zr - )Z r  
Readily available water:
RAW=p×TAW 
Irrigation can be triggered as soil-water depletion approaches the selected threshold.

28. Basin Irrigation and Evapotranspiration
Crop water consumption is:

ET=E+T 
and crop ET can be estimated as:
ETc=KcET0 
where:
- ET0ET 0 = reference evapotranspiration,
- KcK c = crop coefficient.
Therefore irrigation scheduling can use ET to estimate how quickly the basin's stored root-zone water
is being depleted.

29. Basin Irrigation and Critical Moisture
Stages
Water management should pay special attention to stages such as:
- germination,
- seedling establishment,
- flowering,
- pollination,
- fruit set,
- pod formation,
- grain development,
- grain filling,
- seed filling.
The principle is:
Water Stress During Sensitive Stage→Potentially Large Yield Loss 
But excessive water can also be harmful.

30. Basin Irrigation and Field Levelling
The relationship is particularly strong:

Land Levelling→Uniform Basin Elevation→Uniform Water Distribution 
Poor levelling:
Uneven Surface→Uneven Water Depth→Uneven Infiltration

Thus:
Basin Irrigation is a More Controlled Form of Surface Irrigation   
compared with uncontrolled/free flooding.

33. Basin Irrigation vs Border Irrigation
Basin
Water is contained within a relatively enclosed area.

Border
Water moves down a long, relatively narrow strip between borders.
Basin=Enclosed Compartment   
Border=Long Strip   

34. Basin Irrigation vs Drip Irrigation
Feature

Basin
Drip
Delivery
Surface
Localized emitters
Pressure
Usually gravity Pressurized
Wetted area
Broad
Localized
Infrastructure
Relatively simple More complex
Water control
Moderate
High
Filtration
Less critical
Important
Automation
Possible
Highly adaptable
Evaporation/runoff potential Generally higher Often lower

35. Basin Irrigation vs Sprinkler
Basin
Water→Surface→Soil 

Sprinkler
Pressurized Water→Spray→Soil/Crop 
Sprinklers can apply water without requiring the field to be divided into enclosed basins.

36. Basin Irrigation and Water-Use Efficiency
Water-use efficiency:
WUE=YieldWater Used  Water\ Used   
Good basin management attempts to minimize:

- runoff,
- unnecessary evaporation,
- deep percolation,
- excessive ponding.
The goal is:
Maximum Useful Root−Zone Water 
rather than maximum surface ponding.

37. Basin Irrigation and Nutrient Loss
Excess water can increase nutrient movement.
For example:
Excess Irrigation→Deep Percolation→Nutrient Leaching Risk 
This is particularly important for mobile nutrients such as nitrate.
Therefore irrigation and fertilizer management should be coordinated.

38. Basin Irrigation and Soil Erosion
High inflow rates can disturb soil.
Excessive Flow→Soil Detachment→Erosion 
Controlled inlet flow and stable bunds can reduce this risk.

39. Basin Irrigation and Crop Layout
Crop arrangement should be compatible with basin dimensions.
For orchards:
Tree Spacing↔Basin Size 
For field crops:
Crop Rows↔Basin Dimensions 
The basin should allow water to reach the intended root zone without excessive waste.

40. Basin Irrigation and Mechanization
Large numbers of bunds can interfere with:
- tractor movement,
- planting,
- harvesting,
- machinery turning.
Therefore basin design should consider machinery requirements.
There is often a trade-off:
More Small Basins→Better Control+More Field Obstacles 

41. Basin Irrigation and Orchards
For orchards, basin irrigation can be arranged around trees.
As root systems expand:
Tree Growth→Root Zone Expansion→Basin Modification 
The water application area should correspond reasonably to the active root zone rather than always
being concentrated immediately at the trunk.

42. Improving Basin-Irrigation Efficiency
1. Proper land levelling
Improves water distribution.

2. Correct basin size
Avoid excessively large or small compartments.

3. Controlled inlet flow
Prevents erosion and overflow.

4. Appropriate irrigation depth
Avoid excessive ponding.

5. Soil-moisture-based scheduling
Apply water according to actual depletion.

6. Good bund maintenance
Prevents leakage.

7. Adequate drainage
Prevents prolonged waterlogging.

8. Appropriate crop layout
Ensures water reaches the effective root zone.

43. Practical Basin-Irrigation Workflow
1. Assess Crop 
↓
2. Determine Root Zone 
↓
3. Assess Soil 
↓
4. Level Field 
↓
5. Construct Basin Bunds 
↓
6. Provide Inlet 
↓

↓
8. Apply Controlled Water 
↓
9. Allow Infiltration 
↓
10. Stop Before Excessive Ponding/Runoff 

↓
11. Monitor Drainage and Crop Response 

44. Common Problems and Causes
Problem
Possible cause
Uneven wetting
Poor land levelling
Excess ponding
Slow infiltration/excess water

Waterlogging
Excessive irrigation/poor drainage
Bund leakage
Damaged or poorly constructed bund
Overflow

Erosion
High inflow velocity
Deep percolation
Excessive irrigation duration
Uneven crop growth
Uneven water distribution
Salt accumulation
Saline water + evaporation + poor drainage

45. Common Mistakes
Mistake 1 — Thinking basin irrigation means permanent flooding
It does not.
Basin Irrigation≠Permanent Submergence 

Mistake 2 — Ignoring land levelling
Uneven basins produce uneven irrigation.

Mistake 3 — Making basins unnecessarily large
Large basins can make uniform water distribution more difficult.

Mistake 4 — Excessive water application
More water does not automatically mean more yield.

Mistake 5 — Ignoring drainage
Ponding plus poor drainage can cause root-zone oxygen deficiency.

Mistake 6 — Ignoring crop stage
Water requirements and sensitivity change during crop development.

Water volume

with consistent units.

47. Complete Conceptual Model
The complete basin-irrigation process can be remembered as:

48. The Most Important Comparison
Think of surface irrigation geometrically:
Flood=Broad Surface =   
Basin=Enclosed Area =   
Border=Long Strip =   
Furrow=Linear Channel =   
This makes the differences much easier to remember.

49. Exam-Ready Definition
Basin irrigation is a surface-irrigation method in which agricultural land is divided
into relatively level, enclosed compartments called basins using bunds or small
embankments. Water is introduced into each basin and allowed to spread over the soil
surface under gravity, where it infiltrates into the soil and replenishes the crop root
zone. Proper land levelling, basin dimensions, controlled water inflow, soil infiltration,
irrigation scheduling and drainage are essential for efficient operation.

50. Final One-Line Memory
Basin Irrigation = Divide the field into level enclosed areas → fill each basin with water → allow water
to infiltrate into the root zone.   

Remember:
Basin = Area   Furrow = Channel  
Border = Long Strip  
Flood = Broad Surface  
And the central principle:
Good Basin Irrigation = Proper Levelling + Controlled Inflow + Adequate Root-Zone Infiltration + Pro
per Scheduling + Good Drainage   

## Module 11 — Water & Irrigation
## Management
11. Sprinkler Irrigation
## 1. Introduction
Sprinkler irrigation is a pressurized irrigation method in which water is conveyed through pipes
under pressure and discharged through sprinkler nozzles so that it falls on the field in the form of
droplets, similar to rainfall.
The basic principle is:
Water Source→Pump/Pressure→Pipes→Sprinkler Nozzle→Water Droplets→Soil/Crop Root Zone xed          
Unlike surface irrigation methods such as flood, basin and furrow irrigation, sprinkler irrigation does
not require water to flow across the entire field surface by gravity.

## 2. Definition
Sprinkler irrigation is a pressurized irrigation method in which water is conveyed
through a pipe network and discharged through sprinkler devices as droplets over the
crop and soil surface, simulating natural rainfall.

One-line definition
Sprinkler Irrigation = Applying pressurized water to crops as artificial rainfall.  

3. Basic Principle
The fundamental principle is:
Pressure Energy→Water Velocity→Nozzle→Droplets→Rain-like Application       
Water is pressurized and forced through a small opening in the sprinkler nozzle.
The nozzle converts the pressurized water stream into droplets.

4. Simple Diagram
Sprinkler

💧
💧 💧 💧
💧 💧 💧 💧
│
│
┌───┴───┐
│ Nozzle│
└───┬───┘
│
│ Riser
│
════════════════════╪══════════════════
Main/Submain Pipe
│
│
Pump
│
Water Source

5. How Sprinkler Irrigation Works
Step 1 — Water source
Water is obtained from:
- well,
- borewell,
- reservoir,
- canal,
- tank,
- surface-water source.

Step 2 — Pumping
A pump may provide the required pressure.

Step 3 — Water enters the pipe network
Water travels through:
- mainline,
- submain,
- laterals.

Step 4 — Water reaches sprinklers
Water enters sprinkler devices through risers or directly through the lateral.

Step 5 — Nozzle breaks the water stream
The nozzle produces droplets.

Step 6 — Droplets fall on the field
Water is distributed over the target area.

Step 7 — Water infiltrates
Water enters the soil and becomes available to roots.
Sprinkler→Soil→Root Zone→Plant Uptake      

6. Major Components
A sprinkler system generally contains:
1. water source,
2. pump,
3. suction pipe,
4. mainline,
5. submain,
6. laterals,
7. risers,
8. sprinkler heads,
9. nozzles,
10.valves,
11.pressure regulator/control devices,
12.filters where needed,
13.fittings and connectors.
Not every system contains exactly the same components.

7. Water Source
The source must provide adequate:
- quantity,
- quality,
- reliability,
- pressure/flow after pumping.
Water quality matters because poor-quality water can cause:
- emitter/nozzle problems,
- salt accumulation,
- leaf injury in some crops,
- soil salinity/sodicity.

## 8. Pump
The pump provides the pressure needed to move water through the system and operate sprinklers.
Conceptually:
Pump→Pressure→Water Movement→Sprinkler Operation 
Pump selection depends on:
- required discharge,
- required pressure/head,
- pipe losses,
- elevation,
- sprinkler operating requirements.

## 9. Mainline
The mainline transports water from the pump/source toward the irrigation area.
Source/Pump→Mainline   
It is usually the major conveyance pipe.

## 10. Submain
The submain distributes water from the mainline toward different field sections.
Mainline→Submain→Laterals    

## 11. Laterals
Laterals are smaller pipes connected to the submain.
Sprinklers are installed along these pipes.
Submain
════════════════════════════
│

│

│

│

│

│

Lateral Lateral Lateral

💧💧

💧💧

💧💧

## 12. Riser
A riser is a vertical pipe or support that connects the lateral to the sprinkler head.
Lateral→Riser→Sprinkler     
The height of the sprinkler affects its spray pattern and interaction with the crop canopy.

13. Sprinkler Head
The sprinkler head distributes water over a defined area.
It can:
- rotate,
- remain fixed,
- distribute water in a circular pattern,
- distribute water in a partial-circle pattern.

## 14. Nozzle
The nozzle is the opening through which water exits.
Nozzle characteristics affect:
- discharge,
- droplet size,
- spray radius,
- application rate.
Conceptually:
Nozzle Size+Pressure→Discharge+Spray Pattern 

15. Sprinkler Pressure
Adequate operating pressure is required for the sprinkler to produce its intended distribution pattern.
If pressure is too low:
Poor Spray Pattern→Poor Uniformity 
If pressure is too high:
- excessive energy use,
- misting,
- increased evaporation/drift,
- potentially undesirable droplet size.
Therefore:
Correct Pressure→Proper Spray Pattern    

16. Sprinkler Discharge
Discharge is the volume of water delivered per unit time.
Common units include:
- L/s,
- L/min,
- m³/h.
Conceptually:
Q=Vt  t   
where:
- QQ = discharge,
- VV = volume,
- tt = time.

17. Application Rate
Application rate describes how quickly water is applied to the land area.

Application Rate=Water AppliedArea×Time 
 Area Time   
It is commonly expressed in:
- mm/h,
- cm/h.
The sprinkler application rate should generally be compatible with the soil's infiltration capacity.
If:
Application Rate>Infiltration RateApplication\ Rate>Infiltration\ Rate
then:
Runoff/ponding risk↑ 

18. Sprinkler Spacing
Sprinklers must be spaced appropriately to provide adequate overlap.
A sprinkler's coverage pattern is not perfectly uniform.
Therefore:
Sprinkler Spacing→Pattern Overlap→Uniformity 
Insufficient overlap can create dry patches.

19. Why Overlap Is Important
A sprinkler often applies more water near certain portions of its spray pattern and less near other
portions.
Therefore several sprinklers are arranged so their patterns overlap.

💧💧💧
💧 ○ 💧
💧 💧 💧
↘↙
Overlapping
patterns

Thus:
Overlap→More Uniform Application 

20. Types of Sprinkler Irrigation
Sprinkler systems can be classified in several ways.

A. Based on mobility
1. Portable system
Components can be moved from one location to another.

2. Semi-permanent system
Some components remain fixed while others can be moved.

3. Permanent/fixed system
The network remains installed in the field.

21. Based on Method of Operation
1. Conventional sprinkler
Uses standard sprinkler heads mounted on laterals.

2. Rain-gun system
Uses high-discharge sprinklers capable of covering relatively large areas.

3. Center pivot
A long sprinkler-equipped system rotates around a central pivot.

4. Linear move
The sprinkler system moves laterally across the field.

## 5. Micro-sprinkler
Provides relatively localized sprinkler application at lower discharge.

22. Conventional Sprinkler
A conventional system generally consists of:

Pump→Mainline→Laterals→Sprinklers 
It can be arranged in different field layouts depending on crop and terrain.

23. Rain-Gun Irrigation
A rain gun is a large sprinkler capable of applying water over a comparatively large area.
It is useful in some:
- field crops,
- fodder crops,
- plantations,
- irregularly shaped areas.
However, high discharge and pressure requirements can increase energy consumption.

24. Center Pivot Irrigation
A center-pivot system rotates around a central point.
Conceptually:
Sprinkler Line
│
│
│
●───────
Pivot

The system moves around the pivot and irrigates a roughly circular area.
Center Pivot=Rotating Sprinkler System Around a Central Pivot   
Advantages include automation and relatively uniform application when properly designed.

25. Linear-Move System
A linear-move system moves across the field while sprinklers apply water.

Movement Across Field+Sprinkler Application  +
   
It can be suitable for relatively large, appropriately shaped fields.

## 26. Micro-Sprinkler
Micro-sprinklers operate at relatively low discharge and provide localized wetting.
They are used in some:
- orchards,
- nurseries,
- horticultural crops.
Compared with conventional sprinklers:
Micro-sprinkler→More Localized Wetting sprinkler  More\ Localized\
Wetting  

27. Advantages of Sprinkler Irrigation
1. Suitable for uneven terrain
Unlike many surface-irrigation systems, sprinkler irrigation can operate on land that is not perfectly
level, although pressure and design must account for elevation differences.

2. Uniform water application
Properly designed systems can provide relatively uniform distribution.

3. Reduced need for land levelling
Extensive precision levelling is generally less important than in basin irrigation.

4. Suitable for sandy soils
It can apply smaller amounts of water at controlled rates, which can be useful where infiltration is
rapid.

5. Efficient water application
Well-designed systems can reduce some runoff and conveyance losses.

6. Suitable for irregular fields
Pipe networks can be designed for many field shapes.

7. Fertilizer application possible
Some systems allow fertigation.

## 8. Automation
Valves, sensors and controllers can automate operation.

28. Disadvantages of Sprinkler Irrigation
1. Initial cost
Pipes, pump, sprinklers and control systems require investment.

2. Energy requirement
Pressurization requires energy.
Pressure→Energy Requirement 

3. Wind sensitivity
Strong wind can distort the spray pattern.

4. Evaporation losses
Droplets travel through the atmosphere before reaching the soil.

5. Foliage wetting
Water may wet leaves and flowers, potentially increasing disease risk in some conditions.

6. Nozzle clogging
Poor water quality can affect system performance.

7. Pressure management
Incorrect pressure can reduce uniformity.

## 8. Maintenance
Pipes, valves, sprinklers, nozzles and pumps require maintenance.

29. Sprinkler Irrigation and Wind
Wind is a major environmental factor.
Wind↑→Spray Drift↑→Uniformity↓ 
Strong wind can cause:
- water drift,
- uneven distribution,
- increased evaporation,
- reduced effective application.
Therefore sprinkler operation is often better during periods of relatively lower wind, where practical.

30. Sprinkler Irrigation and Evaporation
Water droplets are exposed to atmospheric conditions.
Therefore:
Droplet Exposure→Evaporation Loss 
Losses can increase with:
- high temperature,
- low relative humidity,
- high wind,
- small droplets,
- long flight distance.
This is one reason operating pressure and nozzle selection matter.

31. Sprinkler Irrigation and Humidity
Relative humidity influences atmospheric drying demand.
Recall:
VPD=es−ea 
High VPD means stronger atmospheric demand for water vapor.

Therefore:
VPD↑→Evaporative Demand↑ 
Sprinkler droplet losses can therefore increase under hot, dry and windy conditions.

32. Sprinkler Irrigation and Soil Infiltration
A critical design principle is:
Application Rate≤Suitable Infiltration Capacity 
If application exceeds infiltration:
Application Rate>Infiltration→Ponding+Runoff 
This can waste water and cause erosion.

33. Sprinkler Irrigation and Soil Erosion
Excessive application rate or large droplets can disturb the soil surface.
High Intensity/Impact→Soil Detachment→Erosion 
So nozzle selection, pressure and application rate should be appropriate.

34. Sprinkler Irrigation and Soil Texture
Sandy soils
- rapid infiltration,
- low water-holding capacity.
Sprinklers can help apply smaller irrigation depths more frequently.

Clay soils
- slower infiltration,
- runoff/ponding can occur if application rate is too high.

Loam
Generally intermediate behavior.

Therefore:
Sprinkler Application Rate↔Soil Infiltration 

35. Sprinkler Irrigation and Waterlogging
Although sprinkler irrigation generally avoids prolonged broad surface flooding, excessive irrigation
can still cause waterlogging.
Excess Water→Soil Saturation→AFP↓→O2↓ 
Therefore sprinkler irrigation does not eliminate the need for drainage.

36. Sprinkler Irrigation and Crop Canopy
Unlike furrow irrigation, sprinklers can wet the canopy.
This can be useful in some circumstances, but it can also increase disease risk for susceptible crops
when leaf wetness remains prolonged.
Therefore:
Canopy Wetting→Disease Environment May Change 
Crop-specific disease management is important.

37. Sprinkler Irrigation and Frost Protection
In some agricultural systems, carefully managed sprinkler application can be used for frost protection.
The principle is related to the latent heat released during freezing.
Water Freezing→Latent Heat Release→Tissue Temperature Protection 
However, frost-protection sprinkling requires continuous and correctly managed application under
appropriate conditions. Poorly managed application can worsen injury.

38. Sprinkler Irrigation and Cooling
Water applied to foliage and soil can affect crop temperature.
Evaporation consumes latent heat:

Evaporation→Latent Heat Loss→Cooling 
This can be relevant under heat stress, but the benefit depends on:
- crop,
- weather,
- water availability,
- timing,
- humidity,
- wind.

39. Sprinkler Irrigation and Fertigation
Some sprinkler systems can apply soluble fertilizers through irrigation water.
This is called fertigation.
Conceptually:
Fertilizer+Irrigation Water→Root Zone 
It requires appropriate fertilizer solubility, water quality and system management.

40. Sprinkler Irrigation and Irrigation
Scheduling
Sprinklers still require proper irrigation scheduling.
The decision depends on:
- crop water requirement,
- ET,
- rainfall,
- soil moisture,
- root depth,
- critical moisture stages.
Crop ET:
ETc=KcET0 

Water balance:
ΔS=P+I+CR−R−D−ET 
The sprinkler system determines how water is applied; scheduling determines when and how much.

41. Sprinkler Irrigation and Critical Moisture
Stages
Water should receive particular attention during moisture-sensitive stages such as:
- germination,
- seedling establishment,
- flowering,
- pollination,
- fruit set,
- pod formation,
- grain formation,
- grain filling,
- seed filling.
Therefore:
Crop Stage+Root-Zone Moisture+ET→Irrigation Decision + +    

42. Application Efficiency
Application efficiency can be expressed as:
Ea=Water stored in effective root zoneWater applied×100        100  
Higher efficiency means a larger proportion of applied water becomes useful root-zone storage.
Losses can occur through:
- evaporation,
- wind drift,
- runoff,

- deep percolation,
- leakage.

43. Distribution Uniformity
A sprinkler system should distribute water uniformly.
If one portion receives much more water than another:
Uniformity↓ 
Then:
- some plants may experience water stress,
- other areas may receive excess water.
Therefore system design should consider:
- sprinkler spacing,
- pressure,
- nozzle size,
- pipe diameter,
- elevation,
- wind,
- operating time.

44. Pressure and Elevation
Elevation differences affect pressure.
Generally:
Elevation Difference→Pressure Difference 
Therefore hilly terrain requires careful hydraulic design.
Pressure regulators and appropriately designed pipe networks can help maintain acceptable operating
conditions.

45. Pipe Friction Loss
Water loses pressure as it flows through pipes.
Conceptually:
Pipe Length↑→Friction Loss↑→Pressure Available↓ 
Pipe diameter, flow rate, pipe material and length affect friction losses.

46. Hydraulic Head
Pump requirements are related to total head.
Conceptually:
Total Head=Elevation Head+Pressure Head+Friction Losses+Other System Losses 
The actual hydraulic design should use appropriate engineering equations and manufacturer data.

47. Energy Requirement
Because sprinkler systems operate under pressure:
Pressure Requirement→Pump Requirement→Energy Consumption 
Higher pressure generally requires more pumping energy.
Therefore the system should be designed for adequate—not unnecessarily excessive—pressure.

48. Sprinkler Irrigation and Water-Use
Efficiency
Water-use efficiency:
WUE=YieldWater Used  Water\ Used   
A properly designed sprinkler system can improve water management by controlling:
- application amount,
- application timing,

- spatial distribution,
- irrigation frequency.
But sprinkler irrigation is not automatically water-efficient. Poor design or operation can still cause
major losses.

49. Sprinkler Irrigation and Water Quality
Important water-quality parameters include:
- salinity/EC,
- sodium,
- chloride,
- bicarbonate,
- suspended solids,
- biological contaminants where relevant.
Poor water quality can cause:
Salinity↑→Osmotic Stress↑ 
and/or:
Poor Water Quality→Nozzle/Equipment Problems 

## 50. Maintenance
Important maintenance operations include:

Pipes
Check for:
- leakage,
- cracks,
- loose connections.

Sprinklers
Check:
- rotation,
- nozzle condition,

- pressure,
- spray pattern.

Filters
Clean when required.

Pump
Monitor:
- pressure,
- discharge,
- abnormal vibration/noise,
- energy consumption.

System
Check:
Pressure+Discharge+Uniformity 
periodically.

56. When Sprinkler Irrigation Can Be Useful
It may be particularly useful when:
- water needs controlled application,
- field is irregular,
- land levelling is difficult or expensive,
- soil infiltration is relatively high,
- surface irrigation is difficult,
- portable/flexible irrigation is needed,
- automation is desired,
- fertigation is planned,
- suitable energy and water pressure are available.
But actual suitability is site-specific.

57. When Sprinkler Irrigation May Be Less
Suitable
Potential limitations include:
- very windy conditions,
- high pumping-energy cost,
- severe water-quality problems,
- crops highly sensitive to leaf wetting,
- inadequate water pressure,
- poor-quality equipment,
- applications where surface irrigation is much cheaper and suitable.

58. Sprinkler Irrigation and Zero/Minimum
Tillage
Sprinkler systems can be used with:
- conventional tillage,
- minimum tillage,
- conservation tillage,
- zero tillage.
The irrigation method and tillage method are different dimensions.
Sprinkler Irrigation≠Tillage System   

59. Sprinkler Irrigation and Raised Beds
Sprinklers can irrigate crops planted on:
- flat beds,
- raised beds,
- ridges,
depending on crop and system.
Unlike furrow irrigation, water does not need to be transported through furrows.

60. Sprinkler Irrigation — Complete Process
Water Source   
↓
Pump   
↓

↓

↓

Submain   
↓

↓

↓

↓

↓

↓
Plant Uptake   

61. Core Design Relationship
A sprinkler system must balance:
Pressure+Discharge+Nozzle+Spacing+Overlap+Application Rate+Wind+Soil Infiltration  +   +   +   +   +   +   +    
These determine how uniformly and efficiently water reaches the root zone.

62. Practical Decision Framework
Before installing a sprinkler system, evaluate:

Water
- quantity,
- quality,

- source reliability.

Soil
- texture,
- infiltration,
- water-holding capacity,
- salinity.

Field
- size,
- shape,
- slope,
- elevation variation.

Crop
- root depth,
- row spacing,
- canopy,
- disease sensitivity,
- water requirement.

Climate
- wind,
- temperature,
- humidity,
- rainfall.

Economics
- pump cost,
- pipe cost,
- sprinkler cost,
- energy,
- labor,
- maintenance.

Therefore:
Suitability=f(Water+Soil+Field+Crop+Climate+Energy+Economics) 

63. Common Misconceptions

❌ “Sprinkler irrigation means no water loss.”
False.
There can be:
- evaporation,
- drift,
- runoff,
- deep percolation,
- leakage.

❌ “Higher pressure always improves irrigation.”
False.
Excessive pressure can create undesirable spray and increase energy consumption.

❌ “Sprinklers work equally well in strong wind.”
False.
Wind can significantly alter spray distribution.

❌ “Sprinkler irrigation eliminates drainage requirements.”
False.
Excess irrigation can still cause waterlogging.

❌ “Sprinkler irrigation is always more efficient than surface irrigation.”
False.
Efficiency depends on design, operation, soil, climate and management.

64. Exam-Ready Definition
Sprinkler irrigation is a pressurized irrigation method in which water is pumped or
conveyed under pressure through a network of mainlines, submains and laterals and
discharged through sprinkler heads and nozzles in the form of droplets over the crop

and soil surface, thereby simulating rainfall. Its effectiveness depends on pressure,
discharge, nozzle characteristics, sprinkler spacing and overlap, application rate, soil
infiltration, wind conditions, crop requirements and irrigation scheduling.

65. Final One-Line Memory
Sprinkler Irrigation = Pressurized water → nozzle → droplets → artificial rainfall → root-zone water.
   

Remember the four major surface methods:
Flood = Broad Surface Water  
Basin = Enclosed Area  
Furrow = Channel Between/Along Rows  
Sprinkler = Pressurized Artificial Rainfall  
And the central principle:
Good Sprinkler Irrigation=Correct Pressure+Correct Nozzle+Proper Spacing/Overlap+Suitable Applica
tion Rate+Proper Scheduling  =   +
  +   +   +
   

## Module 11 — Water & Irrigation
## Management
12. Drip Irrigation
## 1. Introduction
Drip irrigation is a localized, pressurized irrigation method in which water is delivered slowly and
directly to the soil near the plant root zone through small outlets called emitters/drippers.
Instead of wetting the entire field, drip irrigation aims to apply water to the active root zone in small,
controlled quantities.
The basic principle is:
Water Source→Pump→Filter→Mainline→Submain→Drip Lateral→Emitter→Root Zone          

One-line memory
Drip Irrigation = Slow, controlled, localized water delivery directly to the root zone.
  

## 2. Definition
Drip irrigation is a pressurized irrigation system in which water is applied slowly and
frequently through emitters installed on or along small-diameter lateral pipes,
delivering water directly or near directly to the crop root zone.

3. Basic Principle
The fundamental idea is:
Small Amount of Water+Controlled Delivery+Root-Zone Placement  +   +    
Water exits the emitter at a controlled discharge rate.
For example, an emitter might be designed to discharge a few litres per hour, depending on the system.
The exact emitter discharge is system-specific.

4. How Drip Irrigation Works
Step 1 — Water source
Water comes from:
- borewell,
- well,
- reservoir,
- canal,
- tank,
- other suitable source.

Step 2 — Pumping
A pump supplies the pressure required by the system.

Step 3 — Filtration
Water passes through filters to remove particles that could clog emitters.

Step 4 — Mainline
Water travels through the main pipe.

Step 5 — Submain
Water is distributed to different field sections.

Step 6 — Laterals
Small pipes carry water along crop rows.

Step 7 — Emitters
Emitters release water at controlled rates.

Step 8 — Root-zone infiltration
Water enters the soil and forms a localized wetted zone.
Emitter→Wetted Soil Volume→Roots     

5. Basic Diagram
