The dots represent emitters.

6. Major Components
A typical drip system contains:
1. water source,
2. pump,
3. filter unit,
4. fertigation unit where applicable,
5. pressure regulator/control valve,
6. mainline,
7. submain,
8. laterals,
9. emitters/drippers,
10.valves,
11.air-release/vacuum-relief devices where needed,
12.flush/end valves,
13.connectors and fittings.

7. Water Source
The water source must provide adequate:
- quantity,
- quality,
- reliability,
- pressure/flow after pumping.
Water quality is particularly important because emitters have small flow passages.

## 8. Pump
The pump supplies the pressure required to move water through:
- filters,
- pipes,
- valves,
- emitters.
Conceptually:
Pump→Pressure→Controlled Emitter Flow 
The required pump capacity depends on the system design.

## 9. Filtration
Filtration is one of the most important parts of drip irrigation.
Because emitter passages can be small:
Suspended Particles→Emitter Clogging 
Filters may include:
- screen filters,
- disc filters,
- media/sand filters,
- combinations of filtration stages.
Filter selection depends on water quality and system requirements.

## 10. Mainline
The mainline carries water from the pump/filter assembly to the irrigation zones.
Pump/Filter→Mainline    

## 11. Submain
The submain distributes water from the mainline to individual field sections.

Mainline→Submain→Laterals     

12. Drip Laterals
A lateral is a small-diameter pipe placed along or near crop rows.
Emitters are installed:
- inside the pipe,
- on the pipe,
- or integrated into the pipe,
depending on the system.
Lateral+Emitters=Localized Water Delivery Line  +   =
   

13. Emitters / Drippers
The emitter is the device that controls water discharge from the lateral.
Its important characteristics include:
- discharge rate,
- pressure response,
- spacing,
- clogging resistance,
- manufacturing uniformity.
Conceptually:
Pressure→Emitter→Controlled Discharge     

14. Emitter Discharge
Emitter discharge is commonly expressed in:
- L/h,
- L/min.
For example, if one emitter delivers 22 L/h:

2 L/h per emitter   
The actual required discharge depends on crop, soil, spacing and system design.

15. Number of Emitters
The number of emitters depends on:
- lateral length,
- emitter spacing,
- number of laterals,
- crop spacing,
- irrigation design.
Conceptually:
Total Flow=Number of Emitters×Emitter Discharge 
If there are 500 emitters and each delivers 2 L/h:
500×2=1000 L/h500 2=1000 
- r:
1 m3/h  3/  

16. Wetted Soil Zone
One of the defining characteristics of drip irrigation is the localized wetted zone.
Water released by the emitter moves:
- downward,
- sideways.
Plant

🌱

│
● ← emitter
\ ↓ /
\
\

↓

/

↓

/

┌───────────┐

│ Wetted

│

│ root zone │
└───────────┘

The exact shape depends on:
- soil texture,
- structure,
- emitter discharge,
- irrigation duration,
- soil moisture,
- layering.

17. Soil Texture and Wetted Pattern
Sandy soil
Water tends to move more rapidly downward.
Vertical Movement relatively strong 

Clay soil
Lateral movement can be relatively greater.
Lateral Spread relatively important 

Loam
Often shows intermediate behavior.
Therefore:
Wetted Pattern=f(Soil+Emitter+Time+Water) 

18. Drip Irrigation and Root Zone
The purpose is not to wet the entire field.
Instead:
Water→Active Root Zone    
This can reduce unnecessary wetting between crop rows.
However, the wetted volume must be adequate for the crop's root system.

19. Wetting Percentage
In drip irrigation, only part of the soil surface/root-zone volume may be intentionally wetted.
This can be described using concepts such as percentage of wetted area or percentage of wetted soil
volume, depending on the design and calculation method.
Therefore:
Localized Wetting≠Entire Field Wetting   

20. Drip Irrigation Scheduling
Drip irrigation is often operated with:
- relatively frequent irrigation,
- smaller application events,
- close monitoring of soil moisture and crop demand.
The objective is to keep root-zone moisture within an appropriate range.
ET+Soil Moisture+Crop Stage→Irrigation Decision 
But drip does not mean irrigation must always be daily. Frequency depends on crop, soil, weather and
system design.

21. Crop Evapotranspiration
Crop water demand can be estimated as:
ETc=KcET0 
where:
- ETcET c = crop evapotranspiration,
- KcK c = crop coefficient,
- ET0ET 0 = reference evapotranspiration.
Irrigation scheduling then considers rainfall, soil storage, application efficiency and crop stage.

22. Soil-Water Balance
The general water balance is:
ΔS=P+I+CR−R−D−ET 
where:
- PP = precipitation,
- II = irrigation,
- CRCR = capillary rise,
- RR = runoff,
- DD = deep drainage,
- ETET = evapotranspiration.
Drip irrigation attempts to make the irrigation component II closely match crop/root-zone
requirements.

23. Available Soil Water
Available water is approximately:
AWC=θFC−θPWP -   
Root-zone total available water:
TAW=(θFC−θPWP)Zr - )Z r  
Readily available water:
RAW=p×TAW 
where pp is the allowable depletion fraction under the chosen crop/system conditions.

24. Drip Irrigation and Water-Use Efficiency
Water-use efficiency can be expressed as:
WUE=YieldWater Used  Water\ Used   
Drip systems can improve water management by:
- reducing unnecessary wetting,
- reducing runoff in suitable soils,

- reducing some evaporation from non-wetted areas,
- delivering water close to roots,
- enabling precise scheduling.
However:
Drip Irrigation≠Automatically 100% Efficient 
Poor design or management can still cause:
- deep percolation,
- leakage,
- clogging,
- uneven distribution,
- over-irrigation.

25. Application Efficiency
Application efficiency can be expressed as:
Ea=Water stored in effective root zoneWater applied×100        100  
Good drip management aims to maximize useful root-zone storage while minimizing losses.

## 26. Fertigation
One of the major advantages of drip systems is their ability to support fertigation—application of
soluble nutrients through irrigation water.
Conceptually:
Fertilizer+Irrigation Water→Drip Emitter→Root Zone 
This can allow nutrients to be supplied:
- in smaller doses,
- at selected growth stages,
- close to the active root zone.

27. Fertigation Benefits
Potential benefits include:
- precise nutrient delivery,
- reduced application labor,
- improved synchronization with crop demand,
- reduced unnecessary nutrient application,
- easier split applications.
But fertigation requires appropriate:
- fertilizer solubility,
- compatibility,
- filtration,
- injection equipment,
- water quality,
- flushing procedures.

28. Drip Irrigation and Nitrogen
Nitrogen is relatively mobile in many soils.
Poor irrigation management can cause:
Excess Water→Deep Percolation→Nitrate Leaching 
Drip fertigation can allow smaller, more controlled applications, potentially reducing losses when
properly managed.

29. Drip Irrigation and Salinity
Drip irrigation does not automatically eliminate salinity.
Because only part of the soil is wetted, salts can accumulate toward the outer boundary of the wetted
zone as water is removed through crop uptake and evaporation.
Conceptually:
Salt Movement→Edge of Wetted Zone 

Under saline conditions, irrigation design must account for:
- irrigation-water EC,
- soil EC,
- leaching requirement,
- drainage,
- root distribution,
- crop salt tolerance.

## 30. Leaching
When salts accumulate, additional water may be required to move salts below the root zone, provided:
- suitable water is available,
- drainage is adequate.
Leaching Water→Salt Movement Downward 
Without drainage:
Leaching without Drainage→Salt Accumulation Elsewhere 
Therefore:
Salinity Management=Water Quality+Leaching+Drainage+Crop Management 

31. Drip Irrigation and Evaporation
Because the soil surface is only partly wetted:
Non-wetted Surface→Evaporation Potential↓ wetted\ Surface  Evaporation\ Potential  
compared with broad surface wetting under otherwise comparable conditions.
But evaporation from the wetted zone still occurs.

32. Drip Irrigation and Runoff
Because water is applied slowly:

Application Rate↓→Runoff Potential↓ 
provided the application rate remains compatible with infiltration.
However, runoff can still occur on:
- steep slopes,
- poorly infiltrating soils,
- excessively high application rates,
- improperly designed systems.

33. Drip Irrigation and Waterlogging
Drip irrigation can reduce broad surface ponding, but excessive application can still saturate the root
zone.
Excess Drip Application→Root Zone Saturation→AFP↓→O2↓ 
Therefore scheduling remains essential.

34. Drip Irrigation and Soil Aeration
Good drip management can maintain an appropriate balance between water and air in the root zone.
Recall:
AFP=n−θv 
If water content becomes too high:
θv↑→AFP↓  v  AFP Therefore excessive irrigation can reduce soil aeration.

35. Drip Irrigation and Fertilizer Placement
Because water is localized, nutrients delivered through fertigation also tend to concentrate in the wetted
root-zone region.
This creates:
Water Distribution↔Nutrient Distribution↔Root Distribution 

Therefore irrigation and nutrient placement should be designed together.

36. Types of Drip Irrigation
1. Surface drip irrigation
Laterals are placed on or near the soil surface.
Lateral→Surface 

Advantages
- easy inspection,
- easy maintenance,
- relatively simple installation.

Limitations
- physical damage,
- sunlight/UV exposure,
- interference with field operations,
- rodents/animals in some situations.

37. Subsurface Drip Irrigation — SDI
In subsurface drip irrigation, laterals are installed below the soil surface.
Lateral→Below Soil Surface 
Water is delivered directly into the subsurface root zone.

Potential advantages
- reduced surface evaporation,
- less interference with field operations,
- protected laterals,
- localized water application.

Challenges
- installation cost,
- clogging detection,

- root intrusion,
- maintenance,
- difficult repair/location,
- specialized design.

38. Inline Drippers
Emitters are manufactured as part of the lateral at regular intervals.
Pipe+Integrated Emitters +   
Useful for crops with regular row arrangements.

39. Online Drippers
Individual emitters are attached to the lateral separately.
This can provide flexibility in emitter placement.

40. Pressure-Compensating Emitters
Pressure-compensating emitters are designed to maintain relatively stable discharge over a specified
- perating-pressure range.
They can be particularly useful where pressure varies along the lateral or across terrain.
Conceptually:
Pressure Variation→Smaller Discharge Variation 
within the emitter's designed operating range.

41. Non-Pressure-Compensating Emitters
Their discharge varies more directly with pressure.
A conceptual relationship is:
q=KPx 
where:
- qq = emitter discharge,

- KK = emitter coefficient,
- PP = pressure,
- xx = emitter flow exponent.
For many emitter types, xx is less than 1.
The actual relationship depends on emitter design.

42. Pressure Regulation
Pressure regulation is important because:
Pressure→Emitter Discharge→Water Distribution 
Too much pressure can cause:
- excessive discharge,
- leakage,
- component damage,
- energy waste.
Too little pressure can cause:
- inadequate emitter discharge,
- poor distribution,
- insufficient irrigation.

43. Drip Lateral Length
Long laterals create pressure losses due to:
- friction,
- elevation,
- flow reduction along the pipe.
As water exits through emitters, flow inside the lateral decreases along its length.
Therefore:
Lateral Design=Length+Diameter+Emitter Spacing+Pressure 

44. Pipe Friction
As water moves through pipes:
Flow→Friction Loss→Pressure Loss 
Longer/smaller pipes and higher flow rates can increase friction losses.
Proper hydraulic design is therefore essential.

45. Drip Irrigation and Elevation
Elevation changes can produce pressure differences.
Elevation Difference→Pressure Difference 
This can lead to unequal emitter discharge in non-pressure-compensating systems.
Pressure-compensating emitters can reduce—but not eliminate—all hydraulic variation within their
- perating range.

46. Filtration and Clogging
Emitter clogging is one of the major drip-irrigation problems.

Physical clogging
Caused by:
- sand,
- silt,
- suspended particles.

Chemical clogging
Can result from:
- precipitates,
- mineral deposits,
- incompatible fertilizer mixtures.

Biological clogging
Can result from:

- algae,
- bacteria,
- organic growth.
Therefore:
Water Quality+Filtration+Flushing→Clogging Control 

## 47. Flushing
Laterals and pipelines may need periodic flushing.
The purpose is to remove:
- accumulated sediments,
- particles,
- deposits.
Conceptually:
Flush→Sediment Removal→Emitter Performance 

48. Chemical Treatment
Depending on water quality and system design, chemical treatment may be used to manage:
- biological growth,
- mineral precipitation.
Treatment must be based on proper diagnosis and compatible system procedures.

49. Root Intrusion
In some systems, roots may enter emitters or subsurface laterals.
This can cause:
Root Intrusion→Emitter Blockage 
Appropriate design, emitter selection and management are important.

50. Drip Irrigation and Weed Management
Because the spaces between rows may remain relatively dry:
Non-wetted Area→Weed Germination Opportunity↓ wetted\ Area  Weed\ Germination\ Opportunity  
However, this does not mean drip irrigation eliminates weeds.
Weeds can still grow in:
- wetted zones,
- rainfall-wetted areas,
- areas receiving runoff.

51. Drip Irrigation and Disease
Drip irrigation generally avoids widespread wetting of the crop canopy.
Therefore, compared with overhead sprinkler irrigation:
Leaf Wetting→Often Lower   
This can reduce favorable leaf-wetness conditions for some diseases.
But soil-borne and root diseases can still occur, especially if the root zone remains excessively wet.

52. Drip Irrigation and Greenhouse Cultivation
Drip irrigation is widely compatible with protected cultivation.
It can be used with:
- greenhouse crops,
- polyhouse crops,
- nursery plants,
- container crops,
- high-value horticultural crops.
It can also be combined with fertigation.

53. Drip Irrigation in Orchards
Drip systems are particularly useful for trees because emitters can be positioned around the active root
zone.
As the tree grows:
Root Zone Expansion→Emitter Arrangement May Need Adjustment 
Multiple emitters may be used per tree depending on tree size and irrigation design.

54. Drip Irrigation and Plant Spacing
Emitter spacing should be coordinated with crop spacing.
Plant Spacing+Emitter Spacing+Soil Wetting 
For closely spaced crops, emitters may be installed at relatively regular intervals.
For widely spaced trees, emitters may be positioned around each tree.

55. Drip Irrigation and Critical Moisture Stages
Water should receive special attention during:
- establishment,
- flowering,
- pollination,
- fruit set,
- pod formation,
- grain formation,
- grain filling,
- seed filling.
The system provides the delivery mechanism:
Drip System 
while scheduling determines:
When+How Much 

56. Advantages of Drip Irrigation
1. Highly localized water application
Water is delivered near the root zone.

2. High control
Water can be applied in small, controlled amounts.

3. Reduced unnecessary wetting
Large areas between rows may remain relatively dry.

4. Reduced evaporation potential
Compared with broad surface wetting, less soil surface is exposed as a wetted surface.

5. Reduced runoff potential
Low application rates can reduce runoff where properly designed.

## 6. Fertigation
Nutrients can be delivered through the irrigation system.

7. Suitable for uneven land
Pressure-controlled systems can work on slopes with appropriate hydraulic design.

8. Reduced foliage wetting
Compared with overhead sprinklers.

## 9. Automation
Sensors, valves and controllers can automate irrigation.

10. Suitable for horticulture
Particularly useful for many orchard and high-value crop systems.

57. Disadvantages of Drip Irrigation
1. High initial cost
Requires:

- pipes,
- emitters,
- filters,
- valves,
- pump/control equipment.

## 2. Clogging
Emitters require clean water and proper filtration.

## 3. Maintenance
Regular inspection and flushing may be required.

4. Technical knowledge
Hydraulic design and fertigation require management skills.

## 5. Damage
Laterals can be damaged by:
- machinery,
- animals,
- rodents,
- field operations.

6. Salt accumulation
Localized wetting can alter salt distribution.

7. Root intrusion
Especially relevant to some subsurface systems.

8. Dependence on pressure
Pump and hydraulic problems can affect uniformity.

9. Limited wetting area
Incorrect emitter placement can leave portions of the root zone too dry.

61. Drip vs Flood Irrigation
Flood=Broad Surface Wetting    Drip=Localized RootZone Wetting   
Drip provides much greater control over the location and rate of water delivery.

62. Drip Irrigation and Water Saving
The potential water-saving pathway is:
Localized Application→Unnecessary Wetting↓→Evaporation/Runoff Potential↓→Water Productivity
Potential↑ 
But actual water savings depend on management.
If a farmer simply applies excessive water through drip:
Drip+Over-irrigation→Water Waste   Water\ Waste  

63. Drip Irrigation and Water Productivity
Water productivity can be represented as:
WP=Crop YieldWater Consumed  Water\ Consumed   
Drip irrigation can improve water productivity when it allows better matching of irrigation with crop
demand.

64. Drip Irrigation and Soil Moisture
Monitoring
Useful monitoring methods include:
- soil moisture sensors,
- tensiometers,
- capacitance/FDR/TDR sensors,
- gravimetric measurements,

- crop observations,
- ET-based calculations.
The principle is:
Measure→Interpret→Schedule→Irrigate 

65. Sensor-Based Drip Irrigation
A modern system can operate using:
Soil Moisture+Weather+ET+Crop Stage→Controller→Valve→Drip Irrigation 
This is a foundation of automated/precision irrigation.

## 66. Automation
Automation may include:
- solenoid valves,
- timers,
- soil moisture sensors,
- flow meters,
- pressure sensors,
- weather stations,
- controllers,
- remote monitoring.
This allows:
Scheduled/Measured Demand→Automatic Irrigation 

67. Drip Irrigation and Precision Agriculture
Drip irrigation is naturally compatible with precision agriculture because water can be controlled
spatially and temporally.
Precision Irrigation=Right Place+Right Time+Right Amount 

This can be further combined with:
- sensors,
- GIS,
- remote sensing,
- weather data,
- variable-rate management,
- automated valves.

68. Practical Design Factors
A drip system should be designed based on:

Crop
- water requirement,
- root depth,
- plant spacing,
- growth stage.

Soil
- texture,
- infiltration,
- water-holding capacity,
- salinity.

Water
- quantity,
- EC,
- pH,
- suspended solids,
- biological/chemical clogging potential.

Field
- area,
- slope,

- elevation,
- shape.

System
- emitter discharge,
- emitter spacing,
- lateral length,
- pipe diameter,
- operating pressure.
Therefore:
Drip Design=f(Crop+Soil+Water+Field+Hydraulics+Climate) 

69. Irrigation Duration
A simple conceptual relationship is:
Water Applied=Emitter Discharge×Number of Emitters×Time 
Therefore:
Time=Required WaterTotal Emitter Discharge   Total\ Emitter\
Discharge   
The actual calculation must account for system efficiency and the desired root-zone water
replenishment.

## 70. Example
Suppose:
- 100 emitters,
- each emitter = 2 L/h.
Then:
Q=100×2Q=100 2 Q=200 L/hQ=200 
If the system operates for 3 hours:
V=200×3V=200 3 V=600 L  

Thus 600 L is delivered by those emitters during the 3-hour operation, assuming their discharge
remains as specified.

71. Drip Irrigation and Root-Zone Deficit
If the root zone has a water deficit DD, irrigation should replace an appropriate fraction of that deficit.
Conceptually:
D=(θFC−θcurrent)Zr - )Z r  
Then:
Irrigation Requirement≈Desired Root Zone ReplenishmentApplication Efficiency  Application\ Efficiency   
Actual irrigation calculations may additionally include rainfall, system losses and leaching
requirements.

72. Drip Irrigation and Critical Moisture Stages
The system is particularly useful when irrigation needs to be carefully controlled during:
Establishment→Flowering→Fruit/Pod Set→Grain/Seed Filling 
    
Remember:
Critical Stage≠Maximum Water Requirement

74. Maintenance Checklist
Daily/regular checks
- pump pressure,
- system pressure,
- flow rate,
- visible leaks,
- emitter performance,
- filter condition.

Periodic checks
- filter cleaning,
- lateral flushing,
- pressure measurements,
- emitter uniformity,
- pipe damage,
- fertilizer-injection equipment.

Seasonal checks
- replace damaged laterals,
- inspect valves,
- inspect pump,
- check filtration system,
- review irrigation schedule,
- inspect salt distribution.

75. Important Water-Quality Parameters
For drip irrigation, monitor:
EC+pH+Suspended Solids+Chemical Precipitation Risk+Biological Load + +   
Because water quality affects both:
1. crop/soil health, and

2. system performance.

76. Drip Irrigation and Soil Salinity
Remember the distinction:
Salinity=Soluble Salt Problem   
Sodicity=Exchangeable Sodium/Soil Structure Problem   
Drip irrigation can be used in saline environments, but water quality, leaching and drainage must be
properly managed.

79. Most Important Differences Between
Irrigation Methods
Method
Main idea
Flood
Water spreads broadly across field

Method
Main idea
Basin
Water is contained in enclosed basins
Furrow Water flows through channels
Sprinkler Pressurized water falls as droplets
Drip
Pressurized water is released slowly near roots

Easy memory:
Flood = Surface   Basin = Area  
Furrow = Channel   Sprinkler = Rain   Drip = Root Zone  

80. Common Misconceptions

❌ “Drip irrigation means watering every day.”
Not necessarily.
Frequency depends on:
- soil,
- crop,
- weather,
- root zone,
- ET,
- irrigation system.

❌ “Drip irrigation has no water loss.”
False.
Losses can occur through:
- evaporation,
- deep drainage,
- leakage,
- runoff,
- inefficient scheduling.

❌ “Drip eliminates weeds.”
False.
It can reduce wetting of inter-row areas, but weeds can still grow.

❌ “Drip eliminates salinity.”
False.
Salinity can still occur and salt distribution may become localized.

❌ “Drip needs no maintenance.”
False.

Filtration, flushing, pressure checks and emitter inspection are important.

❌ “More frequent irrigation is always better.”
False.
Over-irrigation can cause:

Waterlogging+Deep Drainage+Nutrient Loss 

81. Practical Decision Framework
Before installing drip irrigation, evaluate:

## 1. Crop
- water requirement,
- root depth,
- spacing,
- economic value.

## 2. Soil
- texture,
- infiltration,
- water-holding capacity,
- salinity,
- drainage.

## 3. Water
- availability,
- EC,
- pH,
- sediment,

- clogging potential.

## 4. Field
- size,
- slope,
- elevation,
- shape.

## 5. Economics
- installation cost,
- pumping energy,
- maintenance,
- replacement cost,
- expected benefits.
Therefore:
Suitability=f(Crop+Soil+Water+Field+Climate+Economics) 

82. Exam-Ready Definition
Drip irrigation is a pressurized, localized irrigation method in which water is
conveyed through a network of mainlines, submains and laterals and discharged
slowly through emitters or drippers at controlled rates near the crop root zone. It
allows precise control of irrigation timing and quantity, reduces unnecessary wetting
- f the field surface and can be integrated with fertigation. Its effectiveness depends on
emitter characteristics, pressure, filtration, water quality, soil properties, crop root
distribution, lateral design and irrigation scheduling.

83. Final Conceptual Summary
The complete concept can be remembered as:
Clean Water→Pressure→Filtration→Pipes→Emitter→Localized Wetting→Root Uptake         
The key design relationship is:

Emitter Discharge+Emitter Spacing+Pressure+Soil Texture+Root Distribution+Irrigation Duration→R
- ot-Zone Water Distribution  +   +
  +   +   +  
  
And the most important principle:
Good Drip Irrigation = Right Amount + Right Place + Right Time + Right Pressure   

Final one-line memory
Drip Irrigation = Deliver water slowly and precisely near the crop roots through emitters.
   

## Module 11 — Water & Irrigation
## Management
Micro-Irrigation
## 1. Introduction
Micro-irrigation is a method of applying water to crops in small quantities, at low rates, and in a
controlled manner, usually directly to or near the crop root zone.
It is a broader category that includes irrigation systems designed for localized and precise water
application, especially:
- Drip irrigation
- Micro-sprinkler irrigation
- Bubbler irrigation
- Other localized low-discharge systems
The central idea is:
Small Amount of Water+Controlled Application+Localized Root-Zone Wetting  +   +    

One-line memory
Micro-Irrigation = Small, controlled and localized application of irrigation water.  

## 2. Definition
Micro-irrigation is an irrigation method in which water is applied at relatively low
discharge rates and in a controlled, localized manner to the soil surface or root zone of
individual plants or small areas.
It is commonly used to improve control over where, when and how much water is supplied.

3. Micro-Irrigation Is a Broad Category
This is extremely important:
Micro-Irrigation⊃Drip Irrigation    
That means drip irrigation is one type of micro-irrigation, not the other way around.
A simplified classification is:
## Micro-Irrigation
│
┌────────────────┼────────────────┐
│

│

│

Drip

Micro-sprinkler

Bubbler

│
┌────┴─────┐
Surface

Subsurface

drip

drip

Exact classifications vary among irrigation systems and standards.

4. Why Is It Called "Micro"?
The word micro refers primarily to the small and controlled rate of water application and localized
delivery.
Instead of:
Large area+large flow + 
micro-irrigation uses:
Small discharge+Localized area+Controlled delivery  +   +    
It does not mean that the irrigation system itself must be physically tiny.

5. Basic Principle
The fundamental principle is:
Water Source→Pressure/Control→Distribution Network→Micro-Emitter→Localized Wetting→Root
Zone        
The objective is to match water application more closely with crop water demand and root distribution.

6. Micro-Irrigation vs Conventional Irrigation
Conventional surface irrigation
Water may be distributed over a large portion of the field.
Examples:
- flood,
- basin,
- border,
- furrow.

Micro-irrigation
Water is applied more locally.
Examples:
- drip,
- micro-sprinkler,
- bubbler.
Therefore:
Surface irrigation→Broad field distribution     Micro-irrigation→Localized distribution 
  

7. Major Types of Micro-Irrigation
1. Drip irrigation
Water is released through emitters near the plant.
Emitter→Root Zone   
This is the most commonly recognized form of micro-irrigation.

2. Micro-sprinkler irrigation
Water is sprayed over a relatively small area using low-discharge sprinkler devices.
Unlike conventional sprinklers, micro-sprinklers generally provide more localized application.
Useful in some:
- orchards,
- nurseries,
- horticultural systems.

3. Bubbler irrigation
Water is discharged from a bubbler at a relatively higher localized flow than typical drip emitters and is
directed around individual plants.
It is particularly applicable to some:
- trees,
- shrubs,
- landscape plants,
- widely spaced crops.

9. Main Components of a Micro-Irrigation
System
A typical pressurized micro-irrigation system contains:
1. water source,
2. pump,
3. filtration unit,
4. fertigation unit,
5. pressure regulator,
6. mainline,
7. submain,
8. laterals,
9. emitters/micro-sprinklers/bubblers,
10.valves,
11.air-release devices where required,
12.flushing arrangements,
13.connectors and fittings,
14.control system.

10. Basic System Layout
## Water Source
│
Pump

│
Filter
│
Pressure Regulation
│
Mainline
│
Submain
┌──────┼──────┐
│

│

│

Lateral Lateral Lateral
│

│

Plants

Plants

│

💧💧 💧💧 💧💧
Plants

11. Water Source
Possible sources include:
- wells,
- borewells,
- reservoirs,
- tanks,
- canals,
- harvested rainwater,
- other suitable surface-water sources.
The source must provide adequate:
Quantity+Quality+Reliability + +   

## 12. Pump
A pump provides the pressure necessary for water movement through:
- filters,
- pipes,

- valves,
- emitters.
Therefore:
Pump→Pressure→Controlled Application 

## 13. Filtration
Filtration is particularly important because micro-irrigation outlets can have relatively small passages.
Without adequate filtration:
Sediment→Clogging→Uneven Irrigation 
Common filtration systems include:
- screen filters,
- disc filters,
- media filters.
The appropriate filter depends on water quality and system requirements.

## 14. Mainline
The mainline transports water from the source/pump/filter assembly toward the field.
Source→Pump→Filter→Mainline 

## 15. Submain
The submain distributes water to different irrigation zones.
Mainline→Submain→Laterals 

## 16. Laterals
Laterals are smaller pipes that carry water toward the individual plants or crop rows.
Different micro-irrigation devices can be attached to them.

## 17. Emitters
An emitter controls water discharge from a drip lateral.
Its characteristics include:
- discharge,
- spacing,
- pressure response,
- uniformity,
- clogging resistance.
Conceptually:
Pressure→Emitter→Controlled Flow 

## 18. Micro-Sprinkler
A micro-sprinkler distributes water over a relatively small area through a spray pattern.
Compared with drip:
Micro-sprinkler→Wider Localized Wetting sprinkler  Wider\
Localized\ Wetting  
It can therefore be useful where a larger portion of the root zone needs wetting.

## 19. Bubbler
A bubbler supplies water locally around an individual plant.
It is particularly useful where plants are:
- widely spaced,
- individually managed,
- relatively large.
For example, some orchard systems can use localized bubblers.

20. Root-Zone Wetting
The main objective is:

Water→Active Root Zone    
rather than:
Water→Entire Field Surface  The required wetted area depends on:
- crop,
- root architecture,
- soil texture,
- plant age,
- emitter type,
- irrigation duration.

21. Wetted Soil Pattern
Water movement from a micro-irrigation outlet is affected by:
- gravity,
- capillary forces,
- soil texture,
- soil structure,
- initial moisture,
- discharge rate,
- application duration.
Conceptually:
Wetted Pattern=f(Soil+Emitter+Flow+Time) 

22. Sandy vs Clay Soil
Sandy soil
Water tends to move relatively rapidly downward.
Therefore irrigation may need:
- smaller applications,
- more frequent irrigation,

- careful monitoring.

Clay soil
Water movement can be slower, with greater potential for lateral spreading.
Therefore excessive application can cause:
- ponding,
- saturation,
- poor aeration.

Loam
Generally intermediate behavior.

23. Irrigation Scheduling
Micro-irrigation does not eliminate the need for scheduling.
The basic decision remains:
When to Irrigate?+How Much to Apply?  +    
Important inputs include:
- crop ET,
- rainfall,
- soil moisture,
- root-zone depth,
- crop stage,
- weather,
- irrigation efficiency.

24. Crop Evapotranspiration
Crop water requirement can be estimated using:
ETc=KcET0 
where:
- ETcET c = crop evapotranspiration,

- KcK c = crop coefficient,
- ET0ET 0 = reference evapotranspiration.

25. Water Balance
A general soil-water balance is:
ΔS=P+I+CR−R−D−ET 
where:
- PP = precipitation,
- II = irrigation,
- CRCR = capillary rise,
- RR = runoff,
- DD = deep drainage,
- ETET = evapotranspiration.
Micro-irrigation provides greater control over II.

26. Soil Available Water
Approximate available water:
AWC=θFC−θPWP -   
Root-zone total available water:
TAW=(θFC−θPWP)Zr - )Z r  
Readily available water:
RAW=p×TAW 
This provides a framework for deciding when irrigation should begin.

27. Frequent Irrigation
Micro-irrigation systems are often compatible with frequent irrigation events, particularly where the
soil has low water-holding capacity or crop demand changes rapidly.
But:

Frequent irrigation≠automatic requirement   
A sandy soil and a clay soil may require different schedules.

## 28. Fertigation
Micro-irrigation is highly compatible with fertigation.
Fertilizer→Irrigation Water→Micro-Emitter→Root Zone Emitter  Root\ Zone  
This allows nutrients to be supplied close to where roots are actively taking them up.

29. Advantages of Fertigation
Potential benefits include:
- precise nutrient delivery,
- split nutrient application,
- reduced labor,
- synchronization with crop demand,
- improved nutrient-use management.
But fertilizer must be:
- sufficiently soluble,
- compatible with water chemistry,
- compatible with the equipment.

30. Micro-Irrigation and Water Saving
The potential water-saving mechanism is:
Localized Application→Unnecessary Wetting↓→Evaporation/Runoff Potential↓ 
However, the actual saving depends on:
- irrigation scheduling,
- system design,

- soil,
- crop,
- climate,
- maintenance.
Therefore:
Micro-irrigation≠zero water loss irrigation  

31. Water-Use Efficiency
A simple expression is:
WUE=YieldWater Used  Water\ Used   
Micro-irrigation can improve water productivity when irrigation is better matched to crop demand.

32. Application Efficiency
Application efficiency can be expressed as:
Ea=Water stored in effective root zoneWater applied×100        100  
The objective is to maximize useful root-zone water storage while minimizing:
- runoff,
- evaporation,
- deep percolation,
- leakage.

33. Micro-Irrigation and Runoff
Because water is applied at low rates:
Application Rate↓→Runoff Potential↓ 
provided the application rate is compatible with soil infiltration.

34. Micro-Irrigation and Evaporation
Only part of the soil surface may be wetted.
Therefore:
Wetted Surface Area↓→Potential Soil Evaporation↓ 
This is one reason localized irrigation can reduce evaporative losses compared with broad surface
wetting.

35. Micro-Irrigation and Deep Percolation
If too much water is applied:
Excess Irrigation→Deep Percolation→Water/Nutrient Loss 
Therefore precise irrigation requires not just low discharge but appropriate irrigation duration and
scheduling.

36. Micro-Irrigation and Waterlogging
Micro-irrigation can reduce broad surface ponding, but over-irrigation can still saturate the root zone.
Excess Water→Soil Saturation→AFP↓→O2↓ 
Recall:
AFP=n−θv 
where:
- nn = total porosity,
- θv  v = volumetric water content.

37. Micro-Irrigation and Soil Aeration
Good irrigation management maintains a balance between:
- water,
- air,

- root respiration.
If soil becomes saturated:
Water Content↑→Air-Filled Pores↓→Root Oxygen↓ Filled\ Pores  Root\ Oxygen  

38. Micro-Irrigation and Salinity
Micro-irrigation can be used in saline conditions, but salinity management must be carefully designed.
As plants remove water:
Water Uptake→Salt Concentration in Remaining Soil Water 
Salts may accumulate toward the edges of the wetted zone.
Therefore saline irrigation requires consideration of:
- irrigation-water EC,
- soil EC,
- crop salt tolerance,
- leaching,
- drainage.

39. Leaching Under Micro-Irrigation
Where necessary and feasible:
Leaching+Adequate Drainage→Salt Movement Below Root Zone 
Without adequate drainage, leaching may simply move salts elsewhere.

40. Micro-Irrigation and Weeds
Because only selected areas are wetted:
Inter-row Wetting↓→Some Weed Growth Opportunity↓ row\ Wetting  Some\ Weed\ Growth\ Opportunity  
But micro-irrigation does not eliminate weeds.
Weeds can grow around emitters and in naturally rainfall-wetted areas.

41. Micro-Irrigation and Diseases
Drip irrigation generally avoids extensive leaf wetting.
Therefore, compared with overhead irrigation:
Leaf Wetness Often↓ 
This may reduce favorable conditions for some foliar diseases.
Micro-sprinklers, however, can wet foliage depending on their design and operation.

42. Micro-Irrigation and Root Development
Roots tend to proliferate where water and nutrients are available.
Therefore:
Water/Nutrients→Localized Root Activity 
If the wetted zone is too small:
Root Zone Restriction→Reduced Effective Root Exploration 
Thus the wetted volume should be appropriate for the crop.

43. Micro-Irrigation and Plant Spacing
Emitter arrangement must correspond with crop geometry.
Plant Spacing+Emitter Spacing+Wetted Zone 

Closely spaced crops
Regular lateral/emitter arrangement may be suitable.

Widely spaced trees
Multiple emitters, micro-sprinklers or bubblers may be used around the tree's root zone.

44. Micro-Irrigation in Orchards
It is particularly compatible with many orchard systems because individual trees can be managed
separately.

🌳

💧 💧
💧
💧 💧
The irrigation system can be expanded or modified as the tree canopy and root system develop.

45. Micro-Irrigation in Vegetables
Micro-irrigation can be used with many vegetable crops.
Advantages include:
- controlled root-zone moisture,
- compatibility with plastic mulch,
- fertigation,
- reduced inter-row wetting.

46. Micro-Irrigation in Protected Cultivation
Micro-irrigation is highly compatible with:
- greenhouse,
- polyhouse,
- nursery,
- container production,
- high-value horticulture.
A typical arrangement is:
Drip+Fertigation+Protected Cultivation 

47. Micro-Irrigation and Mulching
Micro-irrigation and mulch can complement each other.

Micro-irrigation+Mulch→Evaporation Reduction+Root Zone Moisture Conservation irrigation + Mulch  Evaporation\ Reduction + Root\ Zone\ Moisture\ Conservation  
Mulch may also suppress weeds and moderate soil temperature.

48. Pressure Management
Pressure affects micro-emitter discharge.
Conceptually:
Pressure→Emitter Flow→Irrigation Uniformity 
Too low:
Pressure↓→Discharge↓Pressure  Discharge Too high:
Pressure↑→Excess Flow/Stress on ComponentsPressure  Excess\ Flow/Stress\ on\
Components
Therefore pressure regulation is important.

49. Pressure-Compensating Emitters
Pressure-compensating emitters are designed to maintain relatively consistent discharge over a
specified pressure range.
They can be useful where:
- laterals are long,
- terrain varies,
- pressure differences occur.
But they have operating limits and do not eliminate all hydraulic variation.

## 50. Clogging
One of the biggest technical challenges is clogging.

Physical clogging
- sand,
- silt,

- suspended particles.

Chemical clogging
- mineral precipitation,
- fertilizer precipitation.

Biological clogging
- algae,
- bacteria,
- organic growth.
The basic pathway is:
Poor Water Quality→Clogging→Emitter Discharge↓→Uneven Irrigation 

51. Clogging Prevention
Important measures include:
Good Filtration+Water Treatment Where Appropriate+Flushing+Correct Fertigation 

## 52. Flushing
Pipes and laterals may need periodic flushing to remove accumulated material.
Flushing→Sediment/Deposit Removal→Emitter Performance 

53. Micro-Irrigation and Automation
Modern systems can use:
- soil-moisture sensors,
- flow meters,
- pressure sensors,
- weather stations,
- timers,
- solenoid valves,

- controllers.
The system can therefore operate using:
Soil Moisture+Weather+ET+Crop Stage→Controller→Valve→Irrigation 

54. Precision Irrigation
Micro-irrigation can form the physical infrastructure for precision irrigation.
The goal is:
Right Amount+Right Place+Right Time  +   +
   
This can improve water management when combined with accurate sensing and scheduling.

55. Micro-Irrigation and Critical Moisture
Stages
Water management is especially important during crop-sensitive stages such as:
- germination,
- establishment,
- flowering,
- pollination,
- fruit set,
- pod formation,
- grain formation,
- grain filling,
- seed filling.
The principle is:
Crop Stage+Root Zone Moisture+ET→Irrigation Decision 

56. Advantages of Micro-Irrigation
1. Localized application
Water is delivered near the crop.

2. Precise control
Flow can be controlled at emitter/device level.

3. Potential water savings
Unnecessary wetting can be reduced.

4. Reduced runoff potential
Low application rates can reduce runoff.

## 5. Fertigation
Nutrients can be delivered with irrigation water.

6. Reduced inter-row wetting
Potentially reduces evaporation and some weed pressure.

7. Reduced foliage wetting with drip
Useful for some disease-management situations.

## 8. Automation
Easy to integrate with sensors and controllers.

9. Suitable for uneven terrain
With proper hydraulic design.

10. Suitable for horticulture
Especially useful for orchards and high-value crops.

57. Disadvantages of Micro-Irrigation
1. Initial investment
Requires pipes, filters, valves and application devices.

## 2. Clogging
Small outlets are vulnerable to blockage.

## 3. Maintenance
Regular inspection is necessary.

4. Technical requirements
Correct hydraulic and irrigation management is important.

5. Water-quality sensitivity
Poor-quality water can cause physical, chemical or biological problems.

6. Damage to laterals
Can occur through:
- machinery,
- rodents,
- animals,
- field operations.

7. Localized salt accumulation
May occur if saline water is poorly managed.

8. Root-zone limitation
If the wetted zone is too small, plants may not access enough soil volume.

9. Dependence on equipment
Pump, filter or pressure failures can affect the crop.

62. Micro-Irrigation vs Flood
Flood=Broad Surface Application   
Micro−Irrigation=Localized Controlled Application   
The key difference is the degree of control over water placement and discharge.

63. Micro-Irrigation and Water Productivity
A useful conceptual chain is:
Better Scheduling+Localized Application→Water Losses↓→Root Zone Water Management↑→Water P
roductivity Potential↑ 
But actual results depend on crop, soil, climate and management.

64. Practical Design Factors
Before designing a micro-irrigation system, evaluate:

Crop
- crop water requirement,
- plant spacing,
- root depth,
- growth stage.

Soil
- texture,
- infiltration,
- water-holding capacity,
- salinity,
- drainage.

Water
- quantity,

- EC,
- pH,
- suspended solids,
- clogging potential.

Field
- area,
- slope,
- elevation,
- shape.

System
- emitter discharge,
- spacing,
- pressure,
- pipe diameter,
- lateral length,
- filtration.

Economics
- installation cost,
- energy,
- maintenance,
- replacement.
Therefore:
Micro-Irrigation Design=f(Crop+Soil+Water+Field+Hydraulics+Climate+Economics)
 Irrigation\ Design =
f(Crop+Soil+Water+Field+Hydraulics+Climate+Economics)  

65. Irrigation Duration
A simple conceptual equation for drip-type systems is:
V=q×N×t 
where:

- VV = water delivered,
- qq = discharge per emitter,
- NN = number of emitters,
- tt = operating time.
Therefore:
t=VqN  qN   
Actual irrigation scheduling should account for efficiency, rainfall, soil storage and the desired level of
root-zone replenishment.

## 66. Example
Suppose:
- 200 emitters,
- emitter discharge = 2 L/h,
- operation = 2 hours.
Then:
V=2×200×2V=2 200 2 V=800 L  
So the system delivers approximately 800 L during that operating period if the stated emitter discharge
is maintained.

67. Micro-Irrigation Maintenance Cycle
A useful maintenance cycle is:
Inspect→Filter→Measure Pressure→Check Flow→Flush→Repair→Recheck 
       

68. Common Problems

69. Common Misconceptions

❌ “Micro-irrigation means drip irrigation only.”
False.
Drip is one major type of micro-irrigation.

❌ “Micro-irrigation means no water loss.”
False.
There can still be:
- evaporation,
- deep percolation,
- leakage,
- runoff.

❌ “Micro-irrigation means daily irrigation.”
Not necessarily.
Frequency depends on crop, soil, weather and scheduling.

❌ “Micro-irrigation eliminates salinity.”
False.
Salt accumulation can still occur.

❌ “Micro-irrigation requires no filtration.”
False.
Filtration is especially important for systems with small outlets.

❌ “Low discharge means over-irrigation is impossible.”
False.

Even a low-flow emitter can cause excessive irrigation if operated too long.

A modern system can combine three technologies:

Micro-Irrigation+Fertigation+Automation  +   +
   
For example:
Sensor→Controller→Valve→Water+Nutrients→Root Zone 
This forms the basis of precision irrigation and precision fertigation.

73. Exam-Ready Definition
Micro-irrigation is a controlled irrigation method in which water is applied at
relatively low discharge rates in a localized manner to the soil surface or root zone of
individual plants or small areas. It includes systems such as drip irrigation, microsprinklers and bubblers. The system generally uses a pressurized distribution
network, filtration, pressure regulation and localized application devices, and can be
integrated with fertigation and automation.

74. Final Conceptual Summary
The entire concept can be remembered as:
Micro-Irrigation=Low Rate+Localized Application+Controlled Delivery  =   +   +    
Its major pathway is:
Water→Filter→Pressure Control→Micro-Device→Root Zone       
Its major benefits come from:
Localized Water+Precise Scheduling+Fertigation+Automation 
And the most important distinction to remember:
Micro-Irrigation is the category; Drip Irrigation is one type within that category.   

Final one-line memory
Micro-Irrigation = Delivering small amounts of water precisely and locally where the crop needs it.
  

## Module 11 — Water & Irrigation
## Management
Water Conservation
## 1. Introduction
Water conservation in agriculture means the planned and efficient management of available water so
that water losses are minimized, soil moisture is retained, crop water requirements are met
efficiently, and water resources remain available for future use.
The objective is not simply to use less water.
The real objective is:
Produce More or Adequate Crop Yield with Less Unnecessary Water Loss   
A complete agricultural water-conservation approach combines:
- rainwater harvesting,
- soil-moisture conservation,
- efficient irrigation,
- proper irrigation scheduling,
- reduction of runoff,
- reduction of evaporation,
- reduction of deep percolation,
- groundwater recharge,
- watershed management,
- suitable crops and varieties,
- improved soil health,
- mulching and residue management,
- water-quality management.

One-line memory
Water Conservation = Capture + Store + Retain + Use Efficiently + Reuse + Recharge  

## 2. Definition
Water conservation is the planned management and efficient use of water resources to
reduce unnecessary losses, improve water availability and productivity, conserve soil
moisture, and maintain water resources for sustainable agricultural production.

3. Why Water Conservation Is Important in
Agriculture
Agriculture requires water for:
- seed germination,
- plant establishment,
- photosynthesis,
- nutrient transport,
- cell expansion,
- flowering,
- pollination,
- fruit/pod/grain formation,
- grain and seed filling,
- cooling through transpiration.
The basic pathway is:
Water→Root Uptake→Plant Processes→Growth→Yield 
But not all water supplied to a field becomes useful crop water.
Water can be lost through:
ET+Runoff+Deep Percolation+Evaporation+Leakage 
Therefore water conservation attempts to reduce avoidable losses.

4. Water Conservation Is Not Simply "Using
Less Water"
This is an important distinction.
Suppose a farmer applies too little water:
Water Application↓→Crop Stress↑→Yield↓Water\ Application  Crop\
Stress  Yield That is not necessarily good water conservation.
Instead:
Appropriate Water+Efficient Application+Correct Timing→Higher Water Productivity 
The goal is efficient water use, not crop-damaging under-irrigation.

5. Water Conservation Framework
A useful framework is:
Capture→Store→Conserve→Apply Efficiently→Use by Crop→Reuse/
Recharge        

Capture
Collect rainfall and runoff.

Store
Store water in:
- farm ponds,
- tanks,
- reservoirs,
- soil profile.

Conserve
Reduce:
- evaporation,
- runoff,

- erosion,
- unnecessary drainage.

Apply efficiently
Use:
- drip,
- sprinkler,
- appropriate surface irrigation,
- proper scheduling.

Use by crop
Maintain adequate root-zone moisture.

Reuse/recharge
Reuse suitable water and recharge groundwater where appropriate.

6. Agricultural Water Balance
A simplified water balance is:
ΔS=P+I+CR−R−D−ET 
where:
- ΔS S = change in soil-water storage,
- PP = precipitation,
- II = irrigation,
- CRCR = capillary rise,
- RR = runoff,
- DD = deep drainage,
- ETET = evapotranspiration.
Water conservation aims to increase the proportion of available water that contributes usefully to the
crop-water balance while avoiding unnecessary losses.

7. Major Components of Water Loss
## 1. Evaporation
Water changes from liquid to vapor.
Soil/Water Surface→Atmosphere 

## 2. Transpiration
Water is lost through plant tissues, primarily stomata.
Root→Plant→Atmosphere 
Evaporation + transpiration:
ET=E+T 

## 3. Runoff
Water flows over the soil surface instead of infiltrating.
Rainfall/irrigation>Infiltration Capacity→Runoff 

4. Deep Percolation
Water moves below the effective root zone.
Excess Water→Below Root Zone 
It may contribute to groundwater recharge in appropriate settings, but can also represent an agricultural
water loss and cause nutrient leaching.

## 5. Leakage
Water can be lost through:
- canals,
- pipes,
- tanks,
- irrigation infrastructure.

8. Main Water-Conservation Strategies
Water conservation can be divided into:

A. Soil-based conservation
- mulching,
- residue retention,
- conservation tillage,
- improved soil structure,
- organic matter management,
- contour farming.

B. Water-harvesting strategies
- farm ponds,
- check dams,
- contour trenches,
- field bunds,
- water harvesting structures.

C. Irrigation efficiency
- drip,
- sprinkler,
- properly designed surface irrigation.

D. Scheduling
- soil moisture monitoring,
- ET-based scheduling,
- crop-stage-based irrigation.

E. Crop management
- drought-tolerant varieties,
- appropriate planting date,
- optimum plant population,
- weed management,

- crop rotation.

F. Watershed management
- runoff control,
- groundwater recharge,
- erosion control,
- landscape-level water management.

9. Rainwater Harvesting
Rainwater harvesting means collecting and storing rainfall/runoff for later beneficial use or directing
it toward managed recharge.
Basic pathway:
Rainfall→Collection→Storage/Recharge→Agricultural Use 

10. Farm Pond
A farm pond is a constructed water-storage structure that collects runoff for later use.
Rainfall
↓
Runoff from field
↓
┌─────────────┐
## │ Farm Pond
│

│   │
│

Stored

│

│

Water

│

└──────┬──────┘
↓
Irrigation

Potential uses:
- supplemental irrigation,
- livestock water where appropriate,

- aquaculture in suitable systems,
- groundwater recharge in suitable designs.

11. Field Bunding
Bunds are raised soil structures constructed to slow or control surface runoff.
Bund→Runoff Speed↓→Infiltration Opportunity↑ 
They can also reduce soil erosion.
Types include:
- contour bunds,
- graded bunds,
- field bunds.
Design depends on slope, rainfall, soil and drainage requirements.

12. Contour Farming
Contour farming involves performing field operations approximately along contour lines rather than
directly up and down a slope.
The principle is:
Contour Alignment→Runoff Velocity↓→Infiltration Opportunity↑→Erosion↓ 
It is particularly relevant on sloping agricultural land.

13. Contour Furrows
Furrows can be arranged approximately along contour lines to slow runoff and increase opportunities
for infiltration.
Contour Furrow→Runoff Slowing→Infiltration 
Poorly designed furrows on slopes can instead concentrate water and cause erosion, so design is
important.

## 14. Terracing
Terracing divides sloping land into a series of relatively level or controlled-gradient sections.
 
|
 |
|

|
| 
|

 |

| 

Terraces can:
- reduce slope length,
- slow runoff,
- increase infiltration opportunity,
- reduce soil erosion,
- improve water conservation.
They require appropriate engineering and maintenance.

15. Check Dams
A check dam is a small barrier constructed across a drainage path to slow flowing water.
Flow Velocity↓→Sediment Trapping+Infiltration Opportunity↑ 
They can contribute to watershed management and groundwater recharge where site conditions permit.

16. Contour Trenches
Trenches constructed along contours can temporarily capture runoff.
Runoff→Contour Trench→Storage/Infiltration 
They are used in some watershed and land-restoration systems.

## 17. Mulching
Mulching means covering the soil surface with a protective material.

Materials include:
- crop residues,
- straw,
- leaves,
- compost,
- other suitable organic materials,
- plastic mulch in appropriate production systems.
The main water-conservation pathway is:
Mulch→Soil Surface Protection→Evaporation↓ 

18. How Mulch Conserves Water
Mulch can:
- reduce direct solar heating of soil,
- reduce wind exposure,
- reduce evaporation,
- reduce raindrop impact,
- reduce runoff,
- improve infiltration,
- moderate soil temperature.
Therefore:
Mulch→Evaporation↓+Infiltration Potential↑ 

19. Crop Residue Management
Retaining suitable crop residues on the soil surface can help conserve moisture.
Residue→Surface Cover→Evaporation↓ 
Residues can also:
- reduce erosion,

- improve soil structure,
- increase organic matter inputs,
- support soil biological activity.
But residue management must consider:
- pests,
- diseases,
- sowing equipment,
- nitrogen immobilization,
- decomposition,
- local agronomic conditions.

20. Conservation Tillage
Conservation tillage reduces soil disturbance and generally maintains substantial soil cover.
Reduced Disturbance+Soil Cover→Water Conservation 
Potential effects:
- evaporation reduction,
- infiltration improvement,
- erosion reduction,
- soil structure preservation.

21. Zero Tillage
Zero/no-till establishes crops with no conventional full-field tillage.
Direct Seeding+Residue Cover→Reduced Soil Disturbance 
Potential water benefits include:
- reduced evaporation,
- improved infiltration,
- improved moisture retention.

However, results depend on soil, climate, residue and management.

22. Minimum Tillage
Minimum tillage uses the minimum soil disturbance required for successful crop establishment and
management.
Tillage Intensity↓→Soil Disturbance↓→Potential Moisture Conservation↑ 

23. Soil Organic Matter and Water
Conservation
Organic matter can improve:
- aggregation,
- infiltration,
- water-holding capacity,
- soil structure,
- biological activity.
The relationship is:
Organic Matter→Better Soil Structure→Infiltration+Water Storage 
But the magnitude depends on soil type, organic material and management.

24. Improving Soil Water-Holding Capacity
Available water is approximately:
AWC=θFC−θPWP -   
Root-zone storage:
TAW=(θFC−θPWP)Zr - )Z r  
Improving soil structure and organic matter can increase the soil's ability to retain and supply water in
some soils.

25. Irrigation Scheduling
Correct irrigation timing is one of the most important water-conservation strategies.
The basic question is:
When should irrigation begin?   
and:
How much water should be applied?   
Scheduling can use:
- soil moisture,
- crop stage,
- ET,
- weather,
- rainfall,
- root-zone water depletion.

26. ET-Based Irrigation
Crop evapotranspiration:
ETc=KcET0 
where:
- ETcET c = crop evapotranspiration,
- KcK c = crop coefficient,
- ET0ET 0 = reference evapotranspiration.
This helps estimate crop water demand.

27. Soil-Moisture-Based Irrigation
Irrigation can be triggered when root-zone moisture reaches an appropriate threshold.
Conceptually:
Soil Moisture↓→Allowable Depletion→Irrigation 

Readily available water:
RAW=p×TAW 

28. Critical Moisture Stages
Water conservation does not mean allowing severe stress at every stage.
Water should be prioritized during sensitive stages such as:
- germination,
- establishment,
- flowering,
- pollination,
- fruit set,
- pod formation,
- grain formation,
- grain filling,
- seed filling.
The key principle is:
Water Stress Effect=f(Intensity,Duration,Timing,Crop,Genotype) 

29. Deficit Irrigation
Deficit irrigation deliberately applies less than full crop water requirement during selected periods
while protecting the crop during more sensitive periods.
Conceptually:
Limited Water→Prioritize Critical Stages→Manage Deficit During Less Sensitive Periods 
It must be crop- and environment-specific.

30. Drip Irrigation for Water Conservation
Drip irrigation delivers water locally.

Emitter→Root Zone 
Potential advantages:
- localized application,
- low application rate,
- reduced unnecessary wetting,
- fertigation,
- precise scheduling,
- automation.
But:
Drip Irrigation≠Zero Water Loss 

31. Sprinkler Irrigation
Sprinkler systems apply water through the air as droplets.
They can improve water distribution compared with poorly managed surface systems in appropriate
conditions.
However, losses can occur through:
- wind drift,
- evaporation,
- runoff,
- poor distribution.
Therefore:
Sprinkler+Correct Pressure+Scheduling+Wind Management 
is important.

32. Surface Irrigation Conservation
Surface irrigation can also conserve water if properly designed.
Important practices include:
- land levelling,
- correct field length,

- appropriate flow rate,
- proper irrigation timing,
- suitable furrow/basin design,
- minimizing runoff,
- avoiding excessive application.
Therefore:
Surface Irrigation≠Automatically Inefficient   
Management determines performance.

33. Land Levelling and Water Conservation
Uneven fields cause:
- over-irrigation in low areas,
- under-irrigation in high areas.
Levelling improves uniformity:
Land Levelling→More Uniform Water Distribution→Over/Under Irrigation↓ 
Precision land levelling can be particularly useful in suitable surface-irrigation systems.

34. Laser Land Levelling
Laser-controlled systems use a laser reference plane to guide land shaping.
Basic process:
Laser Transmitter→Receiver→Controller→Scraper→Precise Field Surface 
Potential benefits:
- improved irrigation uniformity,
- reduced ponding,
- reduced over-irrigation,
- improved establishment.

35. Rainwater Use
Rainfall should be viewed as a water resource.
A useful conceptual sequence is:
Rainfall→Infiltration→Soil Storage→Root Uptake 
rather than:
Rainfall→Runoff→Lost 
Water conservation therefore tries to increase useful infiltration where appropriate.

36. Effective Rainfall
Not all rainfall contributes equally to crop water supply.
Effective rainfall is the portion of rainfall that contributes to meeting crop water requirements after
considering:
- runoff,
- evaporation,
- deep drainage,
- interception,
- soil storage.
Therefore:
Irrigation Need≈Crop Water Requirement−Effective Rainfall 
subject to soil-water storage and other factors.

37. Rainfall Intensity and Water Conservation
If:
Rainfall Intensity>Infiltration Capacity 
then runoff potential increases.
Therefore conservation practices such as:
- mulch,

- residues,
- contour farming,
- bunding,
- improved aggregation,
- terraces
can increase opportunities for infiltration and reduce erosion.

38. Weed Management and Water Conservation
Weeds consume water.
Crop+Weed→Shared Water Resource 
Therefore effective weed management can increase the fraction of soil water available to the crop.
However, weed-control methods themselves should be selected carefully to avoid unnecessary soil
disturbance or erosion.

39. Crop Selection
Water availability should influence crop choice.
A water-limited farm may consider:
- drought adaptation,
- crop duration,
- rooting depth,
- market value,
- water requirement,
- planting season.
Conceptually:
Crop Choice=f(Water Availability+Climate+Soil+Market) 

40. Drought-Adapted Varieties
Drought-adapted varieties can maintain better performance under water deficit through traits such as:

- deeper roots,
- water-use efficiency,
- osmotic adjustment,
- stomatal regulation,
- early maturity,
- reproductive resilience.
But:
Drought-Adapted≠Drought-Proof Adapted Drought Proof  

41. Crop Duration
Shorter-duration crops may sometimes reduce total seasonal water requirement by shortening the
period of crop water use.
However:
Short Duration≠Automatically Lower Water Requirement 
Water requirement depends on:
- ET rate,
- crop coefficient,
- climate,
- biomass,
- planting date,
- crop physiology.

42. Crop Rotation and Water Conservation
Crop rotation can influence:
- rooting depth,
- soil structure,
- residue cover,
- organic matter,
- water use,

- pest/weed pressure.
For example:
Deep-Rooted+Shallow-Rooted→Different Soil Layers Explored 
Rooted+Shallow Rooted  Different\ Soil\ Layers\ Explored  
The actual water benefit depends on crop combination and management.

43. Intercropping and Water Use
Intercropping can improve resource use in some systems by combining crops with different:
- root architectures,
- canopy structures,
- water demand patterns,
- growth durations.
But competition can also increase.
Therefore:
Intercropping→Complementarity OR Competition 
depending on the crop combination and environment.

44. Groundwater Recharge
Water conservation is not only about irrigation.
Rainwater can be directed toward suitable recharge areas.
Rainfall/Runoff→Recharge Structure→Groundwater 
Examples include:
- recharge pits,
- recharge trenches,
- check dams,
- percolation structures.
Site suitability and water quality are essential.

45. Watershed Management
A watershed is an area draining toward a common outlet.
Watershed water conservation integrates:
- soil conservation,
- runoff management,
- water harvesting,
- groundwater recharge,
- vegetation,
- agriculture,
- drainage.
Conceptually:
Rainfall→Landscape→Runoff/Infiltration→Storage/Recharge→Agriculture 

46. Soil Conservation and Water Conservation
Are Connected
Erosion removes fertile topsoil.
Erosion→Topsoil Loss→Infiltration/Water Storage Potential↓ 
Therefore:
Soil Conservation↔Water Conservation 
Practices such as mulching, contour farming, terracing and residue retention can serve both purposes.

47. Water Conservation and Soil Health
Healthy soil can improve:
- infiltration,
- water storage,

- root growth,
- biological activity,
- nutrient availability.
Therefore:
Soil Health→Better Water Function 
Important properties include:
- structure,
- organic carbon,
- porosity,
- bulk density,
- infiltration,
- water-holding capacity.

48. Improving Infiltration
Water conservation does not mean keeping all water on the surface.
The goal is often:
Runoff↓+Useful Infiltration↑ 
Methods include:
- residue retention,
- mulching,
- organic matter,
- reduced disturbance,
- contour practices,
- appropriate bunding,
- improved soil aggregation.

49. Avoiding Over-Irrigation
Over-irrigation wastes water and can cause:
- deep percolation,

- nutrient leaching,
- waterlogging,
- salinity problems,
- unnecessary pumping costs.
The pathway is:
Excess Irrigation→Deep Percolation+Waterlogging+Nutrient Loss 

50. Avoiding Under-Irrigation
Under-irrigation can cause:
- water stress,
- stomatal closure,
- photosynthesis reduction,
- poor flowering,
- poor fruit/pod/grain set,
- reduced filling,
- yield loss.
Therefore:
Water Conservation≠Under-irrigation   

51. Water Productivity
A central metric is:
Water Productivity=Crop YieldWater Used  Water\
Used   
For example, if:
Yield=4000 kgYield=4000\ kg
and:
Water Used=4000 m3Water\ Used=4000\ m 3
then:
WP=40004000WP=  4000  WP=1 kg/m3 

The exact definition of "water used" must be stated because water productivity can be calculated using
different water-basis definitions.

52. Irrigation Water-Use Efficiency
A related concept is irrigation water-use efficiency.
One common expression is:
IWUE=YieldIrrigation Water Applied   Irrigation\ Water\ Applied   
Again, definitions vary, so the denominator should always be specified.

53. Conveyance Efficiency
Conveyance efficiency measures how much water remains available for field application after
transport.
Ec=Water Delivered to FieldWater Diverted from Source×100   Water\ Diverted\ from\ Source   100  
Losses may occur through:
- seepage,
- leakage,
- evaporation.

54. Application Efficiency
Ea=Water Stored in Effective Root ZoneWater Applied to Field×100   Water\ Applied\ to\ Field   100  
Higher efficiency means a greater proportion of applied water is stored where the crop can potentially
use it.

55. Distribution Uniformity
Water conservation requires uniform application.
If one part of a field gets too much and another too little:
Over-irrigation+Under-irrigation irrigation+Under irrigation  
can occur simultaneously.

Good distribution uniformity helps reduce this problem.

56. Water Conservation Through Automation
Modern irrigation can use:
- soil moisture sensors,
- weather stations,
- flow meters,
- pressure sensors,
- automated valves,
- controllers.
A basic system:
Sensor→Controller→Valve→Irrigation 
This can reduce unnecessary irrigation when properly calibrated and managed.

57. Weather-Based Irrigation
Weather information can help estimate:
- rainfall,
- temperature,
- humidity,
- wind,
- solar radiation,
- evapotranspiration.
Then:
Weather→ET Estimate→Irrigation Requirement 

58. Mulch + Drip Combination
A particularly useful integrated strategy is:

Drip+Mulch 
Drip controls where water is delivered, while mulch helps control loss from the soil surface.
Drip→Localized Supply 
Mulch→Evaporation Reduction 
Together they can improve root-zone moisture management.

59. Conservation Tillage + Residue + Efficient
Irrigation
An integrated approach can be:
Residue Cover+Reduced Tillage+Efficient Irrigation+Scheduling 
This addresses several components of water management simultaneously.

60. Water Conservation in Different Irrigation
Methods
Method
Major conservation consideration
Flood
Reduce unnecessary field wetting and runoff

Furrow
Proper furrow length, flow and timing

Micro-irrigation Localized delivery + proper scheduling

61. Water Conservation at Different Scales
Farm level
- drip/sprinkler,
- mulching,
- scheduling,
- farm pond,
- residue retention.

Field level
- levelling,
- bunding,
- contour farming,
- furrow management,
- soil-moisture conservation.

Watershed level
- check dams,
- recharge structures,
- vegetation,
- erosion control,
- runoff management.

Regional level
- groundwater management,
- irrigation planning,
- crop planning,
- water allocation,
- watershed development.

62. Water Conservation and Groundwater
Excessive groundwater extraction can lead to:
- declining water tables,
- higher pumping costs,
- reduced availability,
- quality problems in some regions.
Therefore:
Groundwater Conservation=Extraction Management+Recharge+Efficient Use 
Recharge alone does not guarantee sustainable groundwater use if extraction remains excessive.

63. Water Quality Conservation
Water conservation also includes maintaining water quality.
Agricultural activities can introduce:
- nutrients,
- pesticides,
- sediments,
- salts.
Therefore:
Good Water Management=Water Quantity+Water Quality 

64. Nutrient Management and Water
Conservation
Excess fertilizer combined with excessive irrigation can cause:
Excess Fertilizer+Excess Water→Nutrient Loss/Leaching 
Proper fertigation and nutrient scheduling can reduce unnecessary losses.

65. Water Conservation and Salinity
Poor irrigation and drainage can contribute to salt accumulation.
Poor Irrigation/Drainage→Salt Accumulation 
Therefore saline environments require:
- suitable water,
- appropriate irrigation,
- leaching where required,
- adequate drainage,
- salt-tolerant crops where appropriate.

66. Water Conservation and Waterlogging
Conservation does not mean retaining water indefinitely.
The goal is:
Retain Useful Water+Remove Excess Water 
Excess water can cause:
Waterlogging→O2↓→Root Respiration↓→Crop StressWaterlogging  O 2  Root\ Respiration  Crop\ Stress

67. Water Conservation Decision Framework
A practical decision process is:

Step 1 — Assess water availability
Source+Rainfall+Groundwater+Storage 

Step 2 — Assess crop requirement
Crop+Stage+ET 

Step 3 — Assess soil
Texture+Structure+AWC+Drainage 

Step 4 — Identify losses
Runoff+Evaporation+Deep Drainage+Leakage 

Step 5 — Select conservation practices
Harvest+Mulch+Tillage+Irrigation+Scheduling 

Step 6 — Monitor
Soil Moisture+Crop Response+Water Use+Yield 

68. Integrated Water Conservation Model
The strongest approach is usually not a single technology.
Instead:

Rainwater Harvesting+Soil Moisture Conservation+Efficient Irrigation+Scheduling+Crop Management
+Watershed Management  +   +
  +   +   +    
This creates a complete water-management system.

69. Common Misconceptions

❌ "Water conservation means giving crops less water."
No.
It means reducing unnecessary water use/loss while maintaining appropriate crop water supply.

❌ "Drip irrigation automatically saves all water."
No.
Poor scheduling can still waste water.

❌ "Rainwater runoff is always waste."
Not necessarily.
Runoff can sometimes be harvested or directed toward suitable infiltration/recharge structures.

❌ "Keeping all water in the field is conservation."
No.
Excess water can cause waterlogging and nutrient loss.

❌ "Mulching eliminates evaporation."
No.
It can reduce evaporation but cannot eliminate it.

❌ "Drought-tolerant crops don't need irrigation."
No.
They can generally perform better under water deficit but still require water.

❌ "More soil moisture always means better crop growth."
No.
Excess moisture can reduce soil oxygen.
