Photosynthesis
Decreases
Can decrease
Main management Supply/conserve water
Remove excess water
Major tool
Irrigation/moisture conservation Drainage
Important:
Drought=Water Deficit   
Waterlogging=Water Excess + Oxygen Deficit   

65. Waterlogging vs Drainage
Waterlogging
Drainage
Problem/condition
Management process
Excess water remains Excess water is removed
Soil oxygen decreases Soil aeration can improve
Root stress increases Root-zone conditions improve
Can reduce yield
Can reduce waterlogging risk
Thus:
Drainage is one of the primary management tools for waterlogging. 

66. Common Misconceptions

❌ "Waterlogged plants cannot take up any water."
Not necessarily.
Water may be physically abundant, but root function can be severely impaired because of oxygen
deficiency.

❌ "If the field has no standing water, it cannot be waterlogged."
False.

A shallow water table can saturate the root zone without obvious surface ponding.

❌ "All crops die immediately after waterlogging."
False.
Tolerance varies greatly with crop, genotype, stage and duration.

❌ "More drainage is always better."
No.

Excessive drainage can waste water and nutrients.

❌ "Raised beds always solve waterlogging."
No.
They mainly improve surface/root-zone geometry and may not solve subsurface groundwater problems.

❌ "Waterlogging is just a water problem."
It is primarily a water–air balance problem.

67. Key Equations
Air-filled porosity
AFP=n−θv 

Total porosity
n=(1−ρbρs)×100    s  ) 100  

Water balance
ΔS=P+I+CR−R−D−ET 

Crop evapotranspiration
ETc=KcET0 

Available water
AWC=θFC−θPWP -   

Total available water
TAW=(θFC−θPWP)Zr - )Z r  

68. Most Important Cause–Effect Chain
Memorize this:

Excess Water→Saturation→Air-Filled Pores↓→O2↓→Root Respiration↓→Root Function↓→Nutrient
Uptake↓→Photosynthesis/Growth↓→Yield↓      O 2            

69. Exam-Ready Definition
Waterlogging is a condition in which excess water saturates or nearly saturates the
crop root zone for a sufficient period, reducing air-filled pore space and oxygen
availability and thereby impairing root respiration, nutrient uptake, growth,
development and potentially crop yield.

70. Final Conceptual Summary
Waterlogging should be understood as a root-zone oxygen problem created by excess water.
Excess Water→Soil Saturation→Oxygen Deficiency→Root Stress→Growth/Yield Loss       
The solution is not simply to stop irrigating. A complete strategy is:
Control Water Input+Improve Surface Drainage+Improve Subsurface Drainage Where Required+Avoi
d Compaction+Use Suitable Field Geometry+Select Appropriate Crops/Varieties  +   +   +   +   +    

Final one-line memory
Waterlogging = Too much water in the root zone for too long → too little oxygen for healthy roots.
   

## Module 11 — Water & Irrigation
## Management
Drought Management
## 1. Introduction
Drought management is the planned set of agricultural practices used to prevent, reduce, withstand
and recover from the effects of insufficient water availability on crops.
Drought is not simply “low rainfall.” A crop experiences drought when the water available to the
plant becomes insufficient relative to its demand.
A useful conceptual relationship is:
Drought Stress=f(Soil Water,Atmospheric Demand,Duration,Crop,Growth Stage)
  = f( , , ,
 , )  
The objective of drought management is therefore:
Save Water+Supply Water at Critical Times+Reduce Water Loss+Maintain Crop Productivity  +   +   +    

## 2. Definition
Drought management in agriculture is the systematic planning and implementation of
agronomic, soil, water, crop, technological and environmental measures to reduce crop
water stress, conserve available water, prioritize water during critical growth stages
and maintain yield and crop survival during periods of inadequate water availability.

One-line memory
Drought Management = Conserve water + use available water efficiently + protect the crop during wate
r stress.  

3. What Is Agricultural Drought?
Agricultural drought occurs when available water becomes insufficient to meet the water requirements
- f crops, resulting in physiological stress and reduced growth, yield or quality.
It can result from:

- low rainfall,
- irregular rainfall,
- prolonged dry periods,
- high temperature,
- high evapotranspiration,
- low soil-water storage,
- poor irrigation availability,
- shallow rooting,
- excessive competition from weeds.
Therefore:
Low Rainfall≠Always Severe Crop Drought   
and:
Normal Rainfall≠Always No Drought   
because atmospheric demand and soil-water storage also matter.

4. Drought Is a Water-Balance Problem
A simplified soil-water balance is:
ΔS=P+I+CR−R−D−ET 
where:
- PP = precipitation,
- II = irrigation,
- CRCR = capillary rise,
- RR = runoff,
- DD = drainage/deep percolation,
- ETET = evapotranspiration,
- ΔS S = change in soil-water storage.
Drought develops when water losses and crop demand persistently exceed available water
inputs/storage.
Conceptually:

Water Inputs<Crop + Atmospheric Water Demand→Water Deficit  <
    

5. Drought vs Waterlogging
These are opposite water stresses.
Drought
Waterlogging
Water deficit
Excess water
Soil moisture too low
Soil moisture too high
Plant water potential decreases Soil oxygen decreases
Stomatal closure common
Root oxygen stress
Irrigation/conservation needed Drainage needed
Root water uptake limited
Root respiration limited
The key distinction is:
Drought = Water shortage  
Waterlogging = Excess water + oxygen shortage  

6. Types of Drought Relevant to Agriculture
6.1 Meteorological drought
Rainfall is significantly below normal over a period.
Rainfall↓→Meteorological Drought 

6.2 Agricultural drought
Soil water becomes insufficient for crop growth.
Soil Water↓→Crop Stress 

6.3 Hydrological drought
Water availability in:
- reservoirs,
- rivers,
- lakes,
- groundwater
becomes unusually low.

6.4 Socioeconomic drought
Water shortage begins to affect:
- agricultural production,
- food supply,
- livestock,
- markets,
- livelihoods.
These drought types can develop at different times.

7. Types of Drought Stress in Crops
Crop responses can broadly be understood as:

Drought escape
The crop completes its life cycle before severe drought develops.
Early Maturity→Drought Escape 

Drought avoidance
The plant maintains better water status despite environmental dryness.
Mechanisms include:
- deep roots,
- efficient water uptake,
- stomatal regulation,
- reduced water loss.

Drought tolerance
The plant maintains physiological function despite low tissue water status.
Mechanisms may include:
- osmotic adjustment,
- osmoprotectants,
- antioxidant defense,
- cellular protection.

Recovery
Ability to resume growth after water becomes available again.
Drought Response=Escape+Avoidance+Tolerance+Recovery 

8. Why Drought Causes Crop Damage
The basic pathway is:
Soil Water↓→Plant Water Status↓→Stomatal Closure→CO2 Uptake↓→Photosynthesis↓ 
At greater severity:
Growth↓→Flowering/Set↓→Filling↓→Yield↓ 

9. Drought and Plant Water Potential
Water moves from regions of higher water potential toward lower water potential.
Under soil drying:
Soil Water Potential↓→Root Water Uptake↓     
Severe water deficit reduces plant tissue hydration and turgor.
This affects:
- cell expansion,
- stomatal function,
- photosynthesis,
- nutrient transport,
- reproductive development.

10. Drought and Stomata
One of the earliest physiological responses is stomatal regulation.
Water Stress→ABA Signaling→Stomatal Closure 

Stomatal closure reduces water loss:
Transpiration↓ 
but also restricts CO₂ entry:
CO2 Uptake↓→Photosynthesis↓ 
Therefore drought management involves balancing:
Water SavingvsCarbon Assimilation 

11. Drought and Photosynthesis
Drought can reduce photosynthesis through:
- stomatal closure,
- reduced CO₂ availability,
- reduced leaf area,
- chlorophyll damage under severe stress,
- impaired photosynthetic machinery,
- oxidative stress.
Therefore:
Drought→Photosynthesis↓→Biomass Production↓ 

12. Drought and Respiration
Respiration continues during drought, but severe stress can alter metabolic balance.
Meanwhile:
Photosynthesis↓+Maintenance Respiration→Carbon Balance Decline 
Under severe stress, stored carbohydrates may increasingly support maintenance and recovery.

13. Drought and Root Growth
Roots are central to drought response.
Potential adaptive traits include:

- deeper roots,
- greater root length density,
- favorable root:shoot ratio,
- improved root branching,
- root growth into moist soil layers.
Root length density:
RLD=Total Root LengthSoil Volume  Soil\ Volume   
But deeper roots are not automatically beneficial in every soil; they require suitable soil depth, oxygen,
nutrients and accessible water.

14. Drought and Root–Shoot Ratio
Under some drought conditions, plants may allocate relatively more resources to roots.
R:S=Root BiomassShoot Biomass  Shoot\ Biomass   
An increase in root allocation can potentially improve water acquisition.
However, excessive root investment can reduce shoot and reproductive growth.
Therefore:
Optimal Allocation>Maximum Root Growth >   

15. Drought and Leaf Area
Plants may reduce leaf expansion under drought.
This can decrease:
Leaf Area↓→Transpiration↓ 
But it also reduces potential light interception:
Leaf Area↓→Photosynthetic Capacity↓ 
Therefore leaf-area reduction is a water-conservation mechanism with a potential productivity cost.

16. Drought and Transpiration
Transpiration:

ET=E+T 
where:
- EE = soil evaporation,
- TT = plant transpiration.
Drought management should ideally reduce unproductive water losses while preserving useful
transpiration.

17. Evaporation vs Transpiration
This is extremely important.

Evaporation
Water lost directly from:
- soil,
- wet surfaces.

Transpiration
Water lost through plant tissues, primarily stomata.
Therefore:
ET=E+T 
A major drought-management objective is:
E↓while maintaining productive T T  

18. Drought and Evapotranspiration
Crop water requirement can be estimated using:
ETc=KcET0 
where:
- ETcET c = crop evapotranspiration,
- KcK c = crop coefficient,
- ET0ET 0 = reference evapotranspiration.
When atmospheric demand is high:
ET0↑→Crop Water Demand↑ 

Thus a crop can experience drought stress even during a period with moderate rainfall if atmospheric
demand is very high.

19. Drought and VPD
Vapor pressure deficit:
VPD=es−ea 
Higher VPD generally means greater atmospheric drying demand.
Therefore:
VPD↑→Atmospheric Water Demand↑ 
Drought management should therefore consider both:
- soil water,
- atmospheric demand.

20. Soil Moisture and Drought
Important soil-water reference points are:

Field Capacity
Water remaining after excess gravitational water has drained from a well-drained soil.

Permanent Wilting Point
Soil-water condition at which a plant cannot recover turgor under the defined reference conditions.
Available water:
AWC≈θFC−θPWP -   

21. Total Available Water
For a root zone:
TAW=(θFC−θPWP)Zr - )Z r  
where:
- ZrZ r = effective root-zone depth.
A deeper effective root zone can potentially access more stored water, provided the deeper soil contains
available water.

22. Readily Available Water
Not all total available water can be extracted without stress.
A conceptual expression is:
RAW=p×TAW 
where:
- RAWRAW = readily available water,
- pp = allowable depletion fraction.
As the soil dries beyond the crop-specific allowable depletion:
Stress Risk↑ 

23. Critical Moisture Stages
Not all growth stages have equal drought sensitivity.
Important stages may include:
- germination,
- seedling establishment,
- flowering,
- pollination,
- fruit set,
- pod formation,
- grain formation,
- grain filling,
- tuber/root/bulb development.
A useful principle is:
Water Stress Effect=f(Intensity,Duration,Timing)  =
f( , , )  

24. Why Flowering Is Often Important
Water stress during flowering can cause:

- flower abortion,
- pollen problems,
- reduced stigma function,
- reduced fertilization,
- poor fruit/pod/grain set.
Therefore:
Drought at Reproductive Stage→Reproductive Units↓ 

25. Drought During Grain Filling
During grain filling:
Drought→Photosynthesis↓+Senescence↑→Assimilate Supply↓ 
Consequently:
Grain Filling↓→Grain Weight↓ 

26. Drought and Yield Components
A simplified yield equation is:
Yield≈Plants/Area×Reproductive Units/Plant×Weight/Unit 
Drought can affect each component.

Early drought
Plant Population↓Plant\ Population Flowering drought
Reproductive Units↓Reproductive\ Units Filling drought
Unit Weight↓Unit\ Weight This explains why timing matters as much as severity.

27. Drought Management Strategy
Drought management can be divided into:
Before Drought+During Drought+After Drought  +   +    

28. Before Drought — Preparedness
The most effective drought management begins before severe water shortage.
Major practices include:
1. drought-tolerant varieties,
2. appropriate crop selection,
3. timely sowing,
4. soil-water conservation,
5. rainwater harvesting,
6. efficient irrigation,
7. mulching,
8. weed control,
9. appropriate plant population,
10.crop rotation,
11.residue management,
12.farm ponds/storage,
13.soil organic matter management.

29. Drought-Resistant/Tolerant Varieties
Varieties can be selected for improved performance under water deficit.
Possible traits include:
- deep rooting,
- efficient water uptake,
- osmotic adjustment,
- stomatal regulation,

- early maturity,
- heat tolerance,
- reproductive resilience,
- efficient water use,
- recovery after drought.
However:
No Crop Variety Is Completely Drought-Proof   
Performance depends on:
G+E+G×E 

30. Drought Escape Through Early Maturity
A short-duration cultivar can complete critical development before severe drought occurs.
Early Maturity→Drought Exposure Duration↓ 
This is particularly useful where drought has a predictable seasonal pattern.
But early maturity may reduce yield potential under favorable conditions.

31. Timely Sowing
Sowing date should be selected so that important growth stages coincide with favorable moisture and
temperature conditions.
Conceptually:
Sowing Date→Crop Phenology→Critical Stage→Rainfall/Moisture Availability 
This is a major drought-escape strategy.

32. Soil-Water Conservation
The main goal is:
Rainfall→Infiltration→Soil Storage 
instead of:

Rainfall→Runoff 
Useful practices include:
- residue retention,
- mulching,
- cover crops,
- reduced tillage,
- contour farming,
- terracing where appropriate,
- bunding,
- tied ridges,
- conservation furrows,
- rainwater harvesting.

## 33. Mulching
Mulch is material placed on the soil surface.
Examples:
- crop residues,
- straw,
- leaves,
- suitable organic materials,
- selected synthetic mulches.
Mulching can reduce:
Soil Evaporation↓ 
and may also:
- moderate soil temperature,
- reduce raindrop impact,
- suppress weeds,
- improve infiltration under suitable conditions.

34. Residue Management
Crop residues left on the soil surface can:
- reduce evaporation,
- protect soil,
- reduce runoff,
- reduce erosion,
- contribute organic matter.
The pathway is:
Residue Cover→Soil Protection+Evaporation↓+Infiltration Opportunity↑ 

35. Conservation Tillage
Reduced soil disturbance can help conserve soil moisture in suitable systems.
Reduced Disturbance+Residue Cover→Water Conservation 
However, drought management should remain crop- and soil-specific.

36. Rainwater Harvesting
Rainwater harvesting captures and stores runoff for later use.
Basic pathway:
Rainfall→Collection→Storage→Irrigation/Other Use 
Examples include:
- farm ponds,
- check structures,
- storage tanks,
- field bunds,
- watershed structures.

37. Farm Ponds
Farm ponds can store water during periods of rainfall for later use.
Rainy Period→Storage→Dry Period→Supplemental Irrigation 
Benefits depend on:
- catchment,
- rainfall,
- evaporation,
- storage capacity,
- seepage,
- water quality,
- irrigation demand.

38. Efficient Irrigation
During drought, irrigation water should be applied strategically.
The principle is:
Right Time+Right Amount+Right Method  +   +
   
Methods such as:
- drip,
- sprinkler,
- micro-irrigation,
can improve control and reduce some forms of water loss when properly designed and managed.

39. Drip Irrigation for Drought Management
Drip irrigation delivers water near the root zone.
Water Source→Drip Emitter→Root Zone 
Potential advantages:

- localized application,
- reduced nonproductive surface wetting,
- frequent small applications,
- better control of root-zone moisture.
But efficiency depends on:
- system design,
- filtration,
- pressure,
- emitter uniformity,
- scheduling,
- maintenance.

40. Sprinkler Irrigation
Sprinklers apply water through pressurized systems in the form of droplets.
They can be useful where:
- terrain,
- soil,
- crop,
- water availability
make them suitable.
However, evaporation and wind drift can be significant under hot, dry and windy conditions.
Therefore:
Irrigation Method Must Match Local Conditions   

41. Deficit Irrigation
Deficit irrigation deliberately applies less water than full crop evapotranspiration requirement during
selected periods.
The idea is:

Limited Water→Prioritize Sensitive Stages 
Rather than distributing insufficient water equally across all stages.
This must be crop- and stage-specific.

42. Supplemental Irrigation
Supplemental irrigation provides additional water when rainfall is insufficient.
Rainfall+Supplemental Irrigation→More Reliable Root-Zone Moisture Zone\ Moisture  
It is particularly useful in rainfed systems when limited irrigation water is available.

43. Irrigation Scheduling During Drought
Scheduling should consider:
- current soil moisture,
- crop stage,
- ET,
- rainfall forecast,
- root depth,
- soil water-holding capacity,
- irrigation capacity,
- remaining water supply.
Conceptually:
Soil Moisture+ET+Crop Stage+Weather→Irrigation Decision 

44. Prioritizing Critical Stages
When water is limited, irrigation can be prioritized for stages where stress causes large yield losses.
For example, conceptually:
Establishment→Flowering/Set→Filling 

The exact priority depends on the crop.

45. Weed Management During Drought
Weeds compete with crops for:
- water,
- nutrients,
- light,
- space.
Under drought:
Weeds+Crop→Water Competition↑ 
Therefore timely weed management can improve water availability to the crop.
However, soil-covering weeds may sometimes affect evaporation/erosion, so management should be
based on crop competition and system objectives.

46. Plant Population Management
Very high plant density can increase competition for limited water.
Plant Density↑→Water Demand per Area↑ 
Under drought-prone conditions, crop-specific adjustments to population may be appropriate.
But excessively low population wastes land and may reduce yield.
Therefore:
Optimum Population≠Maximum Population 

47. Crop Rotation
Rotation can improve long-term soil function and water management.
For example:
Crop A→Crop B→Crop C 
Benefits may include:
- improved soil structure,

- diversified rooting,
- residue diversity,
- improved soil organic matter,
- better pest/weed management.
Legumes can also contribute nitrogen to the system.

48. Soil Organic Matter
Organic matter can improve:
- aggregation,
- water retention,
- infiltration,
- biological activity.
Conceptually:
Organic Matter→Better Soil Structure→Improved Water Storage/Infiltration 
But effects vary with soil type and management.

49. Improving Infiltration
The goal is to allow rainfall to enter the soil rather than become runoff.
Infiltration↑→Soil Water Storage↑ 
Useful practices can include:
- residue cover,
- reduced disturbance,
- improved aggregation,
- contour measures,
- appropriate organic inputs,
- controlled traffic.

50. Reducing Runoff
Runoff represents water that leaves the field without entering the soil or being used.
Runoff↓→Potential Soil Water Storage↑ 
Possible measures:
- contour bunds,
- terraces,
- vegetative cover,
- residue,
- grassed waterways,
- conservation structures.

51. Reducing Soil Evaporation
A major drought strategy is:
Soil Evaporation↓ 
Methods include:
- mulch,
- crop residues,
- canopy management,
- appropriate irrigation,
- conservation tillage.

52. Canopy Management
A well-developed canopy can shade the soil.
Canopy Cover↑→Soil Radiation↓→Evaporation Potential↓ 
But excessive canopy may increase crop transpiration and water demand.
Therefore canopy management requires balance.

53. Drought and Heat Stress
Drought and heat often occur together.
Drought+Heat→Compounded Stress 
Heat increases atmospheric demand:
Temperature↑→VPD/ET Demand↑ 
Drought reduces the water available for evaporative cooling.
Therefore:
Water Deficit→Transpiration↓→Cooling↓→Leaf Temperature↑ 
This can intensify heat stress.

54. Crop Temperature and Drought
Canopy temperature depression:
CTD=Tair−Tcanopy -T  canopy   
When transpiration is active:
TcanopyT  canopy 
can remain lower than air temperature.
Under severe drought:
Stomatal Closure→Transpiration↓→Canopy Temperature↑ 
Canopy temperature can therefore serve as an indicator of crop water status in appropriate monitoring
systems.

55. Water-Use Efficiency
Water-use efficiency can be expressed conceptually as:
WUE=Biomass or YieldWater Used  Water\ Used   
Improving WUE means producing more useful output per unit of water.
But:

High WUE≠Automatically High Yield 
A crop could use little water but also produce little yield.
The goal is:
High Productive Water Use 

56. Irrigation Water-Use Efficiency
A more irrigation-specific concept is the amount of useful crop production obtained from irrigation
water.
It should be evaluated alongside:
- application efficiency,
- distribution uniformity,
- yield,
- water productivity.

57. Fertilizer Management During Drought
Water shortage can reduce nutrient uptake.
Therefore excessive fertilizer application may not provide expected benefits and can sometimes
increase osmotic stress or economic losses.
Important principles:
- soil-test-based fertilization,
- balanced nutrition,
- appropriate timing,
- avoid unnecessary excess,
- synchronize nutrient availability with crop demand.

58. Nitrogen and Drought
Drought can reduce:
- root activity,
- nitrate movement,

- nutrient uptake,
- growth.
Therefore:
Drought→N Uptake↓ 
But severe nitrogen deficiency can also reduce drought resilience.
Balanced nutrition is therefore important.

59. Phosphorus and Root Development
Adequate phosphorus supports:
- root growth,
- energy metabolism,
- establishment.
However fertilizer cannot replace missing water.
Nutrients Support Drought Response≠Nutrients Eliminate Drought 

60. Drought and Plant Growth Regulators
Plants naturally use hormonal signaling during drought.
Important hormones/signals include:
- ABA,
- ethylene,
- auxin,
- cytokinins,
- jasmonates,
- salicylic acid.
ABA is especially important in:
Drought→ABA→Stomatal Regulation 

61. Osmotic Adjustment
Under water stress, some plants accumulate compatible solutes such as:
- proline,
- soluble sugars,
- certain amino acids,
- other osmolytes.
These can help maintain cellular water relations.
Conceptually:
Osmolyte Accumulation→Cellular Water Retention 
This is a physiological drought-response mechanism.

62. Antioxidant Defense
Drought can increase oxidative stress.
Reactive oxygen species can accumulate:
Drought→ROS↑ 
Plants use antioxidant systems such as:
- superoxide dismutase,
- catalase,
- ascorbate peroxidase,
- non-enzymatic antioxidants.
Conceptually:
Antioxidants→ROS Detoxification 

63. Drought and Senescence
Severe drought can accelerate leaf senescence.
Drought→Senescence↑→Green Leaf Area↓→Photosynthesis↓ 

Early senescence reduces the period available for carbon assimilation and can reduce yield.

64. Stay-Green Trait
Some genotypes maintain green leaves for longer under stress.
This is often called a stay-green trait.
Potential advantage:
Green Canopy Duration↑→Assimilate Supply Potential↑ 
But stay-green has different forms and is not automatically beneficial under every environment.

65. Drought Management in Rainfed
Agriculture
Important practices include:

Before season
- drought-adapted crop/variety,
- timely sowing,
- seed treatment/quality seed,
- soil moisture conservation,
- rainwater harvesting.

During season
- weed control,
- mulching/residue,
- moisture-based irrigation if available,
- critical-stage irrigation,
- balanced nutrition,
- pest/disease monitoring.

Severe drought
- prioritize critical stages,

- conserve remaining water,
- reduce nonproductive losses,
- consider crop-specific contingency measures.

66. Contingency Planning
A drought-prone farm can prepare alternative plans.
For example:
Normal Rainfall→Normal Crop Plan 
Delayed Rainfall→Alternative Sowing Plan  Severe Moisture Deficit→Short Duration/Lower Water Demand Strategy 
Actual decisions must be crop-, region- and season-specific.

67. Drought Management Timeline
Before drought
Prepare→Conserve→Plan 

During drought
Monitor→Prioritize→Irrigate Efficiently→Reduce Losses 

After drought
Rehydrate→Assess Damage→Support Recovery→Plan Next Season 

68. Monitoring Drought Stress
Drought can be monitored using:

Soil indicators
- soil moisture,
- soil-water potential,
- root-zone depletion.

Plant indicators
- wilting,
- leaf rolling,
- leaf area,
- canopy temperature,
- stomatal conductance,
- chlorophyll/photosynthesis.

Atmospheric indicators
- temperature,
- RH,
- VPD,
- ET.

Remote sensing
- vegetation indices,
- canopy temperature,
- spectral indicators,
- satellite-derived drought indicators.

69. Soil Moisture Sensors
Modern drought management can use:
- TDR,
- FDR/capacitance sensors,
- tensiometers,
- soil-water potential sensors.
The objective is:
Measure Soil Moisture→Estimate Depletion→Irrigate When Appropriate 

70. Weather Forecasting
Weather forecasts can support drought management by predicting:
- rainfall,
- temperature,
- heat waves,
- dry spells,
- atmospheric demand.
Conceptually:
Forecast+Soil Moisture+Crop Stage→Better Water Decision 
Forecasts contain uncertainty, so they should support rather than replace field monitoring.

71. Integrated Drought Management
The strongest approach combines multiple strategies.
Drought-adapted variety+Timely sowing+Soil-water conservation+Efficient irrigation+Critical-stage ir
rigation+Mulching/residue+Weed management+Balanced nutrition+Rainwater harvesting+Monitoring\
boxed    & \\ +& \\ +& \\ +& \\ +& \\
+& \\ +& \\ +& \\
+& \\ +&     
No single technique can eliminate drought risk.

72. Drought Management Decision Framework
Weather + Rainfall Forecast
↓
Soil Moisture
↓
Crop Stage
↓
Water Availability
↓

Drought Risk Assessment
↓
┌────────────────────────┐
│ Select Management

│

└────────────────────────┘
↓
┌────────┼─────────┐
↓

↓

↓

Conserve

Irrigate

Adapt Crop

Water

Efficiently/Strategy
↓
Monitor
↓

Adjust Management

73. Drought Management by Objective
Objective
Possible practices
Increase water availability Rainwater harvesting, farm ponds, supplemental irrigation
Reduce water loss
Mulching, residue, weed control
Increase soil storage
Organic matter, improved structure, infiltration practices
Improve root water access Appropriate soil management, deep rooting, suitable varieties
Use irrigation efficiently Drip, sprinkler, scheduling
Protect yield
Critical-stage irrigation
Reduce drought exposure Early/appropriate maturity, timely sowing
Improve resilience
Crop diversification, rotation, drought-adapted varieties
Monitor stress
Soil sensors, canopy temperature, weather data

74. Drought Management and Crop Selection
Crop choice should consider:
- rainfall reliability,
- available irrigation,
- soil water-holding capacity,
- growing season,

- drought probability,
- market value,
- crop duration.
Conceptually:
Crop Choice=f(Water Availability+Climate+Soil+Market) 
A lower-water-demand crop may sometimes be more suitable than a high-water-demand crop where
water is severely limited.

75. Drought Management and Crop Duration
Long-duration crops may face longer exposure to drought.
Short-duration crops may reduce drought exposure:
Crop Duration↓→Potential Drought Exposure↓ 
But shorter duration does not automatically mean higher yield or better economics.

76. Drought and Planting Geometry
Plant population and spacing influence water competition.
Closer Spacing→Interplant Competition↑ 
Wider spacing:
Competition↓ 
but excessive spacing can reduce land-use efficiency.
Therefore drought-prone systems need crop-specific optimum geometry.

77. Drought and Microclimate
Canopy architecture affects:
- temperature,
- humidity,
- radiation,

- wind,
- transpiration.
For example, canopy shading can reduce soil evaporation, while excessive density may increase
competition for water.
Thus:
Plant Density+Canopy Architecture→Microclimate+Water Use 

78. Drought and Wind
Wind can increase atmospheric water loss.
Wind↑→Boundary Layer↓→Water Vapor Transfer↑→Transpiration Demand↑ 
Windbreaks can sometimes reduce wind speed and water loss.
However they also compete for water and resources, so design and species selection matter.

79. Drought and Soil Conservation
Drought and erosion can interact.
When vegetation cover is poor:
Bare Soil→Runoff/Erosion↑→Topsoil Loss→Water Storage Potential↓ 
Therefore drought management and soil conservation should be integrated.

80. Drought and Water Quality
Limited irrigation water may sometimes be saline.
Salinity itself reduces plant water availability.
Therefore:
Drought+Salinity→Compounded Water Stress 
Water quality should therefore be considered when planning irrigation during drought.

81. Drought and Salinity
Salinity lowers the effective water potential of the soil solution.
Conceptually:
Salinity↑→Osmotic Stress↑→Water Uptake↓ 
Therefore a field can experience physiological drought even when soil contains water.

82. Drought Management vs Water
Conservation
They are closely related but not identical.

Water conservation
Focuses on:
Saving and Storing Water 

Drought management
Focuses on:
Managing Crop Production Under Water Deficit 
Thus:
Water Conservation⊆Drought Management 
in many agricultural planning frameworks.

83. Common Mistakes in Drought Management

❌ Waiting until severe wilting
❌ Irrigating only according to calendar
Stress may already have damaged yield.

Soil and atmospheric conditions vary.

❌ Giving equal water to every growth stage
❌ Applying more fertilizer to compensate for drought
❌ Assuming drought means only low rainfall
❌ Assuming high WUE always means high yield
❌ Assuming drought-tolerant varieties need no irrigation
❌ Ignoring weeds
❌ Using one drought strategy for every crop
Critical stages may deserve priority.

Fertilizer cannot replace water.

ET, soil storage and crop demand matter.

Water productivity and yield must both be considered.

Tolerance reduces damage; it does not eliminate water requirements.

Weeds can consume valuable water.

Crop, soil, climate and growth stage matter.

84. Key Equations
Soil-water balance
ΔS=P+I+CR−R−D−ET 

Crop evapotranspiration
ETc=KcET0 

Available water
AWC=θFC−θPWP -   

Total available water
TAW=(θFC−θPWP)Zr - )Z r  

Readily available water
RAW=p×TAW 

Water-use efficiency
WUE=Yield or BiomassWater Used  Water\ Used   

Root-to-shoot ratio
R:S=Root BiomassShoot Biomass  Shoot\ Biomass   

Canopy temperature depression
CTD=Tair−Tcanopy -T  canopy   

Vapor pressure deficit
VPD=es−ea 

85. Master Drought Management Model
The entire concept can be summarized as:
Rainfall/Water Storage→Soil Moisture→Root Water Uptake→Plant Water Status→Photosynthesis/
Growth→Yield        
Drought management acts at every point:
Increase water input/storageReduce runoffReduce evaporationImprove root accessImprove irrigation ef
ficiencyProtect critical stagesUse adapted genotypesReduce competing weedsMonitor and respond earl
y  & \\ & \\
& \\ & \\ & \\
& \\ & \\ & \\
&     

86. Exam-Ready Definition
Drought management is the planned use of crop, soil, water, agronomic and
technological practices to conserve and efficiently use available water, reduce crop
water stress, protect critical growth stages and maintain crop productivity and
survival during periods of inadequate water availability.

87. Final Conceptual Summary
The most important principle is:
Drought management is not simply about giving more water.   
It is about managing the entire water balance and protecting the crop during the periods when
water matters most.

The complete pathway is:
Conserve Rainfall→Store Water→Reduce Losses→Efficient Irrigation→Protect Critical Stages→Main
tain Root Water Status→Maintain Growth→Protect Yield          

The four major drought strategies
Escape + Avoidance + Tolerance + Recovery   

The four major water-management principles
Store + Conserve + Schedule + Prioritize   

Final one-line memory
Drought Management = Save available water, reduce unnecessary losses, supply water at critical stages
, and use drought-adapted crops and practices to protect yield.   

## Module 11 — Water & Irrigation
## Management
Irrigation Efficiency
## 1. Introduction
Irrigation efficiency describes how effectively irrigation water is captured, conveyed, applied, stored
in the crop root zone, and ultimately used by the crop.
In simple terms:
Irrigation Efficiency=Useful crop/root-zone water obtained from irrigation compared with water suppli
ed =  
The goal is not merely to apply less water. The goal is to make a larger proportion of the supplied
water useful to the crop, while minimizing unnecessary:
- runoff,
- deep percolation,
- evaporation,

- leakage,
- conveyance losses,
- nonuniform application.

## 2. Definition
Irrigation efficiency is the ratio of water beneficially used or stored in the effective
crop root zone to the amount of irrigation water supplied, expressed as a percentage.
A common field-level expression is:
Ea=Water stored in effective root zoneWater applied×100        100  
where EaE a is application efficiency.

One-line memory
Irrigation efficiency = More useful water, less avoidable water loss.  

3. Why Irrigation Efficiency Is Important
Water is lost at several points between its source and the crop.
For example:
Water Source
↓
Conveyance
↓
Field Application
↓
Root Zone
↓
Crop

Losses can occur through:
Leakage + Runoff + Deep Percolation + Evaporation   
Improving efficiency can:

- conserve water,
- increase area irrigated with the same water supply,
- reduce pumping requirements,
- reduce energy use,
- reduce waterlogging,
- reduce nutrient leaching,
- improve water productivity,
- improve crop establishment and yield stability.

4. Basic Concept
Suppose:
- 100 mm of irrigation water is applied,
- 70 mm becomes beneficially stored in the effective root zone.
Then:
Ea=70100×100E a=  100  100 Ea=70% 
The remaining 30 mm may have been lost through:
- runoff,
- deep drainage,
- evaporation,
- other application losses.

5. Irrigation Efficiency Is Not One Single
Efficiency
This is very important.
Water can be lost at different stages.
Therefore irrigation performance can be divided into several efficiencies:
1. Conveyance efficiency
2. Application efficiency

3. Storage efficiency
4. Distribution uniformity
5. Overall irrigation efficiency
6. Water-use efficiency
7. Water productivity
These terms should not be treated as synonyms.

6. Conveyance Efficiency
Conveyance efficiency measures how much water reaches the field compared with the amount diverted
from the source.
Ec=WfWd×100  W d  100  
where:
- WfW f = water delivered to the field,
- WdW d = water diverted from the source.

Example
If 1,000 m³ is diverted from a canal but only 800 m³ reaches the field:
Ec=8001000×100E c=  1000  100 Ec=80% 
The 200 m³ difference represents conveyance losses.

7. Causes of Conveyance Loss
Water can be lost during transport through:
- seepage,
- leakage,
- evaporation,
- overtopping,
- cracks,
- poorly maintained channels.
For an unlined earthen channel:
Seepage Loss can be significant 

depending on soil and channel conditions.

8. Improving Conveyance Efficiency
Possible measures include:
- lining suitable channels,
- maintaining channels,
- repairing cracks,
- controlling leakage,
- reducing unnecessary channel length,
- using properly designed pipes,
- controlling flow,
- maintaining appropriate channel capacity.
For example:
Open Channel→Pipe Conveyance→Potentially Lower Seepage 
But the economics and suitability must be considered.

9. Application Efficiency
Application efficiency describes how much of the water delivered to the field is stored in the effective
root zone.
Ea=WsWf×100  W f  100  
where:
- WsW s = water stored in the effective root zone,
- WfW f = water delivered/applied to the field.

10. Application Losses
Water applied to a field can be lost through:

Runoff
Water→Surface runoff 

Deep percolation
Water moves below the effective root zone.
Water→Below Root Zone 

Evaporation
Water is lost directly to the atmosphere.

Poor distribution
Some areas receive too much while others receive too little.

11. Deep Percolation
Deep percolation occurs when water moves below the effective crop root zone.
Excess Irrigation→Water moves below Root Zone→Crop cannot effectively use it 
It can cause:
- water wastage,
- nutrient leaching,
- groundwater contamination in some situations,
- rising water tables,
- waterlogging.
However, some deep percolation can be necessary for salinity management/leaching under
appropriate conditions.
Therefore:
Deep Percolation≠Always Waste 
The goal is to control it according to crop and soil requirements.

12. Runoff Loss
If water is applied faster than the soil can absorb it:
Application Rate>Infiltration Capacity→Runoff 
Runoff can carry away:

- soil,
- nutrients,
- pesticides,
- organic matter.
Therefore irrigation rate should be compatible with:
- soil infiltration,
- field slope,
- field condition,
- irrigation method.

13. Storage Efficiency
Storage efficiency measures how effectively the irrigation replenishes the soil-water deficit in the
effective root zone.
A commonly used conceptual expression is:
Es=Water stored in root zoneWater needed to refill the root zone×100        100  
It helps determine whether irrigation adequately refilled the soil profile.

14. Distribution Uniformity
An irrigation system should distribute water relatively evenly.
Imagine:
Plant 1

Plant 2

20 mm

50 mm

Plant 3
90 mm

Plant 4
30 mm

The field received water, but distribution is poor.
Some plants may suffer drought while others experience excessive water.
Therefore:
High Applied Water≠High Irrigation Quality 

15. Distribution Uniformity and Efficiency
A good system aims for:
Uniform Application+Appropriate Application Amount 
Uniformity is especially important for:
- drip,
- sprinkler,
- mechanized irrigation,
- fertigation.

16. Overall Irrigation Efficiency
At a conceptual level:
Eoverall≈Ec×Ea   E c E a  
when efficiencies are expressed as fractions.
For example:
Ec=0.80E c=0.80
and:
Ea=0.75E a=0.75
Then:
Eoverall=0.80×0.75=0.60E  overall =0.80 0.75=0.60
- r approximately:
60% 
This illustrates why improving only field application may not solve losses occurring before water
reaches the field.

17. Irrigation Efficiency vs Water-Use Efficiency
These are different.

Irrigation efficiency
Focuses on how effectively irrigation water supplied is captured/stored or beneficially used.

IE=Useful irrigation waterIrrigation water supplied  
     

Water-use efficiency
Relates crop production to water use.
WUE=Yield or biomassWater used        
Therefore:
IE≠WUE 

18. Water Productivity
Water productivity describes useful agricultural output per unit of water.
For example:
WP=YieldWater consumed  Water\ consumed   
- r other clearly defined water-use denominators.
The exact definition must always specify what “water used” means.

19. Irrigation Efficiency vs Water Productivity
A system can have high irrigation efficiency but not necessarily produce maximum yield.
For example:
- water may be stored efficiently,
- but nutrients may be deficient,
- pests may reduce yield,
- temperature may be unfavorable.
Thus:
High Irrigation Efficiency≠Guaranteed High Yield 
Similarly:
High Water Productivity≠Always High Irrigation Efficiency 

20. Relationship Between Irrigation Efficiency
and Irrigation Requirement
If the crop requires a net amount of water NIRNIR, gross irrigation requirement can be approximated
as:
GIR=NIREa  E a   
where EaE a is expressed as a fraction.

Example
If:
NIR=40 mmNIR=40 
and:
Ea=0.70E a=0.70
then:
GIR=400.70GIR=  0.70  GIR≈57.1 mm  
Thus improving efficiency can reduce the gross amount that must be applied to supply the required net
water.

21. Irrigation Efficiency and Soil Texture
Sandy soil
Generally:
- high infiltration,
- potentially high deep percolation,
- low water-holding capacity.
Therefore smaller, more frequent applications may be suitable in some systems.

Clay soil
Generally:
- slower infiltration,
- high water-holding capacity,
- potentially greater runoff/ponding if application is too rapid.

Loam
Often provides a balance between:
- infiltration,
- water storage,
- drainage.
But actual behavior depends on:
- structure,
- compaction,
- organic matter,
- layering,
- initial moisture.

22. Irrigation Efficiency and Soil Structure
Good structure can improve:
- infiltration,
- aeration,
- water storage,
- drainage.
Compaction can cause:
Compaction→Infiltration↓→Runoff/Ponding↑ 
- r restrict root growth and alter water distribution.

23. Irrigation Efficiency and Soil Water-Holding
Capacity
Available water:
AWC=θFC−θPWP -   
Total available water in the root zone:
TAW=(θFC−θPWP)Zr - )Z r  

where ZrZ r is effective root-zone depth.
The irrigation amount should account for how much water the root zone can actually store.

24. Over-Irrigation and Efficiency
Over-irrigation occurs when more water is supplied than the crop/root zone can beneficially use or
store.
Potential consequences:
Over−Irrigation→Deep Percolation+Runoff+Waterlogging+Nutrient Loss 
Therefore:
More Irrigation≠Better Irrigation 

25. Under-Irrigation and Efficiency
Under-irrigation can also be inefficient from a crop-production perspective.
If water does not adequately reach the effective root zone:
Under−Irrigation→Crop Stress→Yield Loss 
Therefore the goal is not simply to maximize the numerical efficiency percentage.
The goal is:
Adequate Root Zone Moisture+Minimum Unnecessary Loss 

26. Irrigation Scheduling and Efficiency
Scheduling is one of the most important factors.
A good schedule considers:
- crop ET,
- rainfall,
- soil moisture,
- root depth,
- crop stage,

- soil texture,
- irrigation method.
Crop ET:
ETc=KcET0 
Water balance:
ΔS=P+I+CR−R−D−ET 

27. Irrigation at the Right Time
Irrigation should generally occur before severe water stress develops.
The crop's critical moisture stages should receive particular attention.
Conceptually:
Soil Moisture+Crop Stage+ET+Weather→Irrigation Timing 

28. Irrigation Efficiency and Critical Moisture
Stages
When water is limited, applying water during highly sensitive stages can protect yield more effectively
than distributing scarce water uniformly.
For example:
Establishment→Flowering→Fruit/Pod/Grain Set→Filling 
Exact priorities depend on crop.

29. Flood Irrigation and Efficiency
Flood irrigation generally has less precise control over water distribution than localized systems.
Potential losses:
- runoff,
- deep percolation,
- evaporation,
- uneven distribution.

Efficiency can be improved by:
- proper land levelling,
- controlled inflow,
- suitable irrigation depth,
- appropriate field design,
- drainage,
- good scheduling.

30. Basin Irrigation and Efficiency
Basin irrigation can provide better control than uncontrolled flooding when basins are properly
designed and levelled.
Efficiency depends on:
- basin size,
- infiltration rate,
- water depth,
- soil texture,
- levelling,
- cutoff timing.

31. Furrow Irrigation and Efficiency
Furrow irrigation directs water through channels between crop rows.
Efficiency depends strongly on:
- furrow length,
- slope,
- inflow rate,
- infiltration rate,
- opportunity time,
- cutoff time.
A useful relationship is:

Opportunity Time=Recession Time−Advance Time 
Too much opportunity time can cause excessive infiltration and deep percolation near the head of the
field.

32. Sprinkler Irrigation and Efficiency
Sprinkler systems distribute water through pressurized spray.
Potential advantages:
- controlled application,
- relatively uniform application when designed properly,
- useful on suitable sloping terrain,
- no need for large open field channels.
Potential losses:
- evaporation,
- wind drift,
- runoff if application rate exceeds infiltration,
- pressure-related nonuniformity.
Thus:
Sprinkler Efficiency≠Automatically High 
System design and operation matter.

33. Drip Irrigation and Efficiency
Drip irrigation applies water near the plant/root zone.
Water→Emitter→Localized Root Zone 
Potential advantages:
- reduced unnecessary surface wetting,
- precise application,
- frequent irrigation,
- suitable for fertigation,

- high control over water placement.
Potential problems:
- emitter clogging,
- pressure variation,
- poor maintenance,
- salt accumulation at wetting-front boundaries,
- excessive application if scheduling is poor.
Therefore:
Drip System≠Good Management Automatically   

## 34. Micro-Irrigation
Micro-irrigation includes localized irrigation methods such as:
- drip irrigation,
- micro-sprinklers,
- bubblers,
- other low-discharge localized systems.
Its central principle is:
Apply Water Slowly and Locally 
Potentially improving:
- application control,
- water distribution,
- water productivity,
- fertigation management.

35. Irrigation Efficiency and Fertigation
When fertilizers are applied through irrigation water:
Irrigation+Fertilizer→Fertigation 
Efficient irrigation can improve nutrient placement.

But poor irrigation can cause:
Excess Water→Nutrient Leaching 
Therefore water and nutrient management should be integrated.

36. Irrigation Efficiency and Salinity
Efficient irrigation must also consider salt accumulation.
Inadequate leaching can allow salts to accumulate in the root zone.
Irrigation Water→Salt Input→Evaporation→Salt Concentration 
Where appropriate, sufficient drainage and leaching are needed.
Therefore:
Maximum Efficiency≠Minimum Water at All Costs 
  
Some water may need to pass through the root zone for salinity control.

37. Irrigation Efficiency and Waterlogging
Over-irrigation can produce:
Excess Water→Saturation→Air-Filled Pores↓→O2↓→Waterlogging Filled\ Pores  O 2  Waterlogging  
Therefore good irrigation efficiency helps reduce unnecessary waterlogging risk.

38. Irrigation Efficiency and Drought
Management
During drought, improving efficiency allows limited water to cover more crop area or better protect
critical stages.
Limited Water+Higher Useful Application→Greater Crop Benefit 
This makes irrigation efficiency a central component of drought management.

39. Factors Affecting Irrigation Efficiency
Soil factors
- texture,
- structure,
- infiltration rate,
- hydraulic conductivity,
- water-holding capacity,
- compaction,
- slope,
- layering.

Crop factors
- root depth,
- crop coefficient,
- growth stage,
- plant density,
- water sensitivity.

Climate factors
- temperature,
- humidity,
- wind,
- radiation,
- rainfall,
- VPD.

System factors
- irrigation method,
- pressure,
- discharge,
- pipe/channel condition,

- emitter uniformity,
- sprinkler distribution.

Management factors
- timing,
- duration,
- amount,
- field levelling,
- maintenance,
- scheduling.

40. The Role of Land Levelling
Uneven land causes uneven water distribution.
Uneven Field→Uneven Water Distribution 
Appropriate levelling/grading can improve:
- irrigation uniformity,
- application efficiency,
- surface drainage,
- water-use control.
Precision land levelling can be especially useful for suitable surface-irrigation systems.

41. The Role of Field Shape and Slope
Slope affects water movement.
Excessive slope can cause:
Flow Velocity↑→Runoff/Erosion Risk↑ 
Insufficient drainage on very flat land can cause:
Water Movement↓→Ponding Risk↑ 

Therefore field grading must match the irrigation method.

42. Irrigation Efficiency and Root-Zone Depth
A deeper root system can access more soil water:
TAW=(θFC−θPWP)Zr - )Z r  
But applying water below the effective root zone may become deep percolation.
Therefore irrigation depth should match:
Root Zone+Soil Water Holding Capacity 

43. Improving Irrigation Efficiency — Practical
Measures
1. Improve irrigation scheduling
Avoid unnecessary irrigation.

2. Measure soil moisture
Use:
- tensiometers,
- capacitance/FDR sensors,
- TDR,
- other appropriate methods.

3. Use appropriate irrigation methods
Select based on:
- soil,
- crop,
- terrain,
- water,
- economics.

4. Improve land levelling
Especially for surface irrigation.

5. Maintain channels/pipes
Reduce leakage.

6. Maintain drip emitters/sprinklers
Maintain uniform discharge.

7. Control application rate
Match infiltration capacity.

8. Reduce runoff
Use suitable field design.

9. Control deep percolation
Avoid excessive irrigation depth.

10. Use mulch/residue
Reduce soil evaporation.

44. Mulching and Irrigation Efficiency
Mulch reduces direct soil evaporation.
Mulch→Soil Evaporation↓ 
Consequently a greater fraction of available water can remain in the soil for crop use.

45. Weed Management
Weeds consume water.
Crop+Weeds→Competition for Water↑ 
Timely weed control can therefore increase the proportion of water available to the crop.

46. Irrigation Efficiency and Weather
Atmospheric demand changes water requirement.
High temperature and VPD can increase water demand:

Temperature↑+VPD↑→ET Demand↑ 
Therefore irrigation scheduling should respond to weather rather than using a rigid schedule in all
conditions.

47. Improving Efficiency Through Automation
Modern irrigation systems may use:
- soil moisture sensors,
- weather stations,
- flow meters,
- pressure sensors,
- automated valves,
- controllers,
- remote monitoring,
- satellite/remote-sensing information.
Conceptually:
Sensor→Decision→Irrigation→Feedback 
This creates a feedback-based irrigation system.

48. Precision Irrigation
Precision irrigation attempts to apply:
Right Amount+Right Location+Right Time  +   +
   
Potential technologies:
- variable-rate irrigation,
- soil moisture mapping,
## • Gps/Gnss,
- remote sensing,
- IoT sensors,

- automated controllers.

49. Economic Efficiency
Technical irrigation efficiency is not the only consideration.
An irrigation system should also be economically feasible.
Net Return=Gross Revenue−Total Cost 
A highly efficient system with very high installation and maintenance costs may not necessarily be
economically optimal for every farm.

50. Energy and Irrigation Efficiency
Pumping water requires energy.
Reducing unnecessary water application can reduce pumping demand.
Water Pumped↓→Energy Requirement↓ 
However, pressure-based systems such as drip and sprinkler require energy for pressurization.
Therefore total energy economics should be considered.

51. Irrigation Efficiency and Sustainability
Efficient irrigation contributes to:
- groundwater conservation,
- reduced energy use,
- reduced nutrient losses,
- reduced waterlogging,
- reduced salinity risk when properly designed,
- improved water availability,
- resilience to drought.
But efficiency must be considered within the entire water balance.

52. Important Concept: Beneficial vs NonBeneficial Water Use
Not every drop that leaves the irrigation system is necessarily wasted.
For example:
- some deep percolation may recharge groundwater,
- some water may be intentionally used for salinity leaching,
- some runoff may be captured downstream.
Therefore irrigation efficiency should be interpreted according to the defined objective and system
boundary.

53. A Complete Irrigation Water Pathway
Source
↓
Water Diversion
↓
Conveyance
↓
Field Delivery
↓
Field Application
↓
Infiltration
↓
Root-Zone Storage
↓
Root Uptake
↓
Transpiration
↓
Crop Production

Losses can occur at every stage.

54. Complete Efficiency Chain
Source→Conveyance→Application→Storage→Root Uptake→Crop Production 
      
The closer irrigation management gets to matching crop demand and root-zone storage, the more
effectively water resources are used.

55. Example of the Complete Concept
Suppose:
- 1,000 m³ water is diverted,
- 850 m³ reaches the field,
- 700 m³ is stored in the effective root zone.

Conveyance efficiency
Ec=8501000×100E c=  1000  100 Ec=85% 

Application efficiency
Ea=700850×100E a=  850  100 Ea≈82.35% 

Overall efficiency
Eoverall=0.85×0.8235E  overall =0.85 0.8235 Eoverall≈70%  70\% 
This demonstrates how losses at multiple stages combine.

56. Efficiency Improvement Strategy
A practical strategy is:
Measure→Identify Loss→Correct Loss→Schedule→Monitor 
For example:

If leakage is high
Improve conveyance.

If runoff is high
Improve field design/application rate.

If deep percolation is high
Reduce irrigation depth/frequency appropriately.

If distribution is uneven
Improve system design, pressure, levelling or maintenance.

If crop still experiences drought
Improve timing, root-zone targeting or scheduling.

57. Common Misconceptions

❌ "Irrigation efficiency means using the minimum possible water."
Not necessarily.
The goal is appropriate water use, not minimum water regardless of crop needs.

❌ "Drip irrigation always has 100% efficiency."
No.
Drip systems can still suffer from:
- clogging,
- poor uniformity,
- leakage,
- over-irrigation,
- evaporation,
- poor scheduling.

❌ "More irrigation gives higher efficiency."
No.
Excess water can increase runoff, deep percolation and waterlogging.

❌ "High irrigation efficiency guarantees high yield."
No.
Yield also depends on:

- nutrients,
- temperature,
- pests,
- disease,
- genetics,
- radiation,
- soil.

❌ "All deep percolation is wasted."
Not necessarily.
Some may be required for salinity management or may have other hydrological benefits depending on
the system.

❌ "Irrigation efficiency and WUE are the same."
They are different concepts.

58. Key Comparison Table
Concept
Main question
Conveyance efficiency How much water reaches the field?
Application efficiency How much applied water is stored in the root zone?
Storage efficiency
How well did irrigation refill the soil-water deficit?
Distribution uniformity How evenly was water distributed?
Irrigation efficiency
How effectively is irrigation water used/stored?
Water-use efficiency
How much biomass/yield is produced per unit water?
Water productivity
How much useful output is obtained per defined unit of water?

59. Key Formulas
Conveyance efficiency
Ec=Water delivered to fieldWater diverted from source×100   Water\ diverted\ from\ source   100  

Application efficiency
Ea=Water stored in effective root zoneWater applied×100   Water\ applied   100  

Storage efficiency
Es=Water storedWater required to refill root zone×100   Water\
required\ to\ refill\ root\ zone   100  

Overall efficiency
Eoverall≈Ec×Ea  E c E a  
when efficiencies are expressed as fractions.

Gross irrigation requirement
GIR=NIREa  E a   

Crop evapotranspiration
ETc=KcET0 

Soil-water balance
ΔS=P+I+CR−R−D−ET 

Total available water
TAW=(θFC−θPWP)Zr - )Z r  

Water-use efficiency
WUE=Yield or BiomassWater Used  Water\ Used   

60. Master Irrigation Efficiency Model
The complete concept can be represented as:
Water Source→Conveyance→Field Application→Root-Zone Storage→Root Uptake→Crop Growth→
Yield    & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\
& \\ &  
At every stage:
Avoid Unnecessary Loss   
while maintaining:
Adequate Root-Zone Moisture   

61. The 5-R Principle of Efficient Irrigation
A simple way to remember the concept:

Right Source+Right Amount+Right Time+Right Location+Right Method  +   +   +   +    
This produces:
Efficient + Uniform + Productive Irrigation   

62. Final Exam-Ready Definition
Irrigation efficiency is the measure of how effectively irrigation water supplied to an
agricultural system is conveyed, applied, distributed and stored in the effective crop
root zone for beneficial crop use, relative to the amount of water supplied. It is
improved by reducing unnecessary conveyance losses, runoff, excessive deep
percolation, evaporation and nonuniform application while maintaining adequate soil
moisture for crop growth.

63. Final Conceptual Summary
The fundamental idea is:
Irrigation Efficiency=Useful Irrigation WaterIrrigation Water Supplied×100  =         100  
But efficient irrigation is not simply about reducing water.
The real objective is:
Maximum Useful Crop BenefitwithMinimum Unnecessary Water Loss     
The complete management chain is:
Measure Water→Convey Efficiently→Apply Uniformly→Store in Root Zone→Schedule According to
Crop Demand→Monitor        

Most important memory
Efficient Irrigation = Right Amount + Right Time + Right Place + Right Method   
and:
More water applied does NOT mean better irrigation.   

## Module 11 — Water & Irrigation
## Management
Water Quality for Agriculture
## 1. Introduction
Water quality refers to the physical, chemical and biological characteristics of water that determine
whether it is suitable for a particular agricultural use.
In irrigation, water quality is especially important because irrigation water repeatedly enters the soil–
plant system. Poor-quality water can gradually affect:
- soil structure,
- salinity,
- sodicity,
- nutrient availability,
- root water uptake,
- plant growth,
- crop yield,
- irrigation equipment.
The central idea is:
Water Quality→Soil Condition→Root Environment→Plant Growth→Yield       

## 2. Definition
Agricultural water quality is the suitability of water for irrigation, livestock, crop
production or other agricultural uses based on its physical, chemical and biological
characteristics and their potential effects on crops, soil, irrigation systems and the
environment.
For irrigation specifically:
Irrigation Water Quality=Water suitability for sustainable crop and soil use  =    

One-line memory
Water quality = Is the water safe and suitable for the crop, soil, irrigation system and intended use?
  

3. Why Water Quality Matters
Water may look clean but still contain excessive:
- dissolved salts,
- sodium,
- chloride,
- boron,
- bicarbonate,
- carbonate,
- nutrients,
- suspended solids,
- pathogens,
- contaminants.
Therefore:
Clear Water≠Automatically Good Irrigation Water   

4. Major Dimensions of Agricultural Water
Quality
Water quality can be considered under three broad categories:
Physical+Chemical+Biological  +   +    

4.1 Physical quality
Includes:
- temperature,
- turbidity,

- suspended solids,
- sediment,
- color,
- total suspended material.
These characteristics are particularly important for irrigation equipment.

4.2 Chemical quality
Includes:
- pH,
- electrical conductivity,
- total dissolved solids,
- sodium,
- calcium,
- magnesium,
- chloride,
- boron,
- bicarbonate,
- carbonate,
- sulfate,
- nitrate,
- alkalinity,
- hardness.

4.3 Biological quality
Includes:
- bacteria,
- fungi,
- viruses,
- parasites,
- algae,

- other microorganisms.
Biological quality is particularly important when water is used for:
- fresh produce,
- washing produce,
- livestock,
- aquaculture,
- food-processing operations.

5. The Most Important Irrigation Water Quality
Parameters
For irrigation, pay particular attention to:
EC+SAR+Na+Cl+B+HCO3−+pH 
along with:
- TDS,
- calcium,
- magnesium,
- carbonate,
- nitrate,
- suspended solids,
- alkalinity.

6. Electrical Conductivity — EC
Electrical conductivity (EC) indicates the ability of water to conduct electrical current and is widely
used as an indicator of dissolved ionic salts.
Common units include:
dS/m  
and:
1 dS/m=1 mS/cm1 =1 
Higher EC generally indicates greater dissolved-salt concentration.

EC↑→Salinity Risk↑ 

7. Irrigation Water Salinity
Salinity refers to the concentration of soluble salts in irrigation water or soil.
Common ions include:
- Na+Na +
- Ca2+Ca  2+ 
- Mg2+Mg  2+ 
- K+K +
- Cl−Cl • SO42−SO 4  2- 
- HCO3−HCO 3 • CO32−CO 3  2- 
When saline water is repeatedly applied:
Salt Input+Evaporation−Adequate Leaching→Salt Accumulation 

8. How Salinity Affects Plants
Salinity creates an osmotic effect.
As salt concentration increases:
Soil Solution Salinity↑→Soil Water Potential↓→Root Water Uptake↓ 
Therefore a plant can experience physiological water stress even when soil appears moist.
This is sometimes described as:
Physiological drought  

9. Specific Ion Toxicity
Some ions can accumulate to harmful concentrations.
Important examples include:

- sodium,
- chloride,
- boron.
Potential effects include:
- leaf burn,
- marginal necrosis,
- reduced growth,
- reduced photosynthesis,
- nutrient imbalance.
Thus total salinity and specific-ion toxicity are separate concerns.
Total Salt Stress≠Specific Ion Toxicity   

10. Sodium Hazard
Excess sodium in irrigation water can damage soil structure.
Sodium can cause clay particles to disperse.
Na+↑→Clay Dispersion↑→Soil Structure↓ 
This can lead to:
- reduced infiltration,
- reduced hydraulic conductivity,
- surface sealing,
- poor aeration,
- waterlogging after irrigation/rainfall.

11. SAR — Sodium Adsorption Ratio
SAR evaluates the relative sodium hazard of irrigation water.
A commonly used expression is:
SAR=[Na+][Ca2+]+[Mg2+]2    ]+[Mg  2+ ]  2   
 

where concentrations are expressed in appropriate milliequivalent units, commonly mmolc/L or meq/L
depending on the reporting convention.
SAR increases when sodium is high relative to calcium and magnesium.
SAR↑→Sodicity Risk↑ 

12. Why Calcium and Magnesium Matter
Calcium can help maintain soil aggregation relative to excessive sodium.
Conceptually:
Ca2+→Clay Flocculation   Clay\ Flocculation  
whereas:
Na+→Clay Dispersion 
Therefore sodium should not be evaluated in isolation.

13. EC and SAR Must Be Considered Together
This is an important point.
Water with:
- high EC,
- high SAR,
can have different soil effects from water with:
- low EC,
- high SAR.
Therefore irrigation-water assessment should consider both:
Salinity Hazard+Sodicity/Infiltration Hazard  +
   
rather than relying on a single number.

14. Total Dissolved Solids — TDS
TDS represents the total concentration of dissolved substances in water.
It includes dissolved:

- salts,
- minerals,
- ions,
- other substances.
A commonly used approximate relationship is:
TDS≈k×EC 
where kk depends on the water's ionic composition and unit convention.
Therefore EC and TDS are related but are not identical measurements.

15. Water pH
Water pH describes hydrogen-ion activity:
pH=−log⁡10aH+ a  H +   
pH can affect:
- nutrient availability,
- chemical precipitation,
- irrigation equipment,
- fertigation,
- soil reaction.
However:
Water pH≠Soil pH 
They should be measured separately.

## 16. Alkalinity
Alkalinity represents the water's capacity to neutralize acids.
It is commonly associated with:
- bicarbonate,
- carbonate,
- hydroxide.
High alkalinity can contribute to:

- soil pH increase,
- carbonate/bicarbonate effects,
- precipitation of calcium and magnesium,
- emitter clogging in some irrigation systems.

## 17. Bicarbonate
Bicarbonate:
HCO3−HCO 3 can interact with calcium and magnesium.
Under certain conditions:
HCO3−+Ca2+→CaCO3 precipitation   CaCO 3   
This can reduce dissolved calcium and contribute to sodium hazard and irrigation-system problems.

## 18. Carbonate
Carbonate:
CO32−CO 3  2- 
is generally important in alkaline waters.
It can contribute to:
- high alkalinity,
- carbonate precipitation,
- changes in calcium/magnesium availability,
- soil alkalinity.

19. Residual Sodium Carbonate — RSC
RSC is used to assess the potential effect of carbonate and bicarbonate relative to calcium and
magnesium.
A commonly used expression is:

RSC=(HCO3−+CO32−)−(Ca2++Mg2+) ) - (Ca  2+ 
+Mg  2+ )  
with concentrations expressed in equivalent units.
Higher RSC indicates greater potential for precipitation of calcium and magnesium and therefore
greater sodium hazard under suitable conditions.

## 20. Chloride
Chloride:
Cl−Cl can be problematic at high concentrations.
It can cause:
- salinity,
- specific-ion toxicity,
- leaf injury when absorbed through roots,
- leaf burn when deposited through sprinkler irrigation.
Therefore chloride hazard depends partly on how irrigation water is applied.

## 21. Boron
Boron is an essential micronutrient at low concentrations but can become toxic at higher
concentrations.
Thus:
Boron=Essential at low concentration+Toxic at excessive concentration  =
  +    
Sensitive crops can be affected by relatively small increases compared with tolerant crops.

22. Nitrogen in Irrigation Water
Irrigation water may contain:
- nitrate,
- ammonium,
- organic nitrogen.

Nitrate contributes to the crop's total nitrogen supply.
Therefore:
N from Water+N from Fertilizer=Total N Input  +  
=    
Ignoring nutrient contribution from irrigation water can lead to over-fertilization.

23. Water Hardness
Hardness is mainly associated with:
- calcium,
- magnesium.
Hard water can cause:
- scale formation,
- precipitation,
- irrigation equipment problems,
- fertigation compatibility issues.
Hardness is not the same as salinity.
Hardness≠Salinity 

24. Suspended Solids
Suspended solids include:
- soil particles,
- silt,
- clay,
- organic debris,
- algae.
They are especially important for:
- drip irrigation,
- micro-irrigation,
- sprinkler nozzles,

- filters.
High suspended solids can cause:
Sediment→Filter/Emitter/Nozzle Clogging 

25. Biological Water Quality
Water can contain microorganisms that may cause:
- plant disease,
- food-safety problems,
- livestock disease,
- biofilm formation,
- clogging.
Biological quality becomes particularly important when irrigation water contacts edible portions of
fresh produce.

26. Algae and Biofilms
Surface water may contain algae and microorganisms.
These can contribute to:
- filter clogging,
- emitter blockage,
- pipeline biofilms,
- reduced irrigation uniformity.
Therefore water treatment and filtration may be needed for some systems.

27. Irrigation Water Quality and Soil
Poor-quality water can gradually modify soil properties.
The pathway can be:
Poor Water→Salt/Na Accumulation→Soil Chemical/Physical Change→Root Stress→Yield Reduction\
boxed  Poor\ Water  Salt/Na\ Accumulation  Soil\ Chemical/Physical\ Change
 Root\ Stress  Yield\ Reduction  

This is why water quality must be evaluated over the long term, not only for one irrigation event.

28. Water Quality and Soil Salinity
Repeated irrigation adds salts.
If salts are not adequately removed:
Salt Input>Salt Removal→Soil Salinity↑ 
Salt removal can occur through:
- leaching,
- drainage,
- appropriate rainfall,
- other water-management processes.

29. Water Quality and Sodicity
The pathway is:
High Na Water→Exchangeable Na↑→ESP↑→Clay Dispersion→Infiltration↓ 
ESP:
ESP=Exchangeable NaCEC×100  CEC  100  
Thus irrigation-water sodium hazard can eventually contribute to soil sodicity.

30. Water Quality and Infiltration
Poor-quality water, particularly with sodium hazard, can reduce infiltration.
Sodicity→Clay Dispersion→Pore Blocking→Infiltration↓ 
The result can be:
- ponding,
- runoff,
- poor drainage,

- poor root aeration.

31. Water Quality and Soil Structure
Good soil structure supports:
- infiltration,
- aeration,
- root growth,
- water storage.
Excess sodium can destabilize aggregates.
Therefore:
Water Quality→Soil Structure→Water Movement 

32. Water Quality and Nutrient Availability
Water chemistry can influence soil nutrient behavior.
For example:
- high pH can reduce availability of some micronutrients,
- bicarbonate can influence Fe/Zn availability in some soils,
- salinity can create nutrient imbalance,
- excessive sodium can interfere with potassium/calcium nutrition.
Therefore:
Water Quality→Soil Chemistry→Nutrient Availability 

33. Water Quality and Root Uptake
Roots require water in a chemically suitable environment.
Poor water quality can cause:
- osmotic stress,
- ion toxicity,
- nutrient imbalance,

- reduced root growth,
- reduced water uptake.
Thus:
Poor Water Quality→Root Stress→Water/Nutrient Uptake↓ 

34. Water Quality and Crop Selection
Different crops have different tolerance levels to:
- salinity,
- sodium,
- chloride,
- boron,
- alkalinity.
Therefore water quality must be evaluated relative to:
Crop+Soil+Climate+Irrigation Method + + +
   
There is no single universal water-quality value that makes water “good” for every crop.

35. Crop Tolerance
A water source may be usable for a relatively salt-tolerant crop but unsuitable for a sensitive crop.
Therefore:
Water Suitability=f(Water,Crop,Soil,Management) 
This is an important agricultural principle.

36. Water Quality and Irrigation Method
The same water can create different risks under different application methods.

Drip
Localized application but potential:
- emitter clogging,

- salt accumulation around wetting-front boundaries.

Sprinkler
Potential:
- leaf chloride/sodium injury,
- evaporation,
- nozzle clogging.

Surface irrigation
Potential:
- salinity accumulation,
- uneven distribution,
- infiltration problems.
Thus:
Water Quality+Irrigation Method→Actual Risk 

37. Salinity Under Drip Irrigation
Drip irrigation does not eliminate salinity.
As water moves and evaporates:
Salt→Wetting Front/Outer Edge→Salt Accumulation 
Appropriate irrigation and occasional leaching, where required, are important.

38. Water Quality and Sprinkler Irrigation
When sprinkler water contains high concentrations of certain ions:
Sprinkler Wetting→Ion Deposition on Leaves→Potential Foliar Injury 
This can be particularly relevant for sodium and chloride-sensitive crops.

39. Water Quality and Fertigation
Water chemistry can influence fertilizer compatibility.

Potential problems include:
- precipitation,
- clogging,
- pH changes,
- reduced nutrient availability.
Therefore fertigation requires compatibility assessment.

40. Water Quality and Irrigation Equipment
Poor-quality water can cause:
- emitter clogging,
- nozzle blockage,
- filter loading,
- scaling,
- corrosion,
- biofilm formation.
Therefore:
Water Quality→Equipment Maintenance Requirement 

## 41. Filtration
Filtration is particularly important in micro-irrigation.
Possible filtration systems include:
- screen filters,
- disc filters,
- media/sand filters,
- other suitable treatment systems.
The correct filter depends on:
- particle size,
- organic matter,

- algae,
- system discharge,
- emitter requirements.

42. Water Treatment
Depending on the problem, treatment may involve:
- filtration,
- sedimentation,
- aeration,
- chemical treatment,
- acidification,
- chlorination/disinfection where appropriate,
- other specialized treatment.
Treatment must be selected based on the actual water-quality problem.

43. Water Quality Testing
A proper assessment begins with representative water sampling.
Basic workflow:
Sample→LaboratoryAnalysis→Interpret→AssessCrop/SoilRisk→Management 

44. Water Sample Collection
A representative sample should reflect the water actually entering the irrigation system.
Consider:
- source,
- depth,
- pumping conditions,
- season,

- storage tank,
- treatment status.
For example, groundwater quality may differ from stored surface water.

45. Parameters to Test
A basic irrigation-water analysis may include:
Parameter

Main concern
pH
Acidity/alkalinity
EC
Salinity
TDS
Dissolved solids
SAR
Sodium hazard
Na
Sodicity/specific ion toxicity
Ca
Soil structure/nutrient
Mg
Nutrient/salinity
Cl
Salinity/toxicity
HCO3−HCO 3 - Alkalinity/infiltration
CO32−CO 3  2-  Alkalinity
B
Toxicity
Nitrate
Nutrient/environment
Suspended solids Clogging
Microorganisms
Biological/food safety
Additional tests may be required based on the water source and intended use.

46. Water Quality Interpretation
A laboratory report should not be interpreted by looking at only one parameter.
For example:
EC+SAR+RSC+Specific Ions+Crop+Soil 
should be considered together.
The same EC may have different implications depending on:
- crop tolerance,
- soil texture,
- drainage,
- rainfall,

- irrigation frequency,
- leaching,
- climate.

47. Salinity Management
When irrigation water has salinity concerns, possible strategies include:

1. Improve drainage
Drainage→Salt Removal 

2. Appropriate leaching
Good Quality Water→Root Zone→Salt Leaching 
where sufficient suitable water is available.

3. Select tolerant crops/varieties
4. Improve irrigation scheduling
5. Avoid unnecessary evaporation
6. Monitor soil EC

48. Sodicity Management
Where sodium hazard is causing soil sodicity, management may include:
- improving calcium supply where appropriate,
- gypsum application when soil diagnosis indicates suitability,
- leaching,
- drainage,
- appropriate irrigation management,
- selecting tolerant crops.
The important pathway is:
Diagnosis→Calcium Source if Appropriate→Leaching→Drainage 
    

49. Gypsum and Sodicity
Gypsum:
CaSO4⋅2H2O 
can supply calcium.
Conceptually:
Ca2+→Replace exchangeable Na+→Na+ in soil solution→Leaching   Na +  Na +    
But gypsum should not be applied simply because irrigation water contains sodium.
The soil and water system should first be diagnosed.

50. Managing High Bicarbonate Water
Depending on the situation, management may involve:
- monitoring alkalinity,
- acidification where technically appropriate,
- preventing precipitation,
- filtration,
- managing soil pH,
- appropriate fertilizer selection.
Treatment must be based on actual water chemistry.

51. Managing Clogging
For micro-irrigation:
Water Analysis→Filtration/Treatment→Emitter Maintenance 
Possible sources of clogging:
- sediment,
- algae,
- bacteria,

- precipitated minerals,
- organic matter.

52. Water Quality and Drainage
Water quality and drainage are strongly connected.
If saline irrigation water is applied without adequate drainage:
Salt Input+Poor Drainage→Salt Accumulation 
Therefore:
Salinity Management=Water Quality+Leaching+Drainage 

53. Water Quality and Rainfall
Rainfall can sometimes help dilute or leach salts.
But effectiveness depends on:
- rainfall amount,
- rainfall intensity,
- soil infiltration,
- drainage,
- root-zone depth,
- salt distribution.
Therefore rainfall does not automatically eliminate salinity.

54. Water Quality and Climate
Hot, dry conditions increase evaporation.
Therefore:
High Salt Water+High Evaporative Demand→Greater Salinity Risk 
Climate must therefore be included when assessing irrigation-water suitability.

55. Water Quality and Water Conservation
Water conservation should not mean using poor-quality water without considering long-term soil
damage.
The correct principle is:
Water Saving+Water Quality+Drainage+Crop Suitability  +   +   +    
A small quantity of very poor-quality water can sometimes create greater long-term soil problems than
a larger quantity of suitable water.

56. Water Quality and Drought Management
During drought, farmers may use alternative water sources.
Before using a new source, its quality should be checked.
For example:
Drought→Alternative Water Source→Water Quality Test→Suitability Assessment 
This prevents solving a short-term water shortage by creating a long-term salinity or sodicity problem.

57. Water Quality and Waterlogging
Poor drainage combined with irrigation can create waterlogging.
Excess Irrigation+Poor Drainage→Waterlogging 
And:
Waterlogging→Root Oxygen↓ 
Therefore water quality, irrigation quantity and drainage must be managed together.

58. Water Quality and Soil Health
Long-term irrigation with poor-quality water can affect:

Physical health
- structure,

- infiltration,
- aeration,
- aggregation.

Chemical health
- salinity,
- sodicity,
- pH,
- nutrient availability.

Biological health
- microbial activity,
- root environment,
- biological processes.
Therefore:
Water Quality→Soil Health 

59. Water Quality Decision Framework
Identify Water Source
↓
Collect Representative Sample
↓
Laboratory Testing
↓
┌──────────────────────────────┐
│ EC / TDS

│

│ pH

│

│ SAR / Na

│

│ Ca / Mg

│

│ Cl / B

│

│ HCO₃ / CO₃ / RSC

│

│ Suspended Solids

│

│ Biological Quality

│

└──────────────────────────────┘
↓
Assess Crop + Soil + Climate
↓
Identify Hazards
↓
Select Management
↓
Monitor Water + Soil
↓
Adjust Irrigation

60. Main Water Quality Hazards
Hazard
Salinity
Sodicity
Specific-ion toxicity

contamination

Main indicator(s)
