risk

61. Most Important Distinction: Salinity vs
Sodicity
Salinity
Too many soluble salts   
Main indicator:
EC 
Main plant problem:
Osmotic Stress 

Sodicity
Too much exchangeable sodium   
Water indicator:
SAR 
Soil indicator:
ESP 
Main soil problem:
Clay Dispersion→Infiltration↓ 

62. Water Quality vs Water Quantity
These are different dimensions.

Water quantity
How much water is available?

Water quality
How suitable is that water?
Therefore:
Water Supply=Quantity+Quality 
A farm needs both sufficient quantity and suitable quality.

63. Water Quality vs Irrigation Efficiency
Irrigation efficiency asks:
How effectively is supplied irrigation water used?
Water quality asks:
How suitable is the water for the crop, soil and irrigation system?
They interact:
Water Quality+Irrigation Efficiency→Sustainable Irrigation 

64. Water Quality vs Soil Quality
Water quality describes the irrigation water.
Soil quality describes the soil's physical, chemical and biological condition.
But they interact continuously:
Irrigation Water→Soil→Plant 
Poor water quality can gradually degrade soil quality.

65. How to Improve Overall Water Quality
Management
A practical strategy is:

Step 1 — Test
Know what is actually in the water.

Step 2 — Identify the hazard
Is the main issue:
- salinity?
- sodium?
- bicarbonate?
- boron?
- chloride?
- sediment?
- microorganisms?

Step 3 — Match with soil
Check:
- texture,
- drainage,
- CEC,
- pH,
- EC,

- ESP/SAR.

Step 4 — Match with crop
Determine crop tolerance.

Step 5 — Select irrigation method
Consider:
- drip,
- sprinkler,
- furrow,
- basin,
- other systems.

Step 6 — Manage
Use appropriate:
- filtration,
- drainage,
- leaching,
- amendments,
- scheduling,
- crop selection.

Step 7 — Monitor
Test both:
Water+Soil 
- ver time.

66. Complete Water Quality Management
Equation
Conceptually:
Water Suitability=f(Water Chemistry,Crop,Soil,Climate,Irrigation Method,Drainage,Management)
  = f(  ,  ,  ,  ,
 ,  ,   )  

This is more useful than asking whether water is simply “good” or “bad.”

67. Common Mistakes

❌ Clear water is automatically safe
❌ EC alone tells everything
❌ High EC and high SAR mean the same thing
Not necessarily.

EC mainly indicates salinity; sodium, chloride, boron and alkalinity require separate consideration.

No.
EC → salinity.
SAR → relative sodium hazard.

❌ Water pH is the same as soil pH
❌ Salinity and sodicity are identical
❌ Drip irrigation eliminates salinity
No.

They are different problems that can occur together.

No.
Salts can accumulate around the edges of the wetted zone.

❌ Gypsum fixes every poor-quality water problem
No.
Gypsum is mainly relevant to calcium/sodicity management when diagnosis supports its use.

❌ More irrigation automatically removes salts
❌ One water-quality standard works for every crop
Not without suitable drainage and appropriate water quality.

Crop tolerance and soil conditions differ.

68. Key Equations
pH
pH=−log⁡10aH+ a  H +   

SAR
SAR=[Na+][Ca2+]+[Mg2+]2    ]+[Mg  2+ ]  2   
 

RSC
RSC=(HCO3−+CO32−)−(Ca2++Mg2+) ) - (Ca  2+ 
+Mg  2+ )  

ESP
ESP=Exchangeable NaCEC×100  CEC  100  

Water salinity indicator
EC↑→Salinity Risk↑ 

Salinity mechanism
Salinity↑→Water Potential↓→Root Water Uptake↓ 

Sodicity mechanism
Na↑→Clay Dispersion↑→Infiltration↓ 

69. Master Water Quality Model
The complete agricultural pathway is:
Water Source→Water Quality→Irrigation→Soil→Root Zone→Plant→Yield         
Poor water quality can produce:
Salinity+Sodicity+Ion Toxicity+Nutrient Imbalance+Clogging  +  
+   +   +    
while appropriate management can reduce these risks.

70. Exam-Ready Definition
Water quality in agriculture is the suitability of water for irrigation and other
agricultural uses based on its physical, chemical and biological properties and their
effects on crops, soil, irrigation systems, livestock and the environment. For irrigation,
important parameters include EC, TDS, pH, SAR, sodium, chloride, boron,
bicarbonate, carbonate, calcium, magnesium and suspended or biological
contaminants.

71. Final Conceptual Summary
The most important idea is:
Good Irrigation Water = Adequate Quantity + Suitable Quality   
For irrigation, remember the major hazards:
EC → Salinity  
SAR/Na → Sodicity/Infiltration Hazard  
Cl/B → Specific-Ion Toxicity  
HCO3−/CO32−→Alkalinity/Precipitation Risk  3 -/  3  2- 
  
Suspended Solids/Biological Matter → Clogging Risk   
And the complete decision principle is:
Test Water→Identify Hazards→Match Crop + Soil→Choose Irrigation Method→Manage→Monitor W
ater + Soil        

Final one-line memory
Water Quality = The chemical, physical and biological suitability of water for the crop, soil, irrigation s
ystem and intended agricultural use.   

## Module 11 — Water & Irrigation
## Management
Saline Irrigation Water
## 1. Introduction
Saline irrigation water is irrigation water containing a sufficiently high concentration of dissolved
salts that, depending on the crop, soil, climate, drainage and irrigation management, it can create
salinity stress in plants or cause salts to accumulate in the root zone.
The key point is that saline water is not simply “water with salt.” The agricultural problem develops
when salt enters the soil faster than it can be removed, or when the salt concentration becomes high
enough to restrict plant water uptake.
Saline Water→Salt Addition to Soil→Root-Zone Salinity→Osmotic/Ion Stress→Growth and Yield Re
duction       

## 2. Definition
Saline irrigation water is water containing elevated concentrations of dissolved salts
that may adversely affect crop growth, soil properties or irrigation-system
performance when used for irrigation, depending on crop tolerance and management
conditions.

One-line memory
Saline irrigation water = irrigation water with elevated dissolved salts that can create root-zone salinity.
  

3. What Makes Irrigation Water Saline?
Dissolved salts commonly include ions such as:

Cations
- Na+Na + — sodium
- Ca2+Ca  2+  — calcium
- Mg2+Mg  2+  — magnesium
- K+K + — potassium

Anions
- Cl−Cl - — chloride
- SO42−SO 4  2-  — sulfate
- HCO3−HCO 3 - — bicarbonate
- CO32−CO 3  2-  — carbonate
The total concentration of dissolved ions contributes to water salinity.

4. Main Indicator — Electrical Conductivity
Electrical conductivity (EC) is one of the most widely used indicators of salinity.
Common unit:
dS/m  
Higher EC generally means greater dissolved-ion concentration:
EC↑→Salinity Risk↑ 
However, EC alone does not describe every water-quality hazard.

5. EC vs TDS
EC
Measures electrical conductivity.

TDS
Represents total dissolved solids.
They are related, but not identical.
A rough relationship is:
TDS≈k×EC 
where kk depends on the composition of the water and the units used.
Therefore:
EC≠TDS 

6. Saline Water vs Sodic Water
This distinction is extremely important.

Salinity
Concern:
Total soluble salts  
Primary water indicator:
EC 
Main plant problem:
Osmotic stress  

Sodicity
Concern:
Excess sodium relative to calcium and magnesium  
Important water indicator:
SAR 
Main soil problem:
Clay dispersion and reduced infiltration  
Thus:
Salinity≠Sodicity   
A water source can have:
- salinity without severe sodicity,
- sodicity hazard without very high total salinity,
- both simultaneously.

7. How Saline Irrigation Water Enters the Soil
Every irrigation event introduces salts.
Suppose irrigation water contains dissolved salts:
Irrigation Water→Water + Dissolved Salts    

The water may later:
- be taken up by plants,
- evaporate,
- transpire,
- drain,
- move below the root zone.
But salts do not evaporate with the water.
Therefore:
Water Loss→Salt Concentration Remaining 

8. Salt Balance
A simplified conceptual salt balance is:
ΔSalt=Salt Inputs−Salt Outputs 
Therefore:

If
Salt Inputs>Salt OutputsSalt\ Inputs>Salt\ Outputs
then:
Root-Zone Salinity↑ Zone\ Salinity  

If
Salt Inputs≈Salt OutputsSalt\ Inputs Salt\ Outputs
then salinity may remain relatively stable.

If
Salt Outputs>Salt InputsSalt\ Outputs>Salt\ Inputs
then soil salinity can potentially decline.

9. Why Salts Accumulate
Salt accumulation becomes more likely when:
- evaporation is high,

- rainfall is low,
- irrigation water is saline,
- drainage is poor,
- groundwater is shallow and saline,
- leaching is inadequate.
The general pathway is:
Saline Water+High Evaporation+Poor Drainage→Salt Accumulation 

10. Physiological Drought
One of the most important effects of saline water is osmotic stress.
As salt concentration in the soil solution increases:
Soil Salinity↑→Soil Water Potential↓→Root Water Uptake↓ 
The soil may physically contain water, but the plant has greater difficulty extracting it.
This is why salinity can produce:
Physiological drought  

11. Salinity and Water Potential
Plant water movement depends on water-potential gradients.
Increasing dissolved salt concentration makes soil solution water potential more negative.
Therefore:
More Salt→Lower Osmotic Potential→Greater Difficulty Extracting Water 
   
Consequences include:
- reduced cell expansion,
- stomatal closure,
- reduced leaf growth,
- reduced photosynthesis,
- wilting-like symptoms,

- reduced yield.

12. Two Major Components of Salinity Stress
Salinity can affect plants through two broad mechanisms:

12.1 Osmotic stress
Occurs relatively quickly.
Salt↑→Water Uptake↓ 

12.2 Ion toxicity
Specific ions accumulate in plant tissues to damaging concentrations.
Common concerns include:
- sodium,
- chloride,
- boron.
Thus:
Salinity Stress=Osmotic Stress+Ion Toxicity 

13. Specific Ion Toxicity
Sodium
Excess sodium can:
- disturb cellular ion balance,
- compete with potassium,
- damage leaves,
- affect enzymes and membranes.

Chloride
Excess chloride can:
- accumulate in leaves,
- cause leaf-edge/marginal injury,
- interfere with nutrient balance.

Boron
Boron is essential at low concentrations but toxic at excessive concentrations.
Boron=Essential micronutrient + Potential toxin at excessive concentration 
=   

14. Salinity and Nutrient Imbalance
Salinity can interfere with nutrient uptake.
For example, excessive sodium may interfere with potassium nutrition.
High salinity can also alter availability and uptake of:
- nitrogen,
- phosphorus,
- potassium,
- calcium,
- magnesium,
- micronutrients.
Therefore:
Salinity→Ion Imbalance→Nutrient Uptake Problems 

15. Effect on Germination
Saline irrigation water can increase salt concentration around seeds.
This can cause:
- delayed imbibition,
- reduced germination,
- poor emergence,
- abnormal seedlings.
Conceptually:
Salinity↑→Water Uptake by Seed↓→Germination↓ 

16. Effect on Seedling Growth
Young plants are often particularly sensitive to unfavorable soil-water conditions.
Salinity can cause:
- poor root growth,
- reduced shoot growth,
- poor leaf expansion,
- weak establishment.
Therefore saline irrigation during establishment can be particularly problematic for sensitive crops.

17. Effect on Vegetative Growth
Salinity can reduce:
- leaf expansion,
- plant height,
- branching,
- tillering,
- biomass accumulation.
The general pathway is:
Salinity→Water Uptake↓+Ion Stress→Growth↓ 

18. Effect on Photosynthesis
Salinity can reduce photosynthesis through:
- stomatal closure,
- reduced CO₂ entry,
- chlorophyll effects,
- ion toxicity,
- oxidative stress.

Salinity→Stomatal Conductance↓→CO2 Uptake↓→Photosynthesis↓ 

19. Effect on Flowering and Reproduction
Salinity can affect:
- flower initiation,
- pollen viability,
- pollen germination,
- fertilization,
- fruit set,
- seed development.
Thus:
Salinity During Reproductive Stage→Fruit/Seed Set↓ 
Severity depends strongly on crop and developmental stage.

20. Effect on Grain Filling
In grain crops:
Salinity→Photosynthesis↓+Leaf Senescence↑→Assimilate Supply↓ 
which can reduce:
- grain filling rate,
- grain filling duration,
- grain weight.

21. Effect on Fruit Crops
Salinity can affect:
- flowering,
- fruit set,

- fruit enlargement,
- leaf growth,
- yield,
- fruit quality.
In severe conditions:
Root Stress→Canopy Reduction→Yield Reduction 

22. Salinity and Root Growth
Roots are directly exposed to saline soil solution.
Potential effects:
- reduced root elongation,
- altered root architecture,
- reduced water uptake,
- ion accumulation,
- oxidative stress.
However, some salt-tolerant plants can modify root growth and ion regulation to maintain function.

23. Salt Exclusion
Some plants restrict sodium or chloride entry into shoots.
Root→Ion Exclusion/Selective Transport→Shoot 
This can help protect leaves.

24. Ion Compartmentation
Plants can sometimes store excess ions in vacuoles.
Excess Na+→Cellular Compartmentation→Cytoplasmic Protection 
This is one mechanism contributing to salt tolerance.

25. Osmotic Adjustment
Salt-tolerant plants may accumulate compatible solutes such as:
- proline,
- soluble sugars,
- certain amino acids.
These help maintain cellular water relations without severely disrupting metabolism.
Osmotic Adjustment→Better Cellular Water Status 

26. Antioxidant Defense
Salinity can increase reactive oxygen species:
Salinity→Oxidative Stress→ROS↑ 
Plants use antioxidant systems such as:
- superoxide dismutase,
- catalase,
- ascorbate peroxidase,
- other antioxidant compounds.

27. Crop Salt Tolerance
Crops differ substantially in their response to salinity.
Some are relatively sensitive, while others are more tolerant.
Therefore:
Water Suitability≠Same for Every Crop   
It depends on:
Water Quality + Crop + Soil + Climate + Management   

28. Growth Stage Matters
A crop may have different salt sensitivity at different stages.
Therefore:
Salt Tolerance=f(Crop,Genotype,Growth Stage,Duration) 
This is why the same saline water can produce different outcomes depending on when it is applied.

29. Soil Texture and Saline Water
Sandy soils
Generally:
- rapid infiltration,
- lower water-holding capacity,
- potentially easier leaching if drainage is good.

Clay soils
Generally:
- greater water retention,
- slower infiltration,
- potentially more difficult leaching,
- greater risk of waterlogging if drainage is poor.

Loam
Often intermediate.
But actual behavior depends on:
- soil structure,
- compaction,
- organic matter,
- layering,
- drainage.

30. Drainage Is Critical
This is one of the most important rules for saline irrigation.
Saline Irrigation+Poor Drainage→Salt Accumulation 
With adequate drainage:
Saline Irrigation+Leaching+Drainage→Salt Removal Potential 
Therefore:
Leaching without drainage can simply move the problem rather than solve it.   

## 31. Leaching
Leaching means moving soluble salts below the effective root zone using water.
Conceptually:
Fresh/Suitable Water→Root Zone→Salt Dissolution→Downward Movement→Drainage 
The objective is:
Root-Zone Salt↓ Zone\ Salt  

32. Leaching Requirement
Where saline irrigation water is used, additional water may sometimes be required to control salt
accumulation.
The exact calculation depends on:
- irrigation-water salinity,
- desired root-zone salinity,
- crop tolerance,
- evapotranspiration,
- soil,
- drainage.
A commonly used conceptual form is:

LR=Salt concentration in irrigation waterPermissible salt concentration in drainage/root-zone water xed  LR=         
Specific equations and assumptions vary by method and crop.

33. Important Warning About Leaching
Leaching requires:
1. sufficient suitable water,
2. adequate soil permeability,
3. functioning drainage.
Without drainage:
Leaching Water→Waterlogging 
and salts may remain within the system.

34. Salinity and Groundwater
A shallow groundwater table can contribute salts upward.
The pathway is:
Saline Groundwater→Capillary Rise→Evaporation→Salt Accumulation 
Therefore saline irrigation may become more problematic where the groundwater table is shallow.

35. Capillary Rise
Fine-textured soils can support significant capillary movement.
If groundwater contains salts:
Groundwater→Capillary Rise→Root Zone→Evaporation→Salt Concentration 

36. Irrigation Scheduling with Saline Water
Scheduling becomes particularly important.

Avoid:
- unnecessary irrigation,
- excessive evaporation,
- severe water deficits,
- excessive salt concentration.
Scheduling should consider:
- soil moisture,
- crop stage,
- ET,
- water salinity,
- rainfall,
- drainage.

37. Saline Water and Irrigation Frequency
In some systems, frequent smaller applications can help maintain a more stable root-zone water status,
but the effect on salt distribution depends on the irrigation method and soil.
Therefore:
Irrigation Frequency≠Universal Rule 
It must be designed according to the soil–water–crop system.

38. Saline Water and Drip Irrigation
Drip irrigation can be useful for managing saline water because it allows precise root-zone water
application.
Potential advantages:
- localized wetting,
- frequent controlled irrigation,
- reduced unnecessary surface wetting.
However, salts can accumulate near the edges of the wetted zone.
Drip→Localized Wetting→Salt Redistribution 

Therefore periodic leaching and monitoring may be required.

39. Saline Water and Sprinkler Irrigation
Sprinkler irrigation can create additional concerns when water contains high concentrations of:
- sodium,
- chloride,
- boron.
Repeated leaf wetting can result in foliar ion deposition.
Sprinkler+Saline Water→Leaf Wetting→Potential Ion Injury 
Crop sensitivity and climate strongly influence the risk.

40. Saline Water and Surface Irrigation
Surface irrigation methods such as:
- furrow,
- basin,
- border,
can be used with saline water under appropriate conditions.
However, poor distribution or excessive evaporation can promote uneven salt accumulation.
Uneven Irrigation→Uneven Salt Distribution 

41. Salinity Under Furrow Irrigation
Salt movement can occur away from the furrow toward ridges and upper soil zones as water
evaporates.
Therefore crop placement matters.
Properly designed furrow irrigation can sometimes place water away from sensitive plant stems, but
salt redistribution must still be monitored.

42. Salinity and Raised Beds
Raised beds can help with drainage in suitable soils.
However:
Raised Bed≠Salt Removal 
Actual salt management still requires appropriate:
- water quality,
- leaching,
- drainage,
- soil monitoring.

43. Saline Water and Fertilizers
Saline irrigation already contributes dissolved ions.
Adding excessive fertilizer can increase the total salt concentration around roots.
Therefore:
Saline Water+Excess Fertilizer→Osmotic Stress Risk↑ 
Balanced, soil-test-based fertilization is important.

44. Salinity and Fertigation
Fertigation can provide precise nutrient delivery through drip systems, but fertilizer salts add to the
dissolved-ion concentration.
Therefore fertigation should account for:
- irrigation-water EC,
- fertilizer concentration,
- crop sensitivity,
- soil EC.

45. Monitoring Soil Salinity
Water quality should not be the only measurement.
Monitor:
Irrigation Water EC+Soil/Root-Zone EC  +    
This tells whether salts are accumulating over time.

46. Soil EC vs Water EC
Water EC
Describes salinity of irrigation water.

Soil EC
Describes salinity of soil solution/extract under a specified method.
Therefore:
Water EC≠Soil EC 
A saline water source may or may not produce severe soil salinity depending on:
- rainfall,
- irrigation amount,
- leaching,
- drainage,
- evaporation,
- crop water use.

47. ECe and Irrigation Water EC
ECe commonly refers to the electrical conductivity of the soil saturation extract.
It is widely used in soil salinity assessment.
It should not be directly treated as identical to irrigation-water EC because they represent different
materials and measurement contexts.
ECw≠ECe 
where:

- ECwEC w = irrigation-water EC,
- ECeECe = soil saturation-extract EC.

48. Water Quality Testing
Before using a questionable water source, test:
- EC,
- TDS,
- pH,
- sodium,
- calcium,
- magnesium,
- chloride,
- bicarbonate,
- carbonate,
- boron,
- SAR,
- RSC where relevant.

49. SAR of Saline Irrigation Water
SAR evaluates sodium relative to calcium and magnesium:
SAR=[Na+][Ca2+]+[Mg2+]2    ]+[Mg  2+ ]  2   
 
High SAR may create soil infiltration/structure problems.
Therefore a water source should be evaluated for both:
EC+SAR   
rather than EC alone.

50. RSC
Residual sodium carbonate evaluates bicarbonate/carbonate relative to calcium and magnesium:

RSC=(HCO3−+CO32−)−(Ca2++Mg2+) ) - (Ca  2+ 
+Mg  2+ )  
High RSC can increase sodium hazard under suitable soil-water conditions because calcium and
magnesium may precipitate.

51. Water Quality Classification
There is no single universal statement such as:
“Above X EC, water is always unusable.”
Interpretation depends on:
- crop,
- soil,
- climate,
- drainage,
- irrigation method,
- leaching,
- duration of use.
Therefore water-quality guidelines should always be interpreted using the relevant standard and crop
context.

52. Management Strategy for Saline Irrigation
Water
A complete strategy is:
Test→Assess→Select→Manage→Monitor       

53. Step 1 — Test the Water
Determine:
EC, TDS, SAR, Na, Cl, B, HCO3−, CO32−, pH ,\ pH  
and other parameters as needed.

54. Step 2 — Test the Soil
Monitor:
- soil EC,
- pH,
- ESP/SAR-related indicators,
- texture,
- drainage,
- organic carbon,
- water-holding capacity.

55. Step 3 — Select a Suitable Crop
If water quality is poor, crop choice becomes important.
Crop Selection→Water Quality Compatibility 
Salt-tolerant crops may be more suitable than salt-sensitive crops under the same water conditions.

56. Step 4 — Improve Drainage
Where salts must be leached:
Drainage→Salt Removal Pathway 
Possible systems include:
- surface drainage,
- subsurface drainage,
- field drains,
- collector drains.

57. Step 5 — Apply Leaching When Appropriate
Use suitable-quality water to move salts below the root zone.

But:
Leaching+No Drainage≠Reliable Reclamation 

58. Step 6 — Improve Irrigation Efficiency
Use appropriate:
- scheduling,
- drip,
- sprinkler,
- furrow,
- basin,
- other systems.
The objective is:
Adequate Root Zone Moisture+Controlled Salt Accumulation 

59. Step 7 — Reduce Evaporation
Methods include:
- mulch,
- crop residues,
- appropriate canopy,
- conservation tillage,
- efficient irrigation.
Why?
Evaporation→Water Loss+Salt Concentration 
Reducing unnecessary evaporation can help reduce salt concentration near the soil surface.

60. Step 8 — Manage Fertilizers
Use:

- soil testing,
- balanced nutrition,
- appropriate fertilizer rates,
- appropriate placement,
- suitable fertigation concentrations.
Avoid unnecessary salt loading.

61. Step 9 — Monitor Soil EC
After saline-water irrigation begins:
Water EC→Soil EC→Trend Analysis 
The important question is not only:
“How saline is my water?”
but also:
“What is happening to salinity in my root zone over time?”

63. Saline Irrigation Water and Drought
Salinity and drought can interact strongly.
Salinity→Water Uptake↓ 
and:
Drought→Available Water↓ 
Together:
Drought+Salinity→Compounded Water Stress 

64. Salinity and Heat
Hot weather increases evaporation and atmospheric demand.
Therefore:
Salinity+Heat→Greater Crop Water Stress 
This makes saline-water management particularly important under hot, dry conditions.

65. Saline Water and Water Conservation
Using saline water can conserve freshwater resources in some circumstances, but only when soil and
crop impacts are managed.
The correct principle is:
Water Conservation+Salinity Management+Drainage  +
  +    
Water quantity alone should not determine irrigation decisions.

66. Long-Term Use of Saline Water
Repeated use of saline water can gradually change soil conditions.
The long-term pathway is:
Repeated Saline Irrigation→Salt Accumulation→Soil EC↑→Plant Stress↑→Yield Potential↓ 
unless sufficient salt removal occurs.

67. Short-Term vs Long-Term Risk
A water source may appear acceptable after one irrigation but cause problems after repeated use.
Therefore:
Water Quality Assessment Must Consider Long-Term Salt Balance   
This is especially important where:
- rainfall is low,
- evaporation is high,
- drainage is poor.

68. Saline Irrigation Water and Soil
Reclamation
If salinity has already accumulated, management may involve:

Diagnosis→Suitable Water→Leaching→Drainage→Salt Reduction 
If sodicity is also present:
Calcium Amendment (e.g., gypsum, where appropriate)+Leaching+Drainage 
may be considered based on soil diagnosis.

69. What Farmers Should Monitor
Water
- EC,
- SAR,
- TDS,
- sodium,
- chloride,
- boron,
- bicarbonate/carbonate,
- pH.

Soil
- EC,
- pH,
- sodium/ESP where relevant,
- moisture,
- drainage.

Crop
- emergence,
- leaf injury,
- growth,
- canopy,
- yield,
- crop-stage response.

70. Major Warning Signs
Possible symptoms of salinity include:
- poor germination,
- uneven emergence,
- stunting,
- reduced leaf expansion,
- leaf burn,
- premature senescence,
- reduced flowering,
- poor fruit/seed set,
- reduced yield.
However, symptoms alone cannot reliably diagnose salinity because drought, nutrient deficiency,
disease and other stresses can produce similar symptoms.
Therefore:
Symptoms + Water Test + Soil Test   
are much more reliable than symptoms alone.

71. Common Mistakes

❌ “Saline water cannot be used for agriculture.”
Not universally true.
Suitability depends on crop, soil, climate, drainage and management.

❌ “If the crop doesn't wilt, salinity isn't a problem.”
❌ “EC is the only thing I need to test.”

Salinity can cause gradual growth and yield reduction without obvious wilting.

No.
SAR, specific ions and alkalinity can also be important.

❌ “Salinity and sodicity are the same.”
No.

EC→Salinity  SAR/ESP→Sodium Hazard 

❌ “More irrigation always removes salt.”
❌ “Leaching without drainage solves salinity.”
❌ “Drip irrigation eliminates salinity.”

Only if water can move through and leave the root zone appropriately.

No.

No.
Salt can accumulate around the wetted zone.

❌ “Fertilizer has nothing to do with salinity.”
❌ “One EC limit applies to every crop.”

Fertilizer salts contribute to the overall dissolved-ion environment.

Crop tolerance differs.

76. Exam-Ready Definition
Saline irrigation water is irrigation water containing elevated concentrations of
dissolved salts that can increase salinity in the crop root zone and cause osmotic stress,
specific-ion toxicity, nutrient imbalance and yield reduction when salt inputs exceed
the capacity of the soil–water system to remove them. Its suitability depends on water
EC, salt composition, crop tolerance, soil properties, climate, drainage and irrigation
management.

77. Final Conceptual Summary
Remember the complete relationship:
Saline Water→Salt Input→Salt Accumulation→Osmotic Stress/Ion Toxicity→Crop Stress→Yield Los
s        
The solution is not simply “use more water.”
The correct strategy is:
Test Water+Choose Suitable Crop+Efficient Irrigation+Leaching+Good Drainage+Soil Monitoring ed    +   +   +
  +   +    

Final one-line memory
Saline irrigation water can be managed when the salt input, crop tolerance, soil water balance, leaching
and drainage are considered together.
